How To Survive

A household pandemic resilience plan — water, power, food, heat, sanitation, health, growing, animals, security and the cycles that repeat.

This file is complete and works offline. It needs no network, no fonts, no server and no apps. Save it, copy it to a phone, a USB stick and an e-reader, and print it. Use your browser's Print command for a paper copy — it is formatted for A4 with each chapter starting on a new page.

Built 2026-09-11 · 29 sections · Metric units, EUR, temperate continental Central Europe · Not medical advice — see the chapter on health.

How To Survive — A Household Pandemic Resilience Plan

A complete, step-by-step plan for getting a household through a severe pandemic with degraded or interrupted public services: water, electricity, food, heat, sanitation, medicine and security.

Two tracks run through every chapter:

  • 🏡 LAND — you have a house with a garden, yard, field or orchard.
  • 🏢 APARTMENT — you have a flat, a balcony at best, and shared infrastructure.

📴 Works offline. The entire plan is also built into a single self-contained file — offline/survival-plan.html — that needs no internet, no server and nothing installed. Copy it to a phone, a USB stick and an e-reader, and print it. Rebuild it any time with make offline (Python standard library only).

Units are metric. Prices are indicative EUR. The garden calendar is written for temperate continental Central Europe (Slovenia/Austria/N. Italy/Croatia — last frost mid-April to mid-May, first frost mid-October). Adjust dates to your own frost dates; everything else transfers.

Read this first: what this plan actually assumes

A pandemic is not an instant apocalypse. It is a long, grinding degradation. Planning for the wrong shape of event is the single most common and most expensive mistake people make.

What actually happens, in rough order:

  1. Weeks 0–4 — News, then panic buying. Shelves empty of exactly the things you need (flour, oil, tinned goods, paracetamol, toilet paper, baby formula). Prices spike. This is the moment when preparing becomes impossible, expensive, and antisocial. Everything in this plan must be bought before this point.
  2. Months 1–6 — Lockdowns or voluntary isolation. Hospitals saturate; ordinary illness (appendicitis, heart attack, childbirth, a broken arm) becomes far more dangerous than usual. Supply chains work but stutter. Some goods simply vanish for months.
  3. Months 2–12+ — Staff absence hits infrastructure: water treatment, electricity distribution, fuel logistics, waste collection, pharmacy restocking. You get intermittent services, not zero services. Rolling blackouts. Boil-water notices. Rubbish uncollected. Deliveries halted. Cash-only, then barter at the margins.
  4. Year 1–3 — Economic damage, inflation, unemployment, shortages of spare parts and imported goods. Recovery is uneven. This is the long tail where "self-sufficiency" actually pays off.

Design target: 12 months of intermittent utilities, and 3 months of no resupply at all, while never leaving your property.

That is the standard this whole plan is written against. It is achievable for a normal household on a normal budget, if you start early and build in order.

The four hard truths

  1. You cannot become fully self-sufficient. Nobody is. Not on a hectare, not with chickens, not with solar. You will still need salt, fuel, medicine, spare parts, seed and metal. The goal is not independence — it is reducing dependence, buying time, and lowering your exposure. A household that needs a shop once a month instead of twice a week is enormously safer.
  2. Skills beat gear. A €3,000 solar system you have never wired, a pressure canner you have never used, and a garden you have never grown will all fail at the worst moment. Every purchase in this plan carries an implied homework assignment: use it now, while mistakes are cheap.
  3. You survive with neighbours, not against them. The lone fortress is a fantasy that gets people killed. Three to eight cooperating households can share a well, a generator, a watch rotation, a nurse and a freezer. One household cannot do any of that, and cannot sleep.
  4. The things that kill people are boring. Not raiders. Dehydration, dysentery from bad sanitation, hypothermia in an unheated house, an untreated infection, a missed insulin dose, and carbon monoxide from cooking indoors. Spend your money and attention in that order.

The priority order (spend money in this sequence)

Rank everything by how fast its absence kills you. Never buy from a lower tier while a higher tier has a gap.

#NeedYou survive without it forChapter
1Air, warmth, shelter3 hours (exposure/CO)04
2Critical medicationHours to days (insulin, cardiac, epilepsy, steroids)06
3Water3 days01
4Sanitation & hygiene~1 week before disease05
5Food3 weeks (but you are useless after 1)03
6Light, power, informationWeeks (morale and safety collapse first)02 · 10
7Renewable food productionMonths (matters from month 3 onward)07 · 08 · 09
8Security, repair, moneyOngoing10 · 11

The four readiness levels

Do not try to jump to Level 3. Complete each level fully — across all categories — before starting the next. A household with 12 months of rice and no water plan is less prepared than one with two weeks of everything.

LevelEnduranceRough cost (family of 4)Time to build
L0 — Ready72 hours, no utilities€250 – €500One weekend
L1 — Buffered2 weeks, no resupply€800 – €1,800One month
L2 — Isolated3 months, no resupply; power & water for critical loads€3,000 – €8,0003 – 6 months
L3 — Self-renewing12 months, with food/energy/water production that repeats€8,000 – €25,0001 – 3 years

L3 is the only level that includes cycles — things that regenerate: seed you saved, chicks that hatched, compost you made, wood you seasoned. Everything below L3 is a countdown timer. See Chapter 12.

How to use this repository

Step 1 — Measure where you actually are. Fill in worksheets/household-audit.md. It takes about an hour and tells you your current survival time in days, per category. Do not buy anything before you do this.

Step 2 — Read the planning framework. docs/00-planning-framework.md — how to set targets, budget, sequence purchases, and define the trigger points that tell you when to act.

Step 3 — Work the categories in priority order. Chapters 01 → 06 are universal and come first. Then take your track: 07 + 08 if you have land, 09 if you do not.

Step 4 — Buy from the tiered lists. worksheets/shopping-lists.md — exactly what to buy at each level, in purchase order, with quantities for your household size.

Step 5 — Test it. Then test it again. worksheets/drill-log.md — the blackout weekend, the water-off day, the isolation drill. Untested preparation is a guess.

Step 6 — Build what you need. builds/ — construction plans with dimensions, cut lists, materials and costs, ordered so the highest-value builds come first. A bucket water filter takes 30 minutes; the chicken coop takes a weekend.

Step 7 — Make it work offline. offline/survival-plan.html is the whole plan in one self-contained file — no internet, no server, no apps. Open it in any browser, or print it. A plan you cannot reach in a blackout is not a plan; see Chapter 13.

Step 8 — Run the cycles. docs/12-operating-cycles-and-calendar.md — the daily, weekly, monthly and seasonal routines that keep stock rotated, gardens producing, and animals breeding. This is the part that turns a stockpile into a system.

  • 02 — Power builds — the complete 12 V solar system with cable-sizing and fusing tables, DC lighting circuit, insulated battery box, adjustable tilt frame, generator noise screen.

Worksheets

  • Quick-reference cards — ⭐ print these first. The numbers you need at 3 a.m. by torchlight: water treatment doses, oral rehydration, bleach dilutions, carbon monoxide, when to seek help, the trigger ladder, the daily routine, your shut-offs and contacts.
  • Household audit — fill this in second.
  • Shopping lists — tiered, in buy-order, with quantities.
  • Drill log — the tests that prove it works.
  • Inventory template — what you have, where, expiry.

Legal, ethical and medical note

  • Prepare early and gradually. Buying a year of food over twelve months is prudent; buying it in the week panic starts takes it from someone who needs it that day. The ethical way to prepare is the same as the effective way: start now.
  • Follow public health guidance and get vaccinated. Nothing in this plan is a substitute for medical care, and none of it is medical advice — build your medication buffer with your doctor and pharmacist, never by skipping doses.
  • Well drilling, rainwater use, greywater, wood stoves, solar grid connection, livestock keeping, slaughter, waste disposal and firearms are all regulated and the rules differ by country and municipality. Check before you build.
  • Keep helping people. A household that is calm, stocked and healthy is the one that can afford to help neighbours — which is exactly what makes the street around you survivable.
↑ Contents

00 — The Planning Framework

How to plan, in what order, with what money, and how to know when to act.

Most people "prepare" by buying things they saw in a video. Six months later they own a water filter, forty tins of beans, and a torch, and they still die of hypothermia or dysentery. This chapter is the method that prevents that.

1. Build the threat model before the shopping list

Write down, on one page, the specific disruptions you are planning against. For a severe pandemic, the realistic list is:

DisruptionLikelihoodDurationEffect on you
Supply chain gaps, empty shelves for specific goodsNear certain1–12 months, intermittentCan't buy flour, oil, medicine, spare parts
Voluntary or mandated isolationNear certainWeeks to months at a timeCan't leave; can't work; deliveries stop
Health system saturationNear certain3–18 monthsOrdinary emergencies become lethal
Price inflation / currency stressLikely1–3 yearsYour savings buy less; cash-only periods
Rolling electricity blackoutsLikelyHours to days at a timeFridge, freezer, heating pumps, water pumps, comms
Water pressure loss or boil-water noticesPossibleDays to weeksYou need stored + treatable water
Waste collection suspendedLikelyWeeksSanitation and vermin problems
Fuel rationing / stations closedPossibleWeeksNo generator fuel, no vehicle, no deliveries
Complete grid failure > 1 weekUnlikely but catastrophic—Everything at once
Civil disorder in your areaLocation-dependentDaysSecurity becomes primary

Your plan's job is to make each row survivable. Not to make you invincible. Print the table, mark your current answer for each row, and you have your priority list for the next six months.

What you are NOT planning for

Be explicit about this too, because it saves money and sanity. You are not planning for permanent civilisational collapse, nuclear war, or defending a compound against an army. If that ever happens, the plan is "leave with the go-bag and find people" — not "hold the house". Everything here is sized for a bad decade, not the end of the world.

2. Audit your household honestly

Fill in worksheets/household-audit.md. The key outputs are:

a) People. Number, ages, weights, and for each person: chronic conditions, prescription medicines (name, dose, how many days you hold), allergies, mobility limits, glasses/dental needs, pregnancy or infants, dietary needs, and pets (they eat too — 12 months of dog food is 100+ kg).

b) Calorie and water demand. Sum the daily requirement for your actual household. Don't use "a person" — use your people (see Ch. 03 and Ch. 01).

c) The building. Own or rent? House or flat? Which floor? Heating type and what happens to it without electricity (most gas boilers and all pellet stoves are dead without power). Insulation quality. Roof area and material (rainwater potential). Is there a cellar, a garage, a loft, a south-facing roof or balcony? Water: mains only, or well/borehole/spring/stream? Sewer or septic?

d) The land. Total m², usable growing m², sun hours, slope, soil type, water access, existing trees, fencing, outbuildings.

e) Skills in the household. Who can garden, cook from raw ingredients, sew, weld, do first aid, fix a bicycle, wire 12 V, butcher an animal, shoot, speak to neighbours, keep calm. List the gaps — those are your training plan.

f) Current endurance, in days. For each category, how many days could you last starting right now with the doors locked:

Water          ___ days      Sanitation     ___ days
Food           ___ days      Medicine       ___ days
Heat           ___ days      Power          ___ days
Cooking fuel   ___ days      Hygiene/soap   ___ days

Your real preparedness is the lowest number on that list. Not the average. Fix the smallest number first, every time. This single rule will direct your spending better than any list.

3. Set targets by level, not by wishlist

Work through the four levels from the README horizontally — every category to L1 before any category to L2.

L0 — Ready (72 hours) · one weekend · €250–500

The legal-minimum civil-defence standard. Everyone should be here already.

  • 30 L water per person stored + a means to treat more
  • 3 days of food needing no cooking
  • Torches, batteries, a radio, a power bank
  • First aid kit, 2 weeks of any prescription meds
  • Cash in small notes; documents in one bag
  • A way to keep warm with no heating

L1 — Buffered (2 weeks) · one month · €800–1,800

Covers the overwhelming majority of real disruptions.

  • 200 L water/person stored or a reliable treatable source
  • 14 days of normal food you actually eat + cooking fuel for it
  • A camping stove + fuel; a way to heat one room
  • Lighting and phone charging independent of the grid (small solar)
  • Full medicine chest, 30+ days of prescriptions
  • Bucket toilet kit and hygiene supplies for two weeks

L2 — Isolated (3 months) · 3–6 months · €3,000–8,000

The core of pandemic preparedness — the "don't leave the house for a season" level.

  • 3 months of staples, packed for long storage, with rotation started
  • A power system that runs the fridge/freezer, lights and comms indefinitely
  • A water source you control (rainwater, well, or 600 L+/person stored) with multi-stage filtration and disinfectant chemicals for 3+ months
  • Wood/propane for 3 months of cooking and one heated room
  • Sanitation fully solved without mains water
  • A pandemic isolation protocol, PPE, and a 3-month medicine buffer
  • First growing season started, first animals if you have land

L3 — Self-renewing (12 months + cycles) · 1–3 years · €8,000–25,000

  • 12 months of stored calories as a buffer, not the plan
  • Food production with saved seed, breeding animals, and made compost
  • Energy: enough solar/battery for year-round critical loads + 2 winters of wood
  • Water: rainwater capture + storage sized for your dry season, or a hand-pumped well
  • Preservation capacity: pressure canner, jars, dehydrator, root cellar, smoker
  • Spares, tools and consumables to keep all of the above running for a year
  • A functioning mutual-aid group

4. Budget: what each amount actually buys

If money is the constraint, spend it in exactly this order. Each tier assumes the previous is complete.

€0 — do these first, this week

  • Drink and store tap water in washed 5 L bottles (free)
  • Write the family contact plan and print phone numbers on paper
  • Learn your water shut-off, gas shut-off and consumer unit
  • Download and print the key references (Ch. 11)
  • Talk to three neighbours. This is the highest-value free action available.
  • Start a compost heap; save seeds from a shop tomato/pepper
  • Book a first aid course and a dental check-up

€300 — the survival floor (L0)

Water containers 60 L · 20 L gravity filter or Sawyer-type · NaDCC tablets · unscented bleach · torches + headlamps + AA/AAA rechargeables + charger · wind-up/solar radio · 20 W solar panel + 20,000 mAh power bank · first aid kit · OTC medicine chest · 2 weeks of tinned/dry food · camping gas stove + 4 canisters · bucket toilet + coir · 2 kg soap · cash · fire extinguisher + CO alarm + smoke alarm

€1,500 — the two-week to one-month household (L1)

Everything above plus: 400–600 L water storage · 1 month of staples in buckets + mylar + O₂ absorbers · 100 W solar + 100 Ah LiFePO₄ + 600 W inverter · better stove + 2× 11 kg propane cylinders · 20 kg firewood-equivalent or a small wood burner if you have a flue · full medicine chest + 3-month prescription buffer · FFP2/FFP3 masks, gloves, goggles, disinfectant · hand tools · radios

€5,000 — the three-month isolated household (L2)

Everything above plus: 1–2 kW solar + 5 kWh LiFePO₄ + hybrid inverter · IBC rainwater tanks (1,000 L each) + first-flush diverter + pre-filter · 3 months of staples for the whole household · chest freezer (A+++) · pressure canner + 100 jars · dehydrator · grain mill (hand + electric) · wood stove with cooktop + 4 m³ seasoned wood · full garden setup (beds, tools, seeds, netting, hoses) OR full apartment kit (sprouting, microgreens, mushrooms, fermentation) · chickens + coop + 6 months of feed

€15,000+ — the self-renewing homestead (L3)

Everything above plus: 4–6 kW solar + 10–15 kWh storage · well with hand pump or solar DC pump · polytunnel/greenhouse · 12 months of staples · orchard and perennials planted · breeding stock (hens + rooster, rabbits, or goats) · 2 years of wood + a chainsaw and safety gear + a log splitter · a proper workshop, spares and consumables · root cellar built

Note the shape of this: the first €300 buys perhaps 60% of your total risk reduction. The last €10,000 buys maybe 10%. Do not let the dream of a homestead stop you from buying a €25 water filter this weekend.

5. Sequencing: the correct order of operations

Within any budget, execute in this order. Each step is cheap and fast compared to the one after it, and each makes the next one smaller.

  1. Reduce demand before adding supply. Insulating a room, buying a thermal cooker, and swapping to LED lighting each cost €20–100 and permanently shrink your fuel, food and power needs. Adding generation to cover waste is the most expensive possible move. Always cut the load first.
  2. Store before you produce. Stored food is instant, certain and cheap. Produced food is slow, uncertain and skill-dependent. Storage buys you the time in which production can fail and be retried.
  3. Water before food. Always. Every time.
  4. Sanitation the same week as water. They are the same problem.
  5. Medicine buffer before any gear. It takes months of ordinary GP visits to build legally. Start the conversation now; it costs nothing today.
  6. Low tech before high tech. A hand pump, a hand grain mill, a wood stove and a bucket work when electronics fail. Buy the manual version of anything critical before the powered version of anything optional.
  7. Redundancy on single points of failure only. Two ways to get water, two ways to cook, two ways to make light, two ways to treat water. Not two of everything — that's hoarding, not planning.
  8. Skills and community last in spending, first in time. They cost almost nothing but take the longest to acquire. Start them on day one, in parallel with everything else.

6. The trigger ladder — knowing when to act

The hardest question is not "what to buy" but "when to move". Decide the thresholds now, in calm, and write them down. Then you act on evidence instead of adrenaline — days before the crowds, without ever making a panic decision.

🟢 GREEN — normal times (this is where you are now)

Posture: Build to your target level. Rotate stock. Grow the garden. Train. Nothing is urgent; everything is progressing. Monitor: WHO Disease Outbreak News, your national public health agency, and one or two boring epidemiology sources. Once a month is enough.

🟡 AMBER — an outbreak with sustained human-to-human transmission outside its country of origin

This is the trigger. Not "cases in my country" — that is too late. Actions, within 72 hours:

  1. Top up everything to L2 now, in one trip. Prices and availability are still normal. This is the single most valuable action in the entire plan and it lasts only a few days.
  2. Fill every water container. Fill the car's fuel tank and all fuel cans.
  3. Refill prescriptions; request early refills and a written repeat.
  4. Draw out cash in small denominations.
  5. Order the long-lead items you have been putting off (filters, seeds, spares, a battery). Delivery times collapse after this point.
  6. Tell your mutual-aid group. Agree a check-in schedule.
  7. Start masking in crowded indoor places and reduce optional exposure. The goal is to be uninfected and stocked when the wave arrives.

🟠 ORANGE — community transmission confirmed in your country

Actions:

  1. Withdraw from optional public contact. Work from home if at all possible.
  2. Begin the quarantine protocol on all incoming goods and people (Ch. 06).
  3. Pull children out of avoidable gatherings; discuss school with your family.
  4. Final gap purchases by delivery, not in person.
  5. Start the garden cycle regardless of season — sprouts and microgreens begin producing in 5–14 days, indoors, any time of year (Ch. 09).
  6. Set up the isolation room and PPE station before anyone is ill.
  7. Switch to the crisis daily routine (Ch. 12) so it is habit before it matters.

🔴 RED — hospitals saturated, or utilities visibly failing, or transmission in your street

Actions:

  1. Full household isolation. Nobody in or out without the decon protocol.
  2. Switch to stored/produced resources; stop consuming anything irreplaceable.
  3. Go to war-footing on hygiene: handwashing station, surface disinfection, isolation of any symptomatic person, ventilation maximised.
  4. Daily health check for every household member (temp + pulse ox + symptoms log).
  5. Light, noise and smell discipline (Ch. 10). Nothing advertises.
  6. Mutual-aid group to daily radio check-in.
  7. Ration deliberately from day one, not when stocks look low. Set the daily allowance based on a 12-month horizon and adjust upward later if resupply returns. It is far easier to relax a ration than to impose one late.

⚫ BLACK — utilities down indefinitely, no authority response

Full off-grid operation. Water from your controlled source only, always treated. Sanitation to the humanure/latrine system. Warm-room only. Cooking on wood. Security watch by rotation with the group. Preserve everything perishable immediately. This is the level that most preparation never needs — but the kit for it is the same kit as ORANGE, just used harder.

Coming back down

Have de-escalation rules too, or you will live in RED for years and burn out. Step down one level after two weeks of improved conditions. Restock to your target level before relaxing the ration.

7. Test everything — the drill schedule

Nothing is real until it has been tested. Untested kit fails at exactly the wrong moment, and the failures are always stupid: the inverter has the wrong plug, the filter needs a fitting you don't have, nobody can light the stove, the stored water tastes so bad the children refuse to drink it.

DrillFrequencyWhat it proves
Blackout weekend — main breaker off, Fri night to Sun nightTwice a year, once in winterPower, light, cooking, heat, fridge/freezer behaviour, morale
Water-off day — main stopcock closed, 24 hTwice a yearStorage, filtration, dish washing, toilets, how much you really use
Eat-the-store week — only stored food, no shoppingQuarterlyRotation, recipes, whether your family will actually eat it, gaps
Isolation weekend — nobody leaves, decon protocol activeOnce a yearThe protocol, PPE fit, boredom, what you forgot
Cold night — heat off, warm-room onlyOnce a winterInsulation, bedding, clothing, the actual temperature
Full inventory countTwice a yearWhat expired, what's missing, what you overbought
Comms check with the mutual-aid groupMonthlyRadios, phone tree, that people still care

Keep the results in worksheets/drill-log.md. Every drill produces a shopping list and a fix list. That is the point.

8. The fifteen mistakes that ruin plans

  1. Buying gear instead of building skills. The gear is the easy 10%.
  2. Storing food nobody eats. If your family won't eat it in good times, they won't eat it in bad times — they'll just be hungry and miserable. Store what you actually cook, and cook what you store.
  3. No water plan. The most common and most lethal gap. Food gets all the attention; water kills you six times faster.
  4. Forgetting sanitation. Second most common. You will produce 1–1.5 kg of waste per person per day whether or not you planned for it.
  5. Ignoring heat. In a cold climate, heating dwarfs every other energy need. A 5 kWh battery is irrelevant next to a house that needs 60 kWh/day of heat.
  6. Underestimating animal feed. Six hens eat ~300 kg of grain a year. If you don't store or grow it, your chickens become your competitors, then your dinner.
  7. Single points of failure. One filter, one stove, one pair of glasses, one can opener, one key person who knows how it all works.
  8. Electronics with no spares. Charge controllers and inverters fail. A spare €60 controller keeps a €4,000 system alive.
  9. Too much gear, no routine. Preparation without operating cycles is a museum.
  10. OPSEC failures. Posting the stockpile online, bragging to colleagues, running a loud generator. You are advertising.
  11. Planning alone. See hard truth #3.
  12. No mental health plan. Months of confinement, fear and boredom break people and marriages. Plan for morale as seriously as for calories.
  13. Waiting for certainty. By the time it's certain, the shelves are empty. The AMBER trigger exists precisely to let you act early and cheaply.
  14. Never testing. See section 7.
  15. Planning for the wrong disaster. Pandemic ≠ war ≠ earthquake. Tactical gear is not a pandemic plan; a well-stocked pantry, a water system and a working thermometer are.

9. The one-page version

If you remember nothing else:

1. Fix the lowest number on your endurance audit. Repeat forever.
2. Water, sanitation, medicine, heat, food — in that order.
3. Cut demand before you add supply.
4. Store first, produce second. Storage buys time for production to fail.
5. Buy the manual version of anything critical.
6. Act at AMBER, not at RED.
7. Test it, or you don't have it.
8. Three neighbours are worth more than three thousand euros of gear.

🔨 Build it: All build plans — construction plans for everything in this plan, in priority order

Next: 01 — Water — the first thing that kills you.

↑ Contents

01 — Water

Three days without it and you are dead. One week of drinking bad water and you are dead more slowly and much worse. This is the chapter to finish first.

1. How much you actually need

Forget "2 litres a day". That is drinking water for a sedentary person in mild weather. A household doing manual work, cooking from raw ingredients, washing by hand and keeping a garden alive needs far more.

UseLitres/person/dayNotes
Drinking2.5 – 3.5Up to 6 L with heavy work or fever/diarrhoea
Cooking & food prep2 – 3Dry beans and grains need soaking and boiling water
Washing hands & face2 – 3Do not cut this. It is your disease control.
Body washing3 – 5A sponge bath is 3 L; a shower is 40–80 L
Dish washing2 – 3Two-basin method: wash + rinse
Toilet flushing6 – 20Only if you use a flush toilet — see Ch. 05
Laundry3 – 5Averaged over a week; ~30 L per hand-wash load
Household total15 – 25Realistic sustainable planning figure
Survival minimum5 – 7Drinking + cooking + minimal hygiene. Survivable for weeks, miserable, and hygiene risk rises.

Add on top:

  • Animals: chicken 0.25–0.5 L/day · rabbit 0.5–1 L · goat 4–8 L · dog 1–2 L · pig 10–20 L
  • Garden: in a dry Central European summer, roughly 3–5 L/m²/day for intensively cropped beds, much less with heavy mulch and drip irrigation (1–2 L/m²/day). A 100 m² garden can want 300 L on a hot day. This is why garden water must come from rain, not from your drinking store.

Your household number

Persons ___ × 20 L         = _____ L/day
Animals (sum)              = _____ L/day
Garden (summer only)       = _____ L/day
                             --------
                 TOTAL/day = _____ L/day
        × 14 (L1 target)   = _____ L
        × 90 (L2 target)   = _____ L

For a family of four: 80 L/day, so 1,120 L for two weeks and 7,200 L for three months. You are not going to store 7,200 L of drinking water in a flat. This is exactly why the plan is storage + a renewable source + treatment, not storage alone.

2. The three-layer water strategy

Every household needs all three layers. Storage alone is a countdown timer.

LAYER 1 — STORED        Immediate, clean, zero effort. Covers days 1–14.
LAYER 2 — SOURCE        Rain, well, spring, stream, lake. Covers months.
LAYER 3 — TREATMENT     Turns Layer 2 into Layer 1. The most important layer.

Treatment is the layer people skip and it is the one that decides whether you get cholera. A household with a stream and no filter is worse off than a household with 200 L and a Sawyer filter.

3. Layer 1 — Storage

How much, and in what

Target: 15 L/person/day × 14 days = 200 L per person minimum (L1). For L2, either 600 L/person stored, or 200 L stored plus a proven source.

ContainerCapacity€/unitNotes
Reused 5 L / 10 L PET bottles5–10 LFreePerfect starter. Washed, filled, dated. Stack in a cool dark place. Replace annually.
Food-grade jerry can20 L€15–25The workhorse. Portable when full (20 kg). Buy 6–10.
Blue food-grade barrel60 / 120 / 200 L€40–90Cheap per litre. A full 200 L drum weighs 200 kg — you will never move it. Site it before filling, on a solid floor, with a siphon pump (€10).
IBC tote (caged tank)1,000 L€80–180 usedBest €/litre by far. Must be food-grade and previously held only food (verify!). Opaque cover or paint — sunlight grows algae. Needs a firm level base; 1,000 kg.
Bathtub bladder (WaterBOB type)250–380 L€25–40Apartment essential. Deploy at AMBER, before pressure fails. Bathtubs themselves leak and are filthy; the liner solves both.
Stainless/plastic water butt200–1,000 L€60–200For rainwater, outdoors.

Rules for stored water

  • Food-grade containers only (HDPE marked with a "2" or "food safe"). Never reuse containers that held chemicals, fuel or milk (milk protein residue is impossible to fully remove and will spoil).
  • Fill with treated mains water if you have it. If your mains is chlorinated, you can store it as-is.
  • To store longer than ~6 months, add 2 drops of unscented 5% sodium hypochlorite bleach per litre (≈ 2 mg/L free chlorine) at fill time, or use a NaDCC tablet at the dose on the packet.
  • Date every container. Rotate mains-filled storage every 6–12 months — the water doesn't "go off" chemically but plastics leach and biofilm grows.
  • Store cool, dark, off concrete floors (use pallets or boards — concrete leaches and sweats), away from petrol, paint and solvents (plastics are permeable to vapours).
  • Freezing splits containers. Leave 10% headspace and keep storage above 0 °C, or store in a place you accept losing. Unheated garages in Central Europe will freeze.

The hidden water already in your home (know this now)

At the moment pressure fails, you already have:

SourceTypical volumeHow to get it
Hot water cylinder / boiler tank80–200 LThe biggest single reserve in most homes. Turn off power/gas to it and close the cold inlet first, then open the drain valve at the bottom and a hot tap upstairs to let air in. Learn where these valves are today.
Toilet cisterns6–9 L eachClean water (the tank, not the bowl). Usable directly.
Pipes in the house5–15 LOpen the lowest tap in the building and a high one to vent.
Ice cubes, drinks, tinned vegetable liquidA few LCounts in an emergency
Central heating system50–150 LNot potable — contains inhibitor chemicals. Fine for flushing toilets.
Waterbed, aquarium, poolLargeNot potable; flushing and washing only. Pool water can be filtered for washing but is chemically loaded.

First action at AMBER: fill everything — bath, bladder, jerry cans, pots, bottles, the washing machine drum, buckets. Water is free and heavy; the moment you want it, it stops being available.

4. Layer 2 — Sources you control

🏡 Rainwater — the best option for anyone with a roof

The maths (memorise this):

Litres = Roof footprint (m²) × Rainfall (mm) × 0.8

The 0.8 is the runoff coefficient (losses to splash, evaporation, first-flush, absorption). Use 0.9 for smooth metal, 0.75–0.8 for tile, 0.7 for rough or mossy surfaces.

So 1 mm of rain on a 100 m² roof ≈ 80 litres.

A house with a 120 m² roof footprint in a climate with 1,000 mm/year (typical inland Central Europe; Slovenia is often 1,000–1,500 mm) yields:

120 × 1000 × 0.8 = 96,000 L per year  ≈  260 L/day averaged

That is more than a family of four needs, from the roof alone. Rain is the answer for the land track, and the limiting factor is storage, not rainfall.

Sizing storage: size for your longest dry spell, not your annual total. In Central Europe, plan for a 3–5 week dry summer period. Family of four at 80 L/day × 30 days = 2,400 L, i.e. three IBC totes. Add more if you irrigate: a garden in a hot July can use 3,000 L/month on its own. Realistic land-track target: 3,000–6,000 L of rainwater storage (3–6 IBCs, or one buried/above ground tank).

Build order for a rainwater system

  1. Gutters and downpipes — clean, sound, with leaf guards.
  2. First-flush diverter — discards the first 0.5–1 mm of each rain event, which carries the bird droppings, dust and pollen. Cheap (€30–60) or DIY from a vertical pipe with a floating ball. Do not skip this; it does more for water quality than any filter downstream.
  3. Coarse screen/leaf filter at the tank inlet (1 mm mesh) — keeps out debris, leaves and, critically, mosquitoes and rodents.
  4. Tank(s), opaque or shaded, with a sealed lid, an overflow routed away from the house foundations, and a drain at the bottom for sludge.
  5. Take-off above the floor — draw water 10–15 cm above the tank bottom so you leave the sediment layer behind (calmed inlet + floating suction is even better).
  6. Link tanks at the base with a manifold so they fill and draw as one.
  7. Treatment before drinking. Always. See Layer 3.

Roof material warnings: Never drink rainwater from asbestos-cement, lead flashing, tar/bitumen shingles (leaches hydrocarbons), copper with acidic rain, or any recently treated/painted roof. Clay tile, concrete tile, slate, glazed tile, and coated steel are fine. If in doubt, use roof water for the garden, flushing and washing only, and drink from stored/other sources.

Winter: disconnect and drain downpipe filters and exposed pipework before the first hard frost, or they split. Keep one tank indoors/insulated, or accept that outdoor tanks are frozen solid in January — which is exactly when you need them. Partially burying a tank, or building a straw-bale surround, keeps it liquid.

🏡 Wells and boreholes

If you have or can get a well, it is the single most valuable resilience asset a property can have — provided you can lift the water without electricity.

  • Shallow/dug well (< 8 m to water): a suction hand pump works. Cheap and simple.
  • Deep well/borehole (8–80 m): needs a deep-well hand pump (piston pump with rods, e.g. Bison/Simple Pump types, €800–2,500) that can often be installed alongside the existing electric submersible pump, or a DC solar pump (€300–900 for a 12/24 V pump + panel). Both approaches are worth it.
  • Test the water (bacteriology + nitrate + arsenic + heavy metals) before you rely on it. Nitrate from agricultural land is common and is not removed by boiling or ordinary filters — it needs reverse osmosis or ion exchange, and it is dangerous for infants.
  • Never assume well water is clean because it is clear. Treat it, or test it annually.
  • Keep a spare pump seal/leather kit, and know how to pull and service the pump.

🏡 Springs, streams, ponds, lakes

Free, renewable, and always contaminated — assume giardia, cryptosporidium, E. coli, and agricultural/industrial runoff. Usable with full treatment.

  • Collect from flowing water where possible; avoid stagnant water and anything downstream of fields, roads, septics or livestock.
  • Pre-settle turbid water in a bucket for several hours, or filter through cloth, before the real filter — silt destroys filter elements fast.
  • Note the distance and the effort: 20 L weighs 20 kg. A 200 m round trip four times a day is a real daily job. Plan a cart, a yoke, or a gravity line.

🏡 Snow and ice

1 volume of snow yields roughly 0.1 of water. Melting snow costs a lot of fuel — never eat snow (it lowers core temperature). Only a last resort.

🏢 Apartment sources

You control almost nothing, so storage and pre-emptive filling are everything:

  • Bathtub bladder (250–380 L) — deploy at AMBER.
  • 8–12 × 20 L jerry cans stored under beds and in cupboards (160–240 L).
  • Your hot water cylinder (80–200 L) if you have one — know the drain valve.
  • Balcony rain catch: even a 2 m² tarp or an awning funnelled into a butt gives 1.6 L per mm of rain — 30–80 L in a decent month. Small but free, and ideal for flushing and washing, which is where most of your litres go.
  • Building roof: if you can legally access it and your block has a flat roof, a single downpipe can supply the whole building. This is a conversation to have with your building committee now, not during a crisis. A shared 1,000 L IBC in a utility room, fed from a roof downpipe, is transformative for a block of flats.
  • Never rely on being able to carry water up 8 flights with no lift. 20 L per trip; a family's daily need is 4 trips. Plan a rope-and-bucket haul from a balcony, and store more so you need fewer trips.

5. Layer 3 — Treatment: filtration and disinfection

Understand the distinction, because most people get it wrong:

  • Filtration physically removes particles, protozoa (giardia, crypto) and — if fine enough (≤ 0.2 µm) — bacteria. Most filters do not remove viruses.
  • Disinfection (chemical or heat) kills bacteria and viruses but does not remove sediment, chemicals, or (for chlorine) cryptosporidium reliably.

Therefore: always do both, in this order.

SETTLE → PRE-FILTER (cloth/sand) → FILTER (≤0.2 µm) → DISINFECT (chlorine/boil/UV) → STORE CLEAN

Boiling — the gold standard

Bring to a rolling boil for 1 minute (3 minutes above 2,000 m). That kills everything biological. It does not remove chemicals, nitrate or sediment, and it costs fuel: roughly 0.1 kWh per litre to heat from 15 °C to boiling — about 1 kg of firewood per 10–15 L, or 90 g of propane per 10 L. Boiling all your water is fuel-expensive; use it for drinking/cooking water when filters fail, and filter+chlorinate for the rest.

Chemical disinfection — cheap, light, stores well

Unscented liquid bleach (sodium hypochlorite, 5–6%, no additives/fragrance):

  • 2 drops per litre of clear water (4 drops if cloudy or cold).
  • ≈ 10 mL (2 tsp) per 40 L, or ≈ 50 mL per 200 L drum.
  • Stir, wait 30 minutes (60 min if cold or turbid). You should smell a faint chlorine odour afterwards — if not, repeat the dose and wait again.
  • Bleach degrades. It loses ~20% potency per year at room temperature, faster when warm. Date your bottles, store cool and dark, and replace annually. This is why bleach is a poor long-term store.

Calcium hypochlorite granules ("pool shock", 65–70% available chlorine) — the long-term answer. Stores for years dry and sealed, and 500 g treats roughly 150,000–190,000 litres.

  • Make a stock solution: 7 g (≈1 heaped tsp) into 7.5 L of water ≈ a 0.5–1% chlorine solution.
  • Then dose 1 part stock solution to 100 parts water to treat, and wait 30 min.
  • Safety: buy only the plain calcium hypochlorite type with no algaecide, clarifier, stabiliser or anti-fungal additives. Store in a sealed glass or HDPE container away from anything flammable, acids, or ammonia — it is a strong oxidiser and reacts violently, including with organic matter and some plastics. Never store in a metal container. Keep it dry; it releases chlorine gas if damp. Store outside the house (shed/garage) if possible.

NaDCC tablets (sodium dichloroisocyanurate — Aquatabs, Katadyn Micropur etc.)

  • The most convenient and safest-storing option. 5–10 year shelf life, exact dosing, no handling hazard. Typically one tablet per 1 L, 5 L or 20 L depending on the product. ~€0.03 per litre treated.
  • Buy a mix: a bulk tin of 20 L tablets for the household store, and a small bottle of 1 L tablets for the go-bag.
  • This should be in every household, both tracks, at every level. Cheap, tiny, and it works.

Iodine — works but tastes vile, is less effective against cryptosporidium, and must not be used long-term by pregnant people or anyone with thyroid disease. Keep as a backup only.

Filters — what to buy

TypePore sizeThroughputLife€Verdict
Gravity ceramic/carbon (Berkey-type, 2 × elements)~0.2 µm4–8 L/h10,000–20,000 L per pair€250–350The household workhorse. No power, no pumping, filters continuously on the worktop. Elements are cleanable (scrub with a scouring pad). Buy spares.
Hollow-fibre squeeze/inline (Sawyer Squeeze / Mini)0.1 µm1–2 L/minup to 100,000+ L€30–45Astonishing value. Buy 3–4. Bucket-mount them for a DIY gravity system for €50 total. Must not freeze — freezing ruptures the fibres invisibly and it will then pass everything.
Gravity bag system (LifeStraw Family/Community)0.02 µm (some remove viruses)5–12 L/h18,000–100,000 L€80–350Excellent for groups. The virus-rated ones are the exception to the "filters don't remove viruses" rule.
Pump filter (MSR/Katadyn)0.2 µm1 L/min1,000–50,000 L€80–350For mobility, not household volume. Tiring at scale.
Under-sink / whole-house cartridges (sediment + carbon)20 / 5 / 1 µmHigh6–12 months€40–120Great as a pre-filter stage to protect the good filter. Buy a year of cartridges.
Reverse osmosisionic0.2–0.6 L/minmembrane 2–3 yr€150–400The only thing that removes nitrate, arsenic, heavy metals, salt. Needs pressure (a pump, so power) and wastes 2–4 L per L produced. Worth it if your water source has a chemical problem.
DIY biosand / slow sand filterbiological20–60 L/hIndefinite with maintenance€30 materialsGenuinely effective (removes 95–99% of pathogens once the biolayer matures over 2–3 weeks). A real L3 renewable option — but it must never dry out or freeze, and it still needs disinfection after.
UV pen (SteriPEN)—1 L/minbattery-limited€70–100Kills everything in clear water, useless in turbid. Needs power. Backup only.

Minimum recommended kit (both tracks): 1 gravity filter (Berkey-type or bucket-mounted Sawyers) + 2 spare Sawyer filters + a tin of NaDCC tablets + 500 g calcium hypochlorite (land) or a 1 L bleach bottle rotated annually (apartment) + a cooking pot you can boil in. Total: €150–400. This covers you for years.

Turning cloudy water clear

Filters clog fast on silty water. Pre-treat:

  1. Settle — stand it in a bucket for 2–12 hours; decant the clear top.
  2. Cloth filter — 4–8 layers of cotton (a folded T-shirt or a tea towel). Remarkably effective; this alone removes a large share of pathogens attached to particles.
  3. Coagulate — a pinch of alum (aluminium sulphate) or even crushed Moringa seed makes particles clump and settle in 30 minutes. Alum is cheap, stores forever, and is worth having.
  4. Sand/charcoal pre-filter — gravel, coarse sand, fine sand, charcoal in a bucket. Improves clarity and taste. It is a pre-filter, not a purifier.

Storing treated water

Once treated, store in clean, closed, dark containers and use within 2–3 days unless you added a chlorine residual. Never dip dirty cups into a store — use a tap or a dedicated pouring vessel. Recontamination at the point of use is one of the most common causes of illness in a crisis.

6. Using less — the highest-leverage move

Cutting demand is cheaper than any tank or filter.

  • Two-basin dishwashing: wash basin + rinse basin. 4 L does a family's meal instead of 20 L of running water. Reuse the rinse water as the next wash water.
  • Sponge bath / bucket bath: 3–5 L versus 60 L for a shower. A solar shower bag (20 L, €15) or a garden-sprayer shower (€20, pressurised, 5 L does two people) makes it civilised.
  • Navy shower: wet, water off, soap, water on, rinse. 10–15 L.
  • Don't flush drinking water. A toilet flush is 6–9 L of potable water. Switch to greywater flushing or a bucket toilet (Ch. 05) on day one. For a family of four this alone saves 50–80 L/day — the single biggest water saving available in any household.
  • Cook in ways that keep the water: steam over a pot and reuse the water; cook pasta in less water; keep vegetable cooking water for soup or the garden.
  • Catch the warm-up water. A bucket under the tap while hot water arrives saves 1–3 L per use, several times a day.

7. Greywater — reusing what you already used

Greywater = washing, bathing, laundry, and dish water (not toilet = blackwater). In a shortage this is free water for flushing and the garden.

  • Use it within 24 hours. It turns anaerobic and foul fast.
  • Use plant-safe soap if it goes on the garden: no boron, no chlorine bleach, low sodium. Simple soap and potash-based soaps are fine; most laundry powders are not (high sodium and phosphate build up in soil).
  • Apply to soil, never to foliage, and never to root crops or anything eaten raw. Fruit trees, berry bushes, sweetcorn, potatoes, and ornamentals are ideal.
  • Do not store it, do not let it pond, and keep it away from the water source and from where children play.
  • The simplest system: a basin in the sink → carry to a bucket → flush the toilet or water the fruit trees. No plumbing needed, and it's legal everywhere.

8. Water safety — the non-negotiables

  • Assume every untreated source is contaminated. Clear, cold and fast-flowing means nothing.
  • Separate clean and dirty containers absolutely. Colour-code them. Mark "RAW" and "DRINK" in permanent marker. Never let them touch or swap roles.
  • Wash hands before handling drinking water. Recontamination at the tap is the classic failure.
  • Diarrhoea is the emergency. It kills by dehydration, fast, especially in children and elderly. Have oral rehydration salts in stock, and know the home recipe: 1 L clean water + 6 level teaspoons sugar + ½ level teaspoon salt. Sip continuously. This saves lives — it is the single highest-value item in your medical kit (see Ch. 06).
  • Don't drink from the heating system, the pool, or anything with antifreeze, inhibitors or algaecide. Ever.

9. Build sequence and shopping

02 — Electricity

You do not need "power". You need a short list of specific things to keep working. Identify that list, shrink it, then build the smallest system that covers it. Everyone who does it the other way round wastes thousands of euros.

1. Step one: the load audit (do this before spending anything)

List every device, its wattage, and hours per day. Multiply. That gives you watt-hours per day (Wh/day) — the only number that matters for sizing.

Wh/day = Watts × hours per day
1,000 Wh = 1 kWh

Measure real wattage with a plug-in energy meter (€10–15). Do not trust labels — they show peak, not average. Leave the meter on the fridge for 24 hours; the result will surprise you.

Typical loads

DeviceWattsTypical Wh/dayNotes
Phone charge10 W15–25Trivial. Two phones ≈ 40 Wh
LED bulb / lamp5–9 W30–60 for a house4 lamps × 4 h = ~100 Wh
LED headlamp / lantern1–3 W10The cheapest light there is
Laptop30–65 W150–300
Radio / DAB / shortwave3–10 W20–50
Internet router + modem10–20 W240–480Surprisingly hungry; runs 24 h
Fridge-freezer, A+++, modern100 W when running300–500Compressor cycles ~25–35% of the time
Fridge-freezer, 10+ years old150 W700–1,400Replacing an old fridge can halve your whole system cost
Chest freezer, full, A++100 W400–700Full freezers are far more efficient than empty ones
Washing machine (cold/30 °C)150–500 W300–600 per loadThe heating element is the load; cold wash is cheap
Washing machine (60 °C)2,000 W1,000–1,500 per loadAvoid
Well pump (submersible, ½ HP)400–800 W running, 2–3 kW surge200–600Surge is the problem, not the running load
Circulation pump (heating)20–60 W500–1,400Runs constantly in winter. A gas boiler is useless without it
Boiler control/ignition50–150 W200–800
CPAP (no humidifier)30–60 W250–500Life-critical for some — size for it explicitly
Oxygen concentrator300–600 W4,000–10,000Huge. Needs a real system or a generator
Medical fridge (insulin)50–80 W200–400Can share the main fridge
LED grow light (microgreens)20–40 W240–56012–16 h/day
Kettle2,000 W200 per boilNever on battery — boil on gas or wood
Microwave1,000 W100 per 6 minOccasional use only
Electric hob / oven2,000–3,000 WThousandsNever. Cook with fuel, not electricity.
Electric heater2,000 W20,000+Never. See Ch. 04.
Immersion heater3,000 WThousandsNever
Power tools (drill, grinder)500–1,500 WShort burstsFine with a decent inverter

The three tiers of load

Sort your list into these three buckets. This sorting is the whole exercise.

TIER 1 — LIFE CRITICAL. Must never stop. Medical devices (CPAP, oxygen, insulin cooling), phone charging, one light, the radio. Typical total: 100–800 Wh/day. Anyone can afford to cover this.

TIER 2 — HIGH VALUE. Protects food, health and sanity. Fridge, freezer, water pump, more lighting, laptop, router, tool charging, washing machine occasionally. Typical total: 1,000–2,500 Wh/day.

TIER 3 — COMFORT. Nice, sacrificable. TV, gaming, dishwasher, hair dryer, air conditioning, electric cooking, electric heating. Typical total: 3,000–20,000 Wh/day. Tier 3 should never touch your off-grid system. Trying to include it is what makes people conclude "solar is unaffordable". It isn't — Tier 3 is.

Write your three totals here:

Tier 1: _______ Wh/day      Tier 2: _______ Wh/day      Tier 3: _______ Wh/day

2. Step two: cut the load before you buy generation

Every watt-hour you remove is a watt-hour you never have to generate, store, convert or repair. Efficiency is 5–10× cheaper than generation.

ActionCostSaves
Replace all bulbs with LED€3080–90% of lighting load
Replace a 10-year-old fridge with A+++€400400–900 Wh/day — often pays back the whole battery
Keep the freezer full (water bottles fill the gaps)Free10–20%, plus 48 h of cold if power fails
Move the fridge/freezer out of the warm kitchen, into a cool cellar/garage in winterFreeUp to 40% in winter
Add a thermal cooker/haybox and pressure cooker€60–12050–70% of cooking energy
Wash cold, dry on a lineFree60–80% of laundry energy
12 V DC LED strips and USB charging direct from the battery€40Avoids 10–15% inverter loss and lets you switch the inverter off entirely
Switch the inverter OFF when not in useFreeIdle draw is 10–30 W = 240–700 Wh/day wasted on nothing
Use headlamps instead of room lighting€2090% of lighting
Kill standby loads with switched power strips€15100–400 Wh/day

Do all of these before buying a single panel. A household that cuts Tier 2 from 2,500 to 1,200 Wh/day halves the cost of its entire power system.

3. The four power tiers — pick one

P0 — Lights & phonesP1 — EssentialsP2 — Household coreP3 — Whole-house off-grid
CoversPhones, LED lights, radio+ fridge, laptop, router, tools+ freezer, water pump, washing machine, workshop+ heat pump, cooking, most things except resistive heat
Daily energy100–300 Wh1,000–1,500 Wh3,000–6,000 Wh10,000–20,000 Wh
Solar50–150 W400–800 W1.5–3 kW5–10 kW
Battery250–600 Wh1.2–2.5 kWh5–10 kWh15–30 kWh
Inverternone / 300 W1,000–1,500 W2,400–3,600 W5–8 kW hybrid
Cost€150–400€900–2,000€4,000–9,000€12,000–30,000
WhoEveryone, immediately. Apartment L0/L1Apartment L2, house L1House L2 — the sweet spotHouse L3, or homes with electric heat

Recommendation for almost everybody: build P0 now (this month, €200), then jump to P1 or P2 as budget allows. P0 alone removes the panic from a blackout.

4. Sizing the system properly

Solar — the winter problem

A solar panel's rating is its output in perfect conditions. Real daily yield depends on peak sun hours (PSH) for your location and month.

Central Europe (≈46 °N), fixed panels at ~35° tilt facing south:

MonthPSH/dayYield per 1 kW of panels
June–July5.0 – 5.54.0 – 4.5 kWh/day
April / August4.0 – 4.53.2 – 3.6 kWh/day
March / September3.02.4 kWh/day
October2.01.6 kWh/day
November–January0.8 – 1.20.6 – 1.0 kWh/day

Apply a system derate of 0.75–0.8 for wiring, controller, temperature, dirt and inverter losses — the yields above already include it.

The rule that matters: size for December, not for June. Winter yield is 4–6× worse than summer. In Central Europe you may also get 5–10 consecutive overcast days in December with near-zero output.

Worked example — P2 system, family of four:

Winter critical load (fridge, freezer, lights, comms, pump) = 2,000 Wh/day
December yield per kW                                        = 0.8 kWh/day
Panels needed for December autonomy  = 2,000 / 800           = 2.5 kW

2.5 kW of panels costs about €700–1,000 today. In June the same array makes 11 kWh/day — a massive surplus, which is exactly why a winter generator or wood heat is part of the design, not an admission of failure.

Practical compromises:

  • Size panels for ~60–70% of winter need and cover the gap with a generator running 1–2 hours on the worst days. This is far cheaper than doubling the array.
  • Over-panel the charge controller (within its voltage limits) — panels are now the cheapest component. A 30% oversize massively improves dull-day output.
  • Steepen the tilt in winter (latitude + 15°, so ~60° at 46 °N). Adjustable mounts gain 20–30% in December and shed snow. Two tilt changes a year is a 10-minute job with real returns.
  • Keep panels clear of snow. A dusted panel makes nothing. Steep tilt + a soft brush on a pole.
  • Shade is catastrophic, not proportional. One shaded cell can cut a whole string's output by 50%+. Survey the winter sun path (the sun is very low from November to February — trees and buildings that don't shade in June will in December).

Batteries — LiFePO₄, essentially always

Lead-acid (AGM/flooded)LiFePO₄ (LFP)
Usable depth of discharge50% (going deeper kills it)80–100%
Cycle life300–8003,000–6,000
Usable kWh from a "5 kWh" bank2.5 kWh4.5–5 kWh
Cost per usable kWh over lifeHigh3–5× lower
Weight2–3× heavierLight
ChargingFussy, needs absorption/float stages, gassesSimple, fast, sealed
Cold chargingTolerantMust not charge below 0 °C without a heater/BMS protection
Upfront cost€100–150/kWh€200–350/kWh

Buy LiFePO₄ unless you are salvaging free lead-acid. It is cheaper per delivered kilowatt-hour, safer than other lithium chemistries (no thermal runaway in normal use), and vastly less maintenance.

Sizing: aim for 1.5 – 2 days of your critical load in usable capacity. Enough to ride through one bad day and a night without hitting empty.

Critical load 2,000 Wh/day × 1.5 days = 3,000 Wh usable
LiFePO₄ at 90% usable                → 3.3 kWh nominal → buy 4–5 kWh

Cold weather: keep the battery indoors, above 5 °C. A LiFePO₄ bank in an unheated garage in January will refuse to charge and you will think it's broken. Either buy cells with built-in low-temperature cut-off and heating, or site the bank inside.

Charge controller

  • MPPT, always. It harvests 15–30% more than PWM, more in cold and dull conditions, and lets you use cheap high-voltage panels with a low-voltage battery. The price difference is €50.
  • Size the controller for panel current with headroom (e.g. 2.5 kW at 24 V ≈ 100 A → a 100 A MPPT).
  • Buy a spare controller. It is the most likely electronic failure point, it's small and cheap, and without it your panels and battery are decorations.

Inverter

  • Pure sine wave. Modified sine damages motors, fridges, chargers, and medical devices, and buzzes in audio. Never buy modified sine for a household.
  • Size for peak surge, not average. A fridge compressor or a well pump draws 3–6× its running wattage for a second or two at start. A 500 W pump may need a 2,000–3,000 W inverter.
  • Low-frequency (transformer) inverters handle surge far better than high-frequency ones — worth the extra cost and weight if you must start pumps.
  • Idle consumption matters enormously. A 3 kW inverter idling at 25 W burns 600 Wh/day doing nothing — potentially a third of your generation. Switch it off when it isn't needed, and run lights and USB directly from 12 V DC.
  • A hybrid/all-in-one inverter-charger (inverter + MPPT + mains charger + transfer switch in one box) is the simplest choice for P2/P3 and handles grid/generator/solar changeover automatically.

System voltage

  • 12 V for P0/P1 (huge choice of cheap 12 V appliances, pumps and lights).
  • 24 V or 48 V for P2/P3 (thinner cables, less loss, cheaper per watt).
  • Rule of thumb: over ~1.5 kW of load, go 24 V or 48 V.

5. Generators — the honest assessment

A generator is a fuel-to-electricity converter, not an energy source. Its endurance equals your fuel store, and fuel is the hardest thing to store.

Fuel maths. A small inverter generator makes roughly 2.5–3.5 kWh per litre of petrol at good load, much worse when lightly loaded. A 5 kWh day therefore costs ~2 L/day → 60 L/month → 180 L for three months. Storing 180 L of petrol safely is a serious undertaking, and petrol only keeps 6–12 months with stabiliser.

Therefore: never run a generator to supply loads directly all day. Instead:

The right pattern — batch charging. Run the generator at 60–80% load (its efficient point) for 1–2 hours, charging the battery bank and running the fridge/freezer/washing machine/tools at the same time. Then shut it down and live off the battery. This cuts fuel use by 60–80%, quarters the noise exposure, and multiplies engine life.

Choosing one

  • Inverter generators (Honda EU22i and clones): quiet (~50–60 dB), clean power safe for electronics, fuel-efficient at part load. 2–2.5 kW is enough for battery charging plus a fridge. ~€500–1,500.
  • Dual-fuel (petrol + LPG) is the strategic choice: propane stores indefinitely, burns clean, doesn't gum the carburettor, and is easy to buy and store in 11 kg cylinders. ~€600–1,200.
  • Conventional open-frame 5–7 kW sets are cheap and loud, and produce dirty power. Fine for tools and pumps, poor for electronics and terrible for OPSEC.
  • Diesel stores longer than petrol (1–2 years, longer with biocide and a sealed tank), is more efficient, but the small sets are loud and expensive.

Fuel storage

FuelShelf lifeNotes
Petrol3–6 months bare · 12–24 months with stabiliser (Stabil/Aspen)Buy ethanol-free if available; ethanol absorbs water and destroys carburettors. Alkylate petrol (Aspen) stores 3–5 years but is expensive.
Diesel12 months · 2–5 years with biocide + stabiliser, sealed, coolWatch for diesel bug (microbial growth) — use a biocide
Propane / LPGIndefiniteThe best fuel to store. 11 kg cylinder ≈ 150 kWh of heat. No degradation, no spillage, no fire risk if stored outside and upright.
  • Store fuel outside the house in approved containers, in the shade, away from ignition sources, and check your local legal storage limits (often 20–30 L of petrol domestically).
  • Rotate petrol through your car every 6 months — that's the trick that makes fuel storage practical.
  • Keep the generator's own consumables: oil, oil filter, air filter, spark plugs, and a spare carburettor (€20 and the #1 failure item on small engines).

Safety — this kills people every single winter

  • NEVER run a generator indoors, in a garage, in a cellar, in a porch, or near an open window. Carbon monoxide is odourless and kills in minutes. Not "with the door open". Outside, ≥ 5 m from any opening, with the exhaust pointed away.
  • CO alarms in every sleeping area and in the room with any combustion. €20 each. Non-negotiable. See Ch. 04.
  • Never backfeed a generator into a wall socket to power the house. It kills line workers, destroys your appliances, and is illegal. Use a manual transfer switch or a breaker interlock kit, installed by an electrician (€150–400). Without one, run extension leads to specific appliances only.
  • Let it cool before refuelling. Petrol on a hot exhaust is a fire.

Other generation, honestly assessed

  • Wind: small turbines rarely produce what's claimed. Only worth it on an exposed site with genuine average wind ≥ 5 m/s, on a tall mast. Most domestic wind is money better spent on panels. But: wind produces in winter when solar doesn't, so a good site makes an excellent complement.
  • Micro-hydro: if you have a stream with head and flow, this is the best off-grid power there is — 24/7, year-round, weather-independent. Even 200 W continuous = 4.8 kWh/day, better than a large solar array in winter. Investigate seriously if you have water rights.
  • Pedal generator: a fit person sustains 50–100 W. One hour of hard pedalling ≈ 75 Wh ≈ what a €30 solar panel makes in an afternoon for free. Good for morale and fitness; not a power strategy.
  • Thermoelectric (stove-top TEG, Peltier): 5–15 W. Charges a phone off a wood stove. A nice redundancy, not a system.
  • Car alternator: your car is a 1–2 kW generator you already own. A €30 inverter on the cigarette socket gives 150 W; wired directly to the battery with a proper 1,000 W inverter it will run a fridge while idling. Idling burns ~1 L/h, so use it as an emergency charger, not a habit. Same CO rules — never in a garage.

6. Wiring it safely

  • DC side: correct cable gauge for current and run length (undersized DC cable is the classic fire cause — voltage drop under 3%), fuse or breaker within 30 cm of the battery positive, fuses on every branch, a main isolator switch you can reach in the dark.
  • Battery location: ventilated, above freezing, not in the living space if lead-acid (hydrogen gas), secured so it can't shift, not above or below anything precious.
  • AC side: anything connecting to house wiring must be done by a qualified electrician, with an RCD/GFCI and correct earthing, and must be legal in your jurisdiction. A transfer switch or interlock is mandatory.
  • Grid-tied solar does NOT work in a blackout. Standard grid-tie inverters shut down when the grid drops (anti-islanding), by law. If you already have solar, check whether it has a hybrid inverter with battery and an EPS/backup output — if not, your panels are useless in exactly the situation you bought them for. Retrofitting a hybrid inverter + battery is the fix.
  • Label everything. Breakers, cables, polarity, what circuit feeds what. Write the system diagram on paper and pin it next to the equipment. In six months, at 3 a.m., in the dark, you will not remember.

7. Keeping food cold without much power

This is usually the biggest single reason to have power at all.

  • A full chest freezer holds food safely for 48 hours unopened (24 h for an upright fridge-freezer). Fill air gaps with bottles of water — the frozen mass is thermal storage.
  • Freeze bottles of water as your "battery" — move them into the fridge compartment during outages.
  • Chest beats upright by a wide margin (cold air doesn't fall out when opened). A chest freezer plus a thermostat controller can also run as a fridge at a fraction of a normal fridge's energy (~100–200 Wh/day).
  • Duty-cycle it: run the freezer hard while the sun is up, let it coast overnight. A well-insulated full freezer barely drifts.
  • Winter is free refrigeration. From November to March in Central Europe, an insulated box on the north side of the house, a cellar, or an unheated pantry does the job for nothing. Plan the year so freezer contents are lowest in summer — preserve by canning and drying instead (Ch. 03).
  • Root cellar (land track): a properly built cellar holds 2–8 °C year-round with zero energy and stores potatoes, roots, apples and cabbages for months. It is the highest-return "appliance" on a homestead. See Ch. 03 §7.

8. Lighting — cheap, and hugely important for morale

  • Headlamps first. 1–3 W, hands free, directional. Buy one per person plus two spares. This is the most cost-effective light in existence.
  • Rechargeable AA/AAA (Eneloop-type) + a USB charger. ~€60 for 16 cells and a charger, and then you never buy batteries again. Standardise the whole house on one cell size so everything is interchangeable.
  • 12 V LED strip along a ceiling, run direct from the battery: 5 W lights a whole room and needs no inverter.
  • Solar garden lights — bring them in at night. Crude but free and endless.
  • Candles and oil lamps: useful backup, but they are a fire risk, consume oxygen and produce soot and CO. Keep them for ambience and last resort, never unattended, never near children. Paraffin/lamp oil stores indefinitely.
  • Light discipline: blackout curtains. In a dark street, a lit window is a billboard. See Ch. 10.

9. Build sequence

🏢 Apartment

  1. Now (€150): 2 × 20,000 mAh power banks · 100 W folding panel with USB/DC · 4 headlamps · 16 rechargeable AA + charger · wind-up/solar radio.
  2. L1 (€400–800): A 500–1,000 Wh portable power station (LiFePO₄, pure sine, e.g. EcoFlow/Bluetti/Anker class) + 200 W of folding or balcony panels. This single purchase covers lights, phones, laptop, router and a CPAP — and it's plug-and-play, which matters when you cannot modify the building.
  3. L2 (€1,200–2,500): 1.5–2 kWh station + 400 W balcony solar (check local rules — plug-in "balcony solar" up to 600–800 W is now legal in several European countries) + a 12 V compressor cool box (40 W) as an efficient fridge.
  4. Never a generator in a flat. There is nowhere safe to run it.

🏡 Land / house

  1. Now (€200): P0 kit as above, plus a plug-in energy meter and LED conversion.
  2. L1 (€900–1,800): 400–600 W panels · 100 Ah/12 V LiFePO₄ (1.2 kWh) · 30 A MPPT · 1,000–1,500 W pure sine inverter · proper fusing and cable · 12 V LED lighting circuit.
  3. L2 (€4,000–8,000): 2–3 kW panels on an adjustable mount · 5–10 kWh LiFePO₄ at 24/48 V · 3 kW hybrid inverter-charger · transfer switch installed by an electrician · 2.2 kW dual-fuel inverter generator + 4 × 11 kg propane cylinders · A+++ chest freezer · spare MPPT controller, spare fuses, spare cable, spare connectors.
  4. L3 (€12,000+): 5 kW+ array · 15 kWh storage · well pump on DC · micro-hydro or wind if the site supports it · full house backup circuits · second inverter held as a spare.

10. Electricity checklist

[ ] Energy meter bought; fridge/freezer actually measured
[ ] Loads sorted into Tier 1 / 2 / 3 and totals written down
[ ] All efficiency measures done BEFORE buying generation
[ ] Tier 1 (life-critical) covered by battery alone, with 2 days of autonomy
[ ] System sized on DECEMBER sun, not annual average
[ ] Pure sine inverter; surge rating checked against the biggest motor
[ ] Battery sited above 5 °C
[ ] Spare charge controller, fuses, cable and connectors in stock
[ ] DC fusing correct and a main isolator fitted within reach
[ ] Transfer switch/interlock installed if a generator feeds the house
[ ] CO alarms fitted; generator siting rule written and understood by everyone
[ ] Fuel stored legally and safely, dated, with stabiliser, rotation scheduled
[ ] System diagram drawn on paper and pinned up
[ ] Existing grid-tie solar checked for blackout (EPS) capability
[ ] Headlamps + rechargeables for every person, one battery size
[ ] Blackout weekend drill completed in WINTER
