03 — Food Storage

Stored food is the cheapest, fastest, most certain preparation you can make. It is also the one people get most wrong — by storing the wrong things, in the wrong way, in the wrong quantities, and then never eating any of it.

1. The calorie maths

Everything starts here. Not "cans" or "boxes" — kilocalories.

Personkcal/day sedentarykcal/day with manual work
Adult man2,4003,200 – 4,000
Adult woman1,9002,600 – 3,200
Teenager2,200 – 2,8003,000 – 3,600
Child 4–101,400 – 1,900—
Toddler1,000 – 1,300—
Pregnant / breastfeeding+300 / +500—

Crisis life is manual work. Hauling water, chopping wood, digging, hand washing, walking instead of driving — and in a cold house you burn several hundred extra kcal a day just staying warm. Plan on 2,800–3,000 kcal/day for working adults. Undereating is not a minor discomfort: it destroys your ability to do the physical work the situation demands, wrecks your immune system exactly when infection is the threat, and sinks morale.

Your household's requirement

Adults ___ × 2,800 kcal   = __________
Others (from table)       = __________
                            ----------
            TOTAL / day   = __________ kcal

× 90 days   (L2) = __________ kcal
× 365 days  (L3) = __________ kcal

Family of four (2 adults, 2 children): ≈ 9,000 kcal/day → 810,000 kcal for 3 months → 3,285,000 kcal for a year.

Converting to weight: staple dry goods average roughly 3,400 kcal/kg (grains 3,400 · pasta 3,600 · beans 3,400 · sugar 4,000 · oil 8,800 · rice 3,600).

3,285,000 kcal ÷ 3,400 kcal/kg ≈ 965 kg of dry staples for a family of four per year

That is about 1 tonne of food, occupying roughly 1.5–2 m³ — a stack of about 50 × 20 L buckets, or one corner of a cellar, garage or spare room (2 m × 1 m × 1 m). It is far less space than people imagine, and at bulk prices costs roughly €900–1,600. That is the single best money in this entire plan.

2. The one-year staple base (per adult)

This is the classic, proven long-term storage baseline, adapted metric. It delivers roughly 2,300–2,600 kcal/day with adequate protein. Multiply by your household; scale children at 0.6–0.8.

Categorykg/adult/yearExamples
Grains180 kgWheat berries, rice, oats, maize, barley, pasta, flour
Legumes30 kgDry beans, lentils, chickpeas, split peas, soy
Fats & oils12 kg (≈13 L)Olive oil, sunflower oil, coconut oil, ghee, lard, tallow
Sugars20 kgSugar, honey, jam, syrup, dried fruit
Dairy8 kgWhole/skim milk powder, hard cheese, UHT, butter powder
Salt4 kgIodised table + coarse/pickling salt for preserving
Protein extras15–25 kgTinned fish/meat, dried eggs, TVP, nuts, seeds, jerky
Vegetables & fruit40–60 kgTinned, dried, freeze-dried, plus your own preserves
Extrasas neededYeast, baking powder, bicarbonate, vinegar, spices, tea, coffee, bouillon, cocoa

For a family of four, one year: ~720 kg grain, 120 kg legumes, 50 L oil, 80 kg sugar, 32 kg dairy powder, 16 kg salt.

Do not build this as a monolith

Split your storage into three layers, because they serve different purposes:

Layer A — Everyday deep pantry (2–8 weeks). Ordinary food you eat weekly, just more of it. Tins, pasta, rice, coffee, oil, UHT milk, frozen goods. Rotates naturally. Build this first — it covers 90% of realistic events.

Layer B — Bulk staples (3–12 months). Sacks of grain, beans, sugar, salt and oil, packed for the long term. Cheap per calorie, boring, requires cooking skill and fuel. This is your insurance layer.

Layer C — Morale and micronutrients. Coffee, tea, chocolate, spices, alcohol, sweets, sauces, vitamins, sprouting seed. Weighs almost nothing, costs little, and is worth far more than its calories. Month four of lentils with no seasoning breaks people. This layer is not a luxury; it is what makes Layer B edible for a year.

3. Shelf life — what actually lasts

Effectively indefinite (25–30+ years, packed properly)

White rice · wheat berries · maize/corn · rolled oats · dry beans (they get harder — cook with a pressure cooker) · lentils · pasta · sugar · salt (forever) · honey (forever) · milk powder · dried potato flakes · baking soda · freeze-dried food.

Long (2–10 years)

Tinned food (long past the date if the tin is sound — see below) · white flour (1–2 yrs; wheat berries are far better) · dried fruit · nuts in the shell · vinegar · spirits · bouillon · vitamins (2–3 yrs) · TVP.

Short — rotate hard (6 months – 2 years)

  • Brown rice: 6–12 months. The bran oil goes rancid. Store white rice for the long term; brown rice only in your rotating pantry.
  • Wholemeal flour: 3–6 months. Store the whole grain and mill it as needed.
  • Cooking oils: 1–2 years. Olive and coconut keep best; sunflower and rapeseed go rancid faster. Store cool and dark, buy in tins or dark glass, and rotate relentlessly. Fat is both the hardest thing to store and the hardest thing to produce. Consider ghee (2 yrs at room temperature) and rendered lard/tallow (1 yr cool, longer canned or frozen).
  • Nuts and seeds shelled: 6–12 months (freeze to extend).
  • Coffee beans: 1–2 years sealed; ground, much less. Green (unroasted) coffee beans keep 5+ years — buy green and roast in a pan.
  • Anything with fat in it is on a clock. This is the rule that governs food storage.

About "best before" dates

On shelf-stable food these are quality dates, not safety dates. Tinned goods stored cool are usually good for 5–10 years and often far longer; quality and vitamin content decline, safety usually does not. Exceptions — throw away without tasting: any tin that is bulging, leaking, rusted through, or spurts when opened; anything that smells or looks wrong; any home preserve with a failed seal. Botulism has no taste and no smell. High-acid tinned foods (tomatoes, fruit, anything vinegared) degrade fastest and can corrode the tin from inside — rotate those on the printed date.

4. How to pack for 25 years

The enemies are oxygen, moisture, light, heat, and pests. Remove all five.

The standard method — the only one worth learning:

Dry staple  →  Mylar bag (≥ 5 mil / 100 µm)  →  O₂ absorber  →  heat-seal
            →  food-grade bucket (20 L) with gasket lid  →  cool, dark, dry
  1. Mylar bags, 5–7 mil, 20 L size (typically 50 × 60 cm). €1–2 each. Mylar is an oxygen and light barrier; plastic buckets alone are not (they breathe).
  2. Oxygen absorbers. Sizing: 2,000 cc per 20 L bucket (or 2 × 1,000 cc); 300–500 cc for a 4 L bag. Work fast — once the packet is open, absorbers are live; keep unused ones in a sealed jar with the air squeezed out. A correctly sealed bag goes hard and "bricked" within a day — that is your proof it worked.
  3. Seal with a hair straightener, clothes iron on a board, or an impulse sealer (€30). Leave one corner open, press out the air, seal the last corner.
  4. Food-grade bucket with a gamma-seal or gasket lid. The bucket is pest and crush protection; the mylar does the real work.
  5. Moisture must be < 10% going in, or you create a botulism risk in an anaerobic bag. Shop-dry grains and beans are fine. Never pack anything you dried yourself unless it is cracker-dry, and never pack anything with fat (nuts, brown rice, ground flour) this way for the long term.
  6. Label the outside AND put a label inside: contents, weight, date packed, date to review. You will not remember. Use permanent marker and tape; ink fades.

Alternatives: food-grade 200 L barrels with a bag liner for grain in bulk; glass jars with O₂ absorbers for small quantities (rodent-proof and reusable — excellent for apartments); vacuum sealing (good for 1–5 years, but the bags are oxygen-permeable over decades and puncture easily).

Pest control: weevil eggs are already in most grain when you buy it. Freezing a sack for 72 hours at −18 °C kills eggs and larvae — do this before packing. Diatomaceous earth (food grade) mixed at ~1% also works and is harmless. O₂-absorbed mylar kills insects anyway by suffocation, which is a large part of why the method works.

Storage conditions. Cool is everything: shelf life roughly halves for every +10 °C. Aim for 10–18 °C, dark, dry, off the floor on pallets or boards, away from chemicals and fuel. A hot loft is the worst place in the house; a cellar or a north-facing interior room is the best.

5. The nutrition trap

A year of grain, beans, sugar and oil will keep you alive and will slowly make you ill. Long-term storage diets are reliably deficient in:

NutrientDeficiencyFix
Vitamin CScurvy in 8–12 weeks. Bleeding gums, wounds that won't heal, exhaustion, then death.Sprouts (Ch. 09) · sauerkraut and other lacto-ferments · rosehips · dried/tinned fruit · vitamin C tablets (dirt cheap, buy 3 years' worth — this is the single best-value item in your store)
Vitamin ANight blindness, immune failureCarrots (store well), squash, dried greens, liver, cod liver oil, multivitamin
Vitamin DImmune and bone problems — and near-universal in Central European winters even in normal timesSupplement (everyone, every winter), oily fish, eggs, sunlight
B12Neurological damage, anaemiaTinned fish/meat, eggs, dairy powder, fortified yeast, supplement
Folate, B-complexAnaemia, fatigueSprouts, legumes, greens, supplement
Omega-3Inflammation, mood, cognitionTinned oily fish (sardines/mackerel — cheap, long shelf life, outstanding value), linseed/flax, walnuts
Iron & zincAnaemia, poor immunity, slow healingMeat, legumes + vitamin C to aid absorption, cast-iron cooking
Fibre & gut healthConstipation, miseryWhole grains, ferments, vegetables
FatEnergy collapse, vitamin A/D/E/K absorption failureOils, nuts, fatty meat. Do not build a low-fat store.
Complete proteinMuscle loss, poor healingCombine grains + legumes at the same meal (rice + beans, bread + lentils) — together they are a complete protein

The three cheap interventions that fix most of this:

  1. A jar of sprouting seed (mung, lentil, alfalfa, radish, broccoli, fenugreek) — 5 kg costs €25, stores for years, and gives fresh vitamin-C-rich food in 3–5 days, indoors, with no light, soil or power, in any season. It is the single most important nutritional item in the whole plan.
  2. A multivitamin + vitamin C + vitamin D per person per day for a year. ~€40/person/year. Rotate every 2–3 years.
  3. Cabbage and salt. A 5 kg crock of sauerkraut needs nothing but cabbage, salt and time, keeps for months, and is a serious source of vitamin C and gut flora.

Special diets need special planning: infants (formula has a hard expiry and no substitute — plan explicitly and generously), coeliac, diabetic (a high-carb storage diet is dangerous — build around legumes, fats and proteins instead), allergies. These cannot be improvised and must be solved in GREEN.

6. Preservation — turning harvests into stores

This is the skill set that converts "I grew food" into "I have food in February".

Lacto-fermentation — start here

Easiest, cheapest, needs no power, no equipment beyond a jar, and adds nutrition (vitamin C, B vitamins, probiotics).

  • Brine: 2–3% salt by weight (20–30 g salt per litre of water, or per kg of vegetables for dry-salted kraut).
  • Keep everything under the brine — exposure to air is what spoils it.
  • Ferment at 18–22 °C for 3–14 days, then move somewhere cold (cellar, 2–10 °C) where it keeps for 6–12 months.
  • Works on: cabbage (sauerkraut), cucumbers, carrots, beetroot, turnips, beans, peppers, garlic, mixed vegetables, hot sauce.
  • Buy salt in bulk. It never expires, it is essential to almost every preservation method, and it is one of the few things you genuinely cannot produce at home inland.

Water-bath canning — high-acid foods only

For pH ≤ 4.6: fruit, jams, pickles, tomatoes with added acid, chutneys, anything vinegared. Jars are submerged in boiling water for a set time. Equipment: a big pot with a rack, jars, and new lids every time (screw bands are reusable, sealing lids are not — stock several hundred, or buy the European style with replaceable rubber rings).

Pressure canning — the homestead game-changer

The only safe way to preserve low-acid foods: meat, poultry, fish, stock, beans, and all non-acidified vegetables. It reaches 116–121 °C, which is what destroys Clostridium botulinum spores. Boiling water canning cannot do this, and the failure mode is fatal.

  • A quality pressure canner (All American / Presto class) is €200–400 and is arguably the highest-value single tool a homestead can own: it lets you store meat, stock and vegetables without a freezer and without electricity once the jars are done.
  • Follow a tested, published recipe for time and pressure. Every time. (National Center for Home Food Preservation / Ball Blue Book — print them.) This is not a place for improvisation or old family guesswork.
  • Adjust pressure for altitude.
  • Buy: the canner, a spare gasket and safety plug, 200+ jars, 500 lids, a jar lifter, a funnel.

Drying / dehydrating

Ancient, cheap, and space-efficient — dried food is ~20% of its wet weight.

  • Solar drier (a glazed box with mesh trays and a vent) costs €40 in materials and uses no power. Ideal in a Central European August.
  • Electric dehydrator (€60–150, ~300–500 W) is faster and weather-independent — run it off surplus solar at midday.
  • Works on: fruit, tomatoes, mushrooms, herbs, onions, peppers, beans, courgette, and meat (jerky — must be lean; fat goes rancid).
  • Store dried food in jars or mylar with an O₂ absorber. It must be crisp-dry, or it moulds.

Root cellaring — zero energy, huge capacity

A cellar, a buried barrel, a clamp or an unheated north-facing pantry holding 2–8 °C at 85–95% humidity will keep, with no power at all:

  • Potatoes (dark, 4–8 °C — never below 4 °C or they go sweet): 5–8 months
  • Carrots, beetroot, parsnip, turnip, celeriac (in damp sand): 4–6 months
  • Cabbage (hung by the root): 3–5 months · Onions & garlic (dry, cool, airy, 0–5 °C): 6–9 months · Apples (wrapped, away from potatoes): 3–6 months · Winter squash & pumpkins (dry, 10–15 °C — warmer than the rest): 4–8 months
  • Keep apples separate from everything: their ethylene sprouts potatoes and spoils neighbours.
  • A clamp (potatoes/roots in a heap on straw, covered with straw then 20 cm of soil, with a ventilation chimney) costs nothing and works.

Salting, curing and smoking

Salt pork, bacon, ham, salt fish, biltong. Effective, needs care with nitrates for anything cured cold and slow. Learn from a good book before you have a carcass and no freezer. Simple dry-salting of pork belly and legs is reliable and ancient; cold-smoked charcuterie is an advanced skill with a real risk.

Fat preservation

Rendered lard and tallow keep 6–12 months in a cool dark place, longer canned. Confit — meat cooked slowly in fat and stored submerged in it — keeps for months in a cellar. Fat sealing is an excellent, half-forgotten technique.

Freezing

Fast and easy, but it is the least resilient method — it depends on uninterrupted power for its entire life. Use the freezer as your working buffer, not your strategic store, and plan to empty it into jars, the dehydrator and the smoker if power becomes unreliable. Keep a bag of ice or a frozen bottle in the freezer with a coin on top: if you return to find the coin at the bottom, it thawed and refroze while you were away — do not eat the contents.

Eggs without refrigeration

Unwashed fresh eggs keep 2–4 weeks at room temperature and 2–3 months in a cool cellar — the natural bloom is intact, so don't wash them until use. Water glassing (submerging clean, unwashed, fresh eggs in a 1:10 solution of pickling lime / calcium hydroxide) keeps them edible for 12–18 months without power. This is the classic way to carry spring's egg glut through winter.

7. Rotation — the discipline that makes it real

Stored food you never touch is money spent on rubbish you will throw away.

The rules:

  1. Eat what you store, store what you eat. Every item in Layer A and most of Layer B should appear in your normal cooking.
  2. FIFO. New goes to the back. Physically arrange shelves so this happens automatically (a can rotator, or simply loading from one end).
  3. Date everything with a marker on purchase, not just the printed date.
  4. Keep a written inventory. Use worksheets/inventory-template.csv. Update it when you open a bucket and when you shop. An unknown store is not a store.
  5. The quarterly "eat-the-store week" — cook only from storage for one week every three months. It finds the gaps (no yeast! no oil! nobody likes the dried soya!) while fixing them is still trivial.
  6. Learn to cook it now. Dry beans need soaking and 1–2 hours of fuel (30 minutes in a pressure cooker). Wheat berries need a mill. Powdered milk needs technique to taste acceptable. Sourdough needs a starter you keep alive. Discovering this in month two is a disaster; discovering it this Saturday is a pleasant afternoon.
  7. Buy in tranches, not in one panic. A little extra every shop builds a year of food invisibly, without straining budget or supply.

8. Tools that make stored food usable

Tool€Why it matters
Hand grain mill (quality steel burr, e.g. Wondermill Junior / Komo hand)120–350Wheat berries store 30 years; flour stores 6 months. Without a mill, bulk grain is chicken feed. Buy the hand version even if you own an electric one.
Electric grain mill200–400Convenience while power lasts
Pressure cooker (6–10 L)60–120Cuts bean and grain cooking fuel by 50–70%. Pays for itself in fuel.
Pressure canner200–400The only safe way to preserve meat and vegetables long-term
Thermal cooker / haybox60–120 (or DIY free)15 min of fuel + 4 h of insulated coasting = a cooked stew. Enormous fuel savings.
Dehydrator or solar drier40–150Preserves a glut with no jars
Manual can opener ×315The classic single point of failure. Buy three good ones.
Grain/food scale15Ration control and fermentation salt ratios
Vacuum sealer60–120Medium-term storage, repacking
Impulse heat sealer30For mylar bags
Mortar & pestle / hand grinder20Spices, coffee, small grains
Large stockpot + strainers60Batch cooking, canning, rendering

9. Build sequence

🏢 Apartment

Space is the constraint. ~1 m³ per person-year of staples if densely packed.

  • Under every bed (flat boxes, 20 L bags), in the back of every wardrobe, on top of kitchen units, under the sofa, in the hallway cupboard, on a shelf above the doors. A 60 cm × 200 cm under-bed space holds 150 kg of food.
  • Favour calorie-dense, low-volume, no-cook-needed items: oil, nuts, pasta, rice, sugar, honey, powdered milk, tinned fish, peanut butter.
  • Glass jars with O₂ absorbers on shelves are both storage and rodent-proofing, and they look like an ordinary pantry rather than a stockpile.
  • Fuel is the hidden constraint — dry beans need an hour of gas. Store more quick-cooking items (pasta, couscous, bulgur, instant oats, tinned/ready foods) than a house would, plus a thermal cooker (Ch. 04).
  • Target: L1 (2 weeks) easily; L2 (3 months) with discipline; L3 (12 months) only in a large flat with a cellar or storage unit.

🏡 Land / house

  • Dedicate a cool room, cellar or insulated garage corner. Shelving, pallets, a thermometer/hygrometer, and rodent control (steel, not plastic, bins).
  • Buy bulk direct: farm co-ops, mills, agricultural merchants, wholesale food suppliers. 25 kg sacks are typically 30–60% cheaper per kilo than supermarket packs.
  • Build or convert a root cellar — it multiplies your storage capacity for almost nothing and works forever.
  • Then integrate with production: what you grow (Ch. 07) and raise (Ch. 08) tops up the store each year instead of draining it.

10. Food checklist

[ ] Household daily kcal requirement calculated
[ ] 2-week deep pantry of normal food in place (L1)
[ ] 3-month staple base bought and packed (L2)
[ ] Packing method understood; mylar + O₂ absorbers + buckets in stock
[ ] Grain frozen 72 h before packing (weevils)
[ ] Everything labelled and dated, inside and out
[ ] Written inventory exists and is current
[ ] Storage is cool (< 18 °C), dark, dry, off the floor, rodent-proof
[ ] Fat/oil stock present and on a rotation schedule
[ ] Salt: 4 kg per person per year, in bulk
[ ] Vitamin C, vitamin D and multivitamins for 12 months per person
[ ] Sprouting seed (5 kg+) and a sprouting jar/tray
[ ] Hand grain mill owned AND used at least once
[ ] Pressure cooker owned and used
[ ] Pressure canner owned, tested, tested recipes printed  (land/L3)
[ ] Manual can openers ×3
[ ] Special diets (infant, coeliac, diabetic) explicitly planned
[ ] Pet food included in the plan
[ ] Quarterly eat-the-store week scheduled in the calendar
[ ] Comfort layer: coffee, spices, chocolate, treats — do not skip this

🔨 Build it: Food storage builds — root cellar · buried barrel cellar · root clamp · cool pantry · mylar packing station · cold smoker · curing racks

Previous: 02 — Electricity · Next: 04 — Heating, cooking & fuel

↑ Contents

04 — Heating, Cooking & Fuel

In a Central European winter, heat is a bigger energy problem than electricity, food and water combined — by an order of magnitude. And the ways people improvise heat and cooking indoors kill more people in a crisis than the crisis does.

1. The scale of the problem

A typical Central European house loses 8,000–20,000 kWh of heat per winter (an uninsulated older house much more; a passive house a tenth of it). Compare that with a 5 kWh battery.

Poorly insulated 120 m² house in January:  60–100 kWh/day of heat
A 10 kWh battery bank:                     10 kWh, once
A 3 kW solar array in January:              2.4 kWh/day

You cannot heat a house with electricity in a crisis. Do not try. Every electric heater, oil-filled radiator and fan heater in your house is useless without the grid, and running them off batteries or a generator is absurd — 2 kW for one room is more than most whole off-grid systems produce in a day.

The strategy has three parts, in strict order:

1. SHRINK the space you heat          (free, biggest win)
2. REDUCE the heat that escapes it    (cheap, second biggest win)
3. SUPPLY heat from stored fuel       (expensive, do last)

2. Step one: the warm room

Heat one room, not a house. This is how Europe lived for a thousand years and it works.

  • Pick the room: small, south-facing if possible, with a chimney or flue if you have one, away from the coldest walls, with a door that closes.
  • The whole household lives and sleeps there in the coldest weeks. Mattresses on the floor. It is cramped, it is also warm, and bodies add heat (each person is ~80–100 W).
  • Close doors to every other room. Close off the upstairs. Hang blankets over doorways and at the bottom of stairs (stairwells are chimneys).
  • Keep the rest of the house above freezing (5 °C) to protect pipes, not comfortable. If you cannot, drain the plumbing in the unheated parts before the first hard frost: burst pipes flood a house you cannot get a plumber to.

Result: heating one 15 m² room instead of a 120 m² house cuts the fuel requirement by 70–85%. A whole winter becomes 2–4 m³ of wood instead of 15–20 m³. Nothing else you do comes close to this.

The heated-bed trick: a well-insulated bed with a hot water bottle (or a hot stone/brick wrapped in cloth) needs no room heat at all to be comfortable. Heating a body is a hundred times cheaper than heating a room. Layered wool blankets, a duvet, a hat, and a hot water bottle at the feet will get you through a 5 °C bedroom indefinitely.

3. Step two: cut the losses (cheap, do it this autumn)

MeasureCostEffect
Draught-proofing — door seals, letterbox brush, keyhole covers, chimney balloon in unused flues, sealing around pipes and sockets€50–10010–20% of heat loss. Best €/kWh in the building.
Heavy curtains, closed at dusk, opened at sunrise on sunny days€50 or free (blankets)10–15% through windows
Window insulation film (shrink-fit)€10–20/windowTurns single glazing into quasi-double. 20–30% of window loss.
Bubble wrap on windows you don't look through€10Crude, surprisingly effective
Reflective foil behind radiators€155% for radiators on external walls
Rugs and underlay on bare floorsVariesFloors are 10–15% of loss in older houses
Loft insulation to 300 mm€300–800 DIY20–25% of total heat loss. The single best permanent investment.
Sealing and insulating the cellar hatch / floor void€1005–10%
Door snakes, thermal door curtains€30Noticeable in a warm room
Dressing properly: base layer, wool jumper, hat, thick socks, slippers€100Lets you hold the room at 16 °C instead of 21 °C — a 30–40% fuel saving

The thermal comfort rule: every 1 °C lower costs about 6–8% less fuel. Going from 21 °C to 17 °C saves roughly a quarter to a third of your heating fuel, and with wool and a hot drink it is entirely liveable.

4. Step three: heat supply

What happens to your existing heating without electricity?

Check this today, because most people are wrong about it:

SystemWorks without mains power?
Gas boiler + radiators❌ No. Needs power for the pump, fan and controls. Dead in a blackout.
Gas boiler + gravity-fed system (old, rare)⚠️ Partly, if truly gravity circulated
Heat pump❌ No — and it is a large electrical load anyway
Pellet stove / pellet boiler❌ No. Needs power for the auger and fan. Also needs pellet deliveries.
Oil boiler❌ No (pump and burner are electric)
District heating❌ No, and entirely out of your control
Wood stove / log burner✅ Yes
Masonry/tiled stove (kachelofen)✅ Yes — and it stores heat for 12–24 h
Open fireplace⚠️ Works, but is 10–20% efficient and often net negative — it pulls more warm air up the chimney than it delivers
Bottled gas heater (catalytic/flueless)⚠️ Works, but consumes room oxygen and makes water vapour and CO — ventilation essential, never overnight
Paraffin/kerosene heater⚠️ Same caveats, plus fumes and fire risk

If your answer is ❌, you need a non-electric heat source before winter. A gas boiler can often be kept running with a very small battery/inverter (the pump and controls may only be 50–150 W → 1.2–3.6 kWh/day) — check yours and size for it. That is often the cheapest fix for an existing house.

Wood — the backbone for the land track

Energy content: seasoned hardwood ≈ 4 kWh/kg, or roughly 1,800–2,100 kWh per stacked m³ (beech/oak/hornbeam at the top, spruce/pine around 1,300–1,500 kWh/m³ and burning faster).

How much you need per winter:

ScenarioWood
Warm-room only, one efficient stove, 4–5 months2 – 4 m³
Whole small house, well insulated6 – 10 m³
Whole older house, poorly insulated15 – 25 m³
Cooking only, year-round, on a rocket/efficient stove1 – 2 m³

Seasoning is the thing people get wrong. Freshly cut wood is 45–60% water. Burning it gives you half the heat, coats your chimney with creosote (chimney fire), and produces choking smoke.

  • Split, stacked, covered on top but open at the sides, off the ground.
  • Hardwood: 18–24 months. Softwood: 9–12 months.
  • Target < 20% moisture — buy a €15 moisture meter and actually check.
  • This means you must start two years before you need it. Wood is the one preparation that cannot be bought at AMBER. Buy or cut this year's wood and next year's, and keep a rolling two-year stack forever.

Stoves: a modern clean-burn wood stove is 70–80% efficient versus 10–20% for an open fire. Buy one with a cooktop surface — then the same fuel heats the room, cooks the food, heats water in a kettle and dries clothes. That multi-functionality is worth more than a few percent of efficiency.

  • Installation must be legal and safe: correct flue, register plate, hearth, clearances to combustibles, a swept and lined chimney. Get it signed off.
  • Sweep the chimney at least annually, more with soft or damp wood. Buy your own rod set (€50) — you will not get a sweep in a crisis.
  • A masonry/tiled stove (kachelofen) stores 100+ kWh in its mass and releases it for 12–24 hours from one or two firings. If you are building or renovating, it is the best cold-climate heat storage that exists.
  • Rocket mass heater: a DIY design that burns small wood extremely cleanly and stores the heat in a cob/masonry bench. Very fuel-efficient and cheap to build, but bulky, non-standard and often not compliant with building regulations — check before building.

Sourcing wood without a forest: pallets (untreated/HT only — never MB methyl-bromide-treated or painted), tree surgeons' arisings (often free if you collect), storm-fall, council permits for gathering, coppice agreements with a neighbour, offcuts from a joinery. Build the relationships in GREEN.

Tools: bow saw and a good axe before a chainsaw (they always work). Then chainsaw + PPE (helmet, ear and eye protection, chaps/trousers, gloves), files, chain oil, 2 spare chains, and 2-stroke fuel. A log splitter or a splitting maul and wedges. Chainsaw injuries are the classic homestead disaster — get training.

Bottled gas — the backbone for the apartment track and the best bridge fuel

  • Propane (LPG) stores indefinitely, needs no stabiliser, and delivers about 13.8 kWh/kg — an 11 kg cylinder ≈ 150 kWh.
  • For cooking only, an 11 kg cylinder lasts a family roughly 6–10 weeks. Four cylinders ≈ 6–9 months of cooking. This is a very achievable store.
  • For heating, gas goes fast: a 4 kW heater burns an 11 kg cylinder in ~37 hours of running. Gas is a cooking fuel and a short-term heat fuel, not a winter heating strategy.
  • Store cylinders outside or in a ventilated outbuilding, upright, away from drains and cellars (LPG is heavier than air and pools). Many leases and building rules limit or forbid cylinders in flats — check, and if forbidden, use small butane canisters and an alcohol stove instead.
  • Butane (the blue camping canisters) stops vaporising below about 0 °C — useless outdoors in winter. Propane works to −40 °C. For cold-weather use, buy propane or propane/butane mixes.

5. Cooking without the grid

Rank your cooking options by fuel independence:

MethodFuelIndoor safe?Notes
Wood stove with cooktopWood✅ (properly flued)The champion: heat + cooking + water + drying from one fire
Rocket stoveTwigs, sticks, offcuts❌ Outdoor/covered onlyAstonishingly efficient — boils 1 L on a handful of sticks. €30 to buy, €0 to build from a tin and firebricks. Every land household should have one.
Propane camping/caravan stoveLPG⚠️ With good ventilationFast, controllable, clean. The apartment default.
Butane canister stoveButane⚠️ With ventilationCheap (€20), canisters are compact. Fails in the cold.
Alcohol / meths / TrangiaBioethanol, denatured alcohol⚠️ The safest liquid fuel indoors (burns to CO₂ and water, no soot), still ventilateSlow, quiet, invisible flame — mark the stove. Fuel stores indefinitely.
Thermal cooker / hayboxAlmost none✅Boil 10–15 min, then insulate for 3–6 h. Cuts fuel 50–80% on stews, beans, grains, soups. €70 to buy or free to make (a box, a blanket, a duvet, straw). Buy or build one — it multiplies every other fuel you own.
Pressure cookerAny✅Cuts cooking time and fuel by 50–70%. Beans in 30 min instead of 2 h.
Solar ovenSunlight✅ (outdoors)Free, silent, no smoke, no fuel. 120–150 °C on a sunny day — bakes bread, cooks stews and rice, pasteurises water. Weather-dependent, and useless in a Central European winter. €50 to build from card, foil and glass.
Charcoal / BBQCharcoal❌ NEVER INDOORSCharcoal produces enormous quantities of CO with no smoke to warn you. It kills whole families every year. Outdoors only, always.
Open fireWood❌ OutdoorWasteful, smoky, visible, smellable. Last resort.
Electric induction/hobElectricity✅Only while the grid is up

The fuel-efficient cooking doctrine (this doubles or triples your fuel store):

  1. Soak beans, chickpeas and grains overnight — cuts cooking time by half.
  2. Pressure cook whatever you can.
  3. Bring to the boil hard, then transfer to the thermal cooker for the long simmer. This is the single biggest fuel saving available.
  4. Lids on, always. Uncovered pots waste 2–3× the energy.
  5. Match the pot to the burner; a small pot on a big flame wastes most of it.
  6. Batch cook — one fire, several meals. Cook tomorrow's food with today's fire.
  7. Use the residual heat: bread, then a casserole, then dried herbs, then a hot water bottle, all from one stove firing.
  8. Windshield any outdoor stove — wind can double fuel use.
  9. Eat one-pot food. Stews, porridge, soup, dal. Fewer pots, less washing water, less fuel.

Baking without an oven: a Dutch oven / cast-iron pot with a lid on a wood stove or in embers; a stove-top "bread pot"; a solar oven; a hot-box. Flatbreads, tortillas and pan breads need only a frying pan — learn these, they are the cheapest bread in fuel terms.

6. Carbon monoxide: the thing that will actually kill you

More people die from improvised indoor heating and cooking in a crisis than from almost any other cause. CO is colourless, odourless, tasteless, and the early symptoms — headache, nausea, sleepiness, confusion — are exactly the symptoms that stop you realising you need to act.

The absolute rules:

  • NEVER burn charcoal, a BBQ, a generator, a petrol engine, or a patio heater indoors, in a garage, in a cellar, in a tent, in a porch, or in a caravan. Not with a window open. Not "just for a bit".
  • NEVER use a gas cooker as a room heater.
  • NEVER sleep with a flueless combustion appliance running.
  • Any flame consumes oxygen and produces CO₂, water vapour and some CO. Ventilate — a 2–5 cm window gap is a small heat cost and a large safety gain.
  • Fit CO alarms (EN 50291): one in every sleeping room, one in any room with a combustion appliance, one near the generator's nearest wall. €20 each. Check them monthly, replace every 5–7 years. This is the cheapest life insurance in this entire plan.
  • Fit smoke alarms too, and keep a fire extinguisher (ABC powder, 2 kg) and a fire blanket in the kitchen and by the stove. Crisis cooking on unfamiliar appliances in a crowded warm room is exactly how house fires start, and there may be no fire brigade coming.
  • If anyone gets a headache, feels sick or sleepy when the heater is on and better when outside: get everyone out, ventilate, do not go back in.

7. Hot water

  • On the stove. A kettle or a pot living permanently on the wood stove provides washing water all day for free.
  • Wood stove back boiler — heats a cylinder as a by-product of room heating. Must be correctly installed with a heat leak/dump radiator and pressure relief, or it is a bomb. Use a professional.
  • Solar shower bag (20 L, €15): black PVC bag on a sunny wall reaches 40 °C in a few hours, April–September. The cheapest hot water there is.
  • Solar thermal panel / batch heater: a genuinely good investment in a sunny climate; a DIY batch heater (a black tank in a glazed box) costs €100.
  • Wash with much less: a basin, a flannel and 3 L of hot water gets a person properly clean.

8. Build sequence

🏢 Apartment

  1. Now (€120): 2-burner butane/propane camping stove + 8 canisters · CO alarm · fire blanket · thermal cooker or a DIY haybox · warm bedding and base layers for everyone.
  2. L1 (€250): + 4 more canisters or a small propane cylinder if permitted · alcohol stove + 5 L of fuel (safest indoor option) · window film · heavy curtains · draught excluders · sleeping bags rated to 0 °C.
  3. L2 (€400): + a pressure cooker · more fuel (target 3 months of cooking: ~10 kg of propane or 20 butane canisters) · a room-in-a-room tent for winter (a cheap 4-person tent inside the living room genuinely works — it traps body heat and turns an unheated flat into a survivable one).
  4. Never a generator, charcoal, or a flueless heater overnight. In a flat you cannot make CO safe; you can only avoid making it.

🏡 Land / house

  1. Now (€150): CO + smoke alarms · fire extinguisher + blanket · draught-proofing · thermal cooker · rocket stove.
  2. L1 (€800–2,500): wood stove with cooktop, professionally installed and signed off · 2 m³ of seasoned wood · chimney rods · moisture meter · 2 × 11 kg propane + regulator and hoses · bow saw, axe, maul.
  3. L2 (€1,500–4,000): + loft insulation · 4–6 m³ seasoned wood (this year's and next year's) · chainsaw + full PPE + spares · log store built · solar shower or back boiler · warm-room prepared and tested.
  4. L3: masonry stove or rocket mass heater · two-year rolling wood supply · coppice or woodland access · solar thermal · full-house insulation upgrade.

9. Heating & fuel checklist

[ ] I know exactly what happens to my heating in a blackout
[ ] A non-electric heat source exists for at least one room
[ ] Warm room chosen, prepared, and slept in for one test night in winter
[ ] Draught-proofing, curtains and window film done before autumn
[ ] Loft insulated to 300 mm
[ ] Wood: this winter's supply seasoned AND next winter's stacked
[ ] Moisture meter owned; wood measured under 20%
[ ] Chimney swept this year; own rods in stock
[ ] Cooking: at least TWO fuel-independent methods available
[ ] Thermal cooker / haybox owned and used
[ ] Pressure cooker owned and used
[ ] 3 months of cooking fuel stored (propane preferred)
[ ] Fuel stored legally, outdoors, upright, ventilated
[ ] CO alarms in every sleeping room and near every combustion appliance
[ ] Smoke alarms tested; extinguisher and fire blanket in place
[ ] Everyone in the household knows the "never indoors" rule by heart
[ ] Winter bedding, wool layers, hats and hot water bottles for every person
[ ] Unheated-zone plumbing can be drained before a hard frost
[ ] Cold-night drill completed

🔨 Build it: Cooking & heat builds — rocket stoves · haybox/thermal cooker · solar oven · solar dehydrator · wood store · warm-room tent

05 — Sanitation & Hygiene

Historically, in every siege, refugee camp, flood and collapse, more people die of dysentery, cholera and typhoid than of anything else. This chapter is not optional and it is not glamorous. It is the one that keeps your family alive while everyone else's plan is about food.

1. The problem, stated plainly

Each person produces, every day:

  • ~1.2–1.5 litres of urine (sterile when fresh, smelly and ammoniacal within hours)
  • ~150–250 g of faeces (containing, if that person is ill, billions of infectious organisms)
  • Grey water from washing
  • Household waste

A family of four produces roughly 6 L of urine and 800 g of faeces per day, every day, for as long as the crisis lasts. Over three months that is 540 L of urine and 72 kg of faeces. You must have a plan for it before day one.

And the failure mode is fast: one person with a gastrointestinal infection, one unwashed pair of hands, one fly walking from the bucket to the bread, and the whole household is ill. Diarrhoea while isolated, with a saturated health system, in an unheated house, with limited water, is genuinely life-threatening — especially for children and the elderly.

The controls, in order of importance:

1. Keep hands clean          (the single biggest intervention)
2. Keep faeces away from food, water and flies
3. Keep clean water clean    (see Ch. 01)
4. Keep sick people separate (see Ch. 06)
5. Wash and dry things

2. Handwashing — the highest-value habit in a crisis

Handwashing with soap cuts diarrhoeal disease by 30–50% and respiratory infection meaningfully too. It is more effective than any item you can buy.

When: after the toilet, after handling waste or animals, after being outside or handling deliveries, before preparing food, before eating, before and after caring for anyone ill, after coughing or sneezing.

How, with almost no water:

  • Tippy-tap: a 5 L jug or bottle on a string, pivoted by a foot lever, pouring a thin stream. Costs nothing, uses 0.2 L per wash, and — crucially — you never touch the container with dirty hands. Build one, indoors or out.
  • Tap-fitted jerry can (a 20 L can with a spigot on a shelf) — same effect.
  • Wet, lather 20 seconds, scrub between fingers and under nails, rinse into a greywater bucket. Air-dry or use a dedicated personal towel.
  • Soap matters more than water volume. Even cold, muddy water with soap beats clean water without.
  • Alcohol hand rub (≥ 60%) is a supplement, not a substitute — it does not work well on visibly dirty hands and is poor against norovirus. Have both.

Stock soap generously. It is cheap, light, stores for years (bar soap improves with age as it hardens), and is an outstanding barter good. Target 1 bar per person per month, plus 5 L of liquid soap and 5 L of washing-up liquid, per year. Learn to make soap from lye + rendered fat as an L3 skill.

3. Toilets without mains water

First: can you still flush?

If water supply fails but the sewer/septic still works, you can flush with any water — greywater, rainwater, pool water, stream water.

  • Pour 4–6 L briskly straight into the bowl (not the cistern) to trigger the siphon. Use the cistern only if you have water to spare.
  • Practise "if it's yellow, let it mellow" — flush solids only.
  • Warning: if you live in a block of flats and the sewer or the building's pumping station backs up, flushing pushes sewage into the lowest flats. If there is any sign of backup, or if the mains sewer depends on electric pumping stations in your area, stop flushing immediately and go to the bucket system.

The bucket (sawdust / "humanure") toilet — the household standard

Reliable, cheap, odour-free when done properly, and usable indoors. This is the answer for both tracks.

Build it:

  • Two or more 20 L buckets (one in use, one being emptied/held).
  • A toilet seat with a lid on a simple plywood box, or a purpose-made bucket-toilet seat (€25).
  • A bin of cover material and a scoop.

Use it:

  1. Put 3–5 cm of cover material in the bottom of an empty bucket.
  2. Use the toilet as normal (urine and faeces together is fine, but see separation below).
  3. Cover completely after every single use — a generous handful of material until nothing is visible. This is the whole trick: a covered deposit does not smell, does not attract flies, and composts aerobically.
  4. Keep the lid down between uses.
  5. When two-thirds full, seal and swap to the next bucket.

Cover material — stock this deliberately, you need a lot of it: sawdust (not from treated or painted wood) · wood shavings · shredded dry leaves · coconut coir (compressed bricks — compact to store, ideal for flats) · shredded paper and cardboard · peat-free compost · chopped straw · dry garden soil · rice hulls. Plan ~1–2 L of cover material per person per day → roughly 150–250 L per person per three months. Coir bricks are the apartment answer: one 5 kg brick expands to ~70 L and stores on a shelf.

Urine separation transforms the system. Urine is ~90% of the volume and almost all of the smell when mixed. A urine-diverting seat (€40–80), or simply a separate bottle/jug for urine, means:

  • The solids bucket fills 5× more slowly and barely smells.
  • Much less cover material is needed.
  • The urine is a superb, sterile nitrogen fertiliser (see below).

Men and children can use a dedicated bottle; a urine-diverting insert handles everyone.

Where does it go?

🏡 Land track — compost it.

  • Build two bays of 1 m³ each from pallets, on soil, sited away from any water source and downhill from wells.
  • Start with a 30 cm base of straw or wood chips to absorb liquid.
  • Empty the bucket into the centre of the pile, then cover immediately and completely with straw, leaves or chips. A properly covered humanure pile has no odour and no flies. Never leave deposited material uncovered.
  • Fill one bay for a year, then leave it sealed and undisturbed for a second full year while you fill the other. Two years total is the conservative standard used by experienced practitioners.
  • A thermophilic pile reaching 50–60 °C kills pathogens in days, but piles built in cold weather from small daily additions often don't get there — which is why the long rest period is the real safety margin, not the temperature.
  • Use the finished compost on fruit trees, berries, ornamentals and grain/fodder crops. Do not use it on root vegetables, salads, or anything eaten raw. Be conservative; this is one place where caution costs nothing.
  • Wash hands, and keep a dedicated pair of gloves and a boot brush for the job.

🏡 Land track — the alternative: trench latrine. Faster and simpler if you have space and are not staying forever.

  • At least 30 m from any well, spring or watercourse, and downhill from it.
  • 30 cm wide, 1 m deep, as long as needed. Throw a scoop of soil in after each use; backfill and mound over when 30 cm from the top, and mark it.
  • Not for winter (frozen ground) and not for high water tables.

🏢 Apartment track — the hard problem. This is the single toughest apartment challenge and it must be solved in advance. Options, in order of preference:

  1. Sewer still works → flush with greywater. Easy. Most likely scenario.
  2. Building agreement: a sealed 120–200 L drum in a bin store, courtyard or basement, emptied by the council or a contracted service when collections resume; or a communal composting point in a courtyard garden. Agree this with neighbours and the building committee in GREEN, not in RED. Several flats cooperating can manage this; one flat alone cannot.
  3. Double-bagging: heavy-duty bin liners inside the bucket, tied off, stored in a sealed outer drum on a balcony until collections resume. Add cover material and a scoop of garden lime or wood ash to control odour. Realistic for weeks, unpleasant for months, and it depends on collections eventually returning.
  4. Urine: dilute 1:8 with water and pour down a still-working drain or onto balcony plants/trees below (ask first). Undiluted urine burns plants.
  5. Never dump waste in a stairwell, lift shaft, rubbish chute or watercourse. That is how a building becomes uninhabitable and how an outbreak starts.

Urine as fertiliser (both tracks): fresh urine from a healthy person is sterile and is an excellent nitrogen-phosphorus-potassium fertiliser. Dilute 1:8 to 1:10 with water and apply to soil around growing plants (not on leaves, not on anything about to be eaten raw, and stop 3–4 weeks before harvest). One adult's annual urine contains roughly enough nitrogen for 200–300 m² of vegetable garden — a genuinely significant free input when fertiliser is unavailable. Don't use urine from anyone on medication you'd rather not concentrate in your soil.

4. Washing and laundry

Body washing — see Ch. 01 §6. A basin, a flannel, 3 L of warm water and soap gets a person genuinely clean. A weekly proper wash plus daily face/hands/armpits/ groin/feet is the standard that prevents skin infections and keeps morale up. Do not let washing lapse. Feeling filthy is corrosive to morale in a way that compounds everything else, and skin infections in a crisis are dangerous.

Laundry without a machine:

  • Two-bucket method: wash bucket with hot soapy water, rinse bucket with cool clean water. A plunger-style washer (a "breathing mobile washer", €25) or a clean toilet plunger with holes drilled in it makes agitation far less exhausting.
  • Or a bucket + lid + plunger hole as a manual "washing machine"; or a hand-cranked camping washer (€60–100).
  • A mop wringer or a hand mangle removes far more water than wringing by hand, and drying is the bottleneck in winter.
  • Budget ~30 L per load; reuse the rinse water as the next wash water.
  • Dry indoors in the warm room with ventilation, or outside. Wet laundry in a sealed room causes condensation, mould and respiratory problems — always vent.
  • Wash underwear, socks and anything soiled frequently; outer layers rarely. Air clothes instead of washing them. Wool needs washing far less often than cotton and stays warm when damp — build the household's clothing around it.
  • Stock: washing soda, bar soap or laundry soap, a scrubbing brush, a washboard (€15, genuinely useful), spare pegs and line, and enough clothing that laundry can lag by a week.

Dishes: scrape first, two-basin wash + rinse, air-dry (drying cloths spread bacteria). Add a splash of bleach to the rinse water if anyone is ill.

5. The supplies people forget

ItemQuantity for 4 people, 12 monthsNotes
Toilet paper~150–200 rollsBulky. Or reduce with a bidet bottle (a €3 squeeze bottle or a garden sprayer) — uses 0.5 L and cuts paper use by 80%. Family cloth (dedicated washable cloths, laundered hot) is the zero-consumable option.
Soap50 bars + 5 L liquidAlso a top-tier barter good
Washing-up liquid / laundry soap10 L / 10 kg
Menstrual suppliesCritical, and constantly forgotten.Menstrual cups (€25 each, last 5–10 years) and reusable cloth pads are the strategic answer — they remove the problem permanently. Also keep 12 months of disposables as a buffer and for others.
Nappies/diapers12 monthsReusable cloth nappies + liners + a soaking bucket. Disposables for a year are ~2,000 units and simply won't fit in a flat.
Toothbrushes & toothpaste8 brushes, 12 tubesDental problems in a crisis are agony with no dentist. Bicarbonate of soda + salt works as toothpaste; store a few kg. Floss (also useful as strong thread).
Bin liners, heavy duty200+Waste, sanitation, waterproofing
Bleach / disinfectant10 LCleaning and water treatment. Rotate annually.
Gloves (nitrile)500Sanitation, medical, cleaning
Paper towel / ragsPlenty of old cotton cut into ragsWashable and reusable
Hair clippers (manual)1Hygiene and morale
Nail clippers, razors, combPer personIngrown nails and untreated cuts get infected
Insect screens / meshFor windows and the compostFly control is disease control

6. Waste, flies and rodents

Waste reduction comes first — with no collections, whatever you bring in you keep. Buy in bulk with less packaging, compost all food waste, burn clean paper and card (land track), and separate what will eventually be collected.

Organic waste → compost heap (land) or a small bokashi bucket / wormery (apartment — a wormery under a sink handles a family's vegetable waste silently and produces fertiliser for the balcony).

Non-compostable waste → sealed bins, kept outside the living space, well away from food and water. Rinse anything food-contaminated before storing it.

Flies are the vector that connects faeces to food. Control them:

  • Cover every deposit of waste, always.
  • Screens on windows and food covers on the table.
  • Fly traps and papers; keep a stock.
  • Keep the compost properly covered with a carbon layer.

Rodents eat your food store, chew wiring, and carry leptospirosis, hantavirus and salmonella. Control them:

  • Food in metal or thick plastic bins, never in sacks on the floor.
  • Snap traps (stock 12+) and keep the area clear of nesting material.
  • Block entry points with steel wool and mortar.
  • A cat earns its food.
  • Use poison very cautiously if you have pets, children or eat the local wildlife.

Medical/infectious waste (masks, tissues, dressings from a sick room) → double-bagged, sealed, separate, ideally burned (land) or held apart (flat). See Ch. 06.

7. Disinfection — what concentration for what

Household bleach (5–6% sodium hypochlorite), diluted fresh (chlorine solutions lose strength within ~24 hours):

UseDilutionContact time
Drinking water2 drops per litre30 min
Surfaces, general cleaning1:100 (10 mL per litre)1 min
Body fluids, vomit, faeces, sick-room surfaces1:10 (100 mL per litre)10 min
Soaking dishes/cloths from a sick person1:10010 min
Boots/footbath at a threshold1:50—
  • Never mix bleach with ammonia, acids, or toilet cleaners — it releases chlorine or chloramine gas and it will hurt you badly in a small room.
  • Ventilate while using it.
  • Alternatives: 70% alcohol (surfaces, fast, flammable), hydrogen peroxide 3%, boiling water (dishes, cloths), soap and mechanical scrubbing (always the first step — disinfectant on a dirty surface does little).
  • Wood ash and lime are the zero-supply-chain options: wood ash is alkaline and mildly disinfectant, useful thrown on latrine contents; garden lime (calcium hydroxide) controls odour and pathogens in pits and drums.

8. Build sequence

🏢 Apartment

  1. Now (€60): 2 × 20 L buckets + a bucket-toilet seat · 2 coir bricks · a bidet bottle · 5 kg of soap · 200 bin liners · nitrile gloves · a tippy-tap or a jerry can with a spigot.
  2. L1 (€150): + urine-diverting insert or dedicated urine bottles · 4 more coir bricks · menstrual cups · plunger washer + 2 buckets · fly screens · 5 L bleach · washing soda.
  3. L2 (€250): + 3 months of cover material (8–10 coir bricks) · a sealed 120 L outer drum for the balcony · lime or wood ash · reusable nappies if relevant · a written agreement with the building/neighbours about where waste goes.

🏡 Land / house

  1. Now (€80): as apartment L0, plus a spade and a designated latrine site.
  2. L1 (€200): + two pallet compost bays built · a sawdust/shavings supply (a trailer-load from a sawmill is often free) · straw bales · washboard and mangle · outdoor handwashing station.
  3. L2 (€400): + a dedicated outhouse or a purpose-built composting toilet · greywater line to the orchard · a year of cover material under cover · solar shower · rodent-proof metal bins for the food store.
  4. L3: an established two-bay humanure rotation in its second year · soap-making from lye and tallow · a wormery/bokashi system for kitchen waste · a reed bed or mulch basin for greywater.

9. Sanitation checklist

06 — Health & Pandemic Protocol

This is the chapter that is specifically about a pandemic rather than about self-sufficiency in general. It has two halves: keeping the disease out, and staying healthy when the health system cannot help you.

Not medical advice. Everything here is for planning and preparation. Build your medication supply with your doctor and pharmacist, never by skipping doses or obtaining prescription drugs outside the system. Get vaccinated. Follow official public health guidance — it is written by people with far more information than you have about the specific pathogen. If someone is seriously ill, seek medical care. A saturated hospital is still better than no hospital.

1. The biggest medical risk is not the virus

For most households, the greatest danger in a pandemic is losing routine healthcare: the insulin you can't collect, the blood pressure medicine that runs out, the appendix that bursts while ambulances are queued, the infected cut, the dental abscess, the birth with no midwife.

Therefore the priority order is:

1. Prescription continuity   — start this TODAY, it takes months to build legally
2. Not catching it           — PPE, isolation, ventilation, decontamination
3. Treating what you can     — the medical chest and the skills
4. Knowing when to break isolation and get real help

2. Prescription medicines — the single most important preparation

For anyone in your household on regular medication, this is the top priority in this entire plan. More important than food, water or power, because the timeline is shorter: an insulin-dependent person without insulin has days.

How to build a buffer legally and safely:

  • Talk to your GP now, in GREEN, and explain that you want a modest buffer for supply disruption. Many will support a 90-day prescription instead of 28 days, especially post-2020. Ask about an emergency/holiday supply.
  • Fill repeats as early as the system allows, every time. A 7-day early refill each month builds a full extra month over the course of a year, entirely within the rules.
  • Never skip or halve doses to build a stockpile. That does more harm than the shortage you are insuring against.
  • Rotate: use the oldest first so the buffer is always fresh.
  • Get a written copy of every prescription — drug name (generic, not brand), dose, frequency, and the condition treated. If you ever need care elsewhere, from anyone, this piece of paper is priceless.
  • Storage: cool, dark, dry. Most medicines degrade fastest in heat — the bathroom cabinet is the worst place in the house. Many solid-dose drugs remain potent for years past their date (the US SLEP programme found ~88% of tested drugs still met specification 5+ years past expiry) — but liquids, antibiotics in suspension, insulin, nitroglycerine, EpiPens, eye drops and anything refrigerated are genuine exceptions and lose potency or become unsafe. Never gamble on a life-critical drug being still good.
  • Insulin specifically: unopened vials need 2–8 °C. Plan the cold chain — a solar-powered fridge or 12 V cooler on the critical-load circuit (Ch. 02), a cool cellar, or a clay-pot/evaporative cooler as a last resort. An in-use vial keeps ~28 days at room temperature. This is a Tier 1 electrical load.
  • Oxygen concentrators, CPAP, nebulisers, powered wheelchairs: each is a Tier 1 load. Size the power system for them explicitly, and have a manual fallback where one exists.

Also stock: spare glasses (get an extra pair made and keep a copy of your prescription), contact lens solution, hearing aid batteries, mobility aid spares, and anything else whose absence disables someone.

3. The household medical chest

Build this over several months. Quantities are for a family of four for approximately 6–12 months.

Life-saving basics

ItemQtyWhy
Oral rehydration salts (ORS)40+ sachetsThe most life-saving item on this list. Diarrhoea and fever kill by dehydration. Home recipe: 1 L clean water + 6 tsp sugar + ½ tsp salt.
Digital thermometer (+ spare)2Fever monitoring
Pulse oximeter2Detects the "silent hypoxia" of respiratory illness. €20. Genuinely useful for deciding when someone needs hospital care.
Blood pressure monitor1For anyone with cardiovascular risk
Blood glucose meter + stripsif diabeticStrips expire — rotate
First aid manual (printed)1Paper works in the dark

Pain, fever and everyday medicine

Paracetamol/acetaminophen (adult + children's liquid) · ibuprofen (adult + children's) · aspirin (also for suspected heart attack — know the protocol) · antihistamine, non-drowsy and drowsy · loperamide (use with caution — not for bloody or feverish diarrhoea; rehydration is the treatment) · oral rehydration salts · antacids/omeprazole · laxative and stool softener · anti-emetic · cough suppressant and expectorant · throat lozenges · decongestant · hydrocortisone 1% cream · antifungal cream (athlete's foot, thrush) · antiseptic cream · burn gel · oral gel (toothache) · saline nasal spray · eye wash / saline pods · electrolyte drinks · vitamin C, vitamin D, multivitamin, zinc.

Wound care

Nitrile gloves (500) · sterile saline (or make it: 9 g salt per litre of boiled water) · povidone-iodine and/or chlorhexidine · sterile gauze in several sizes · non-adherent dressings · adhesive dressings, assorted · wound closure strips (steri-strips) and skin glue · micropore and cloth tape · crepe and elastic bandages · a trauma dressing and a CAT tourniquet (know how to use it) · triangular bandages · burn dressings · blister plasters · tweezers, scissors, a scalpel or sterile blades · a splint (SAM splint) · a thermometer cover · instant cold packs · safety pins · a proper first aid bag that is findable in the dark.

Pandemic-specific PPE

ItemQtyNotes
FFP2/FFP3 (N95/N99) respirators100+The workhorse. Fit matters more than rating — a leaking FFP3 is worse than a well-fitted FFP2. Try several shapes now and find one that seals on each adult's face. Beards break the seal.
Surgical masks200For a sick person to wear (source control), and for lower-risk tasks
Nitrile gloves500
Safety goggles or a face shield1 per adultEyes are a transmission route
Disposable aprons / gowns50Or a dedicated washable "care coat"
Hand sanitiser (≥ 60% alcohol)3 L
Disinfectant (bleach)10 LSee the dilution table in Ch. 05 §7
CO₂ meter1 (€80)The best cheap proxy for ventilation. Below 800 ppm = well ventilated. Above 1,500 ppm = the air you are breathing has been in other people's lungs. Invaluable for deciding when a room is safe.
HEPA air cleaner or a DIY Corsi–Rosenthal box1–2A box fan + 4 MERV-13/F7 filters taped into a cube costs ~€60 and outperforms many commercial units. Runs on ~50 W. Genuinely effective at reducing airborne virus indoors.

Skills and references (worth more than the kit)

  • Do a proper first aid course now. Ideally a 2-day one. Then a refresher.
  • Print and keep on paper: Where There Is No Doctor and Where There Is No Dentist (both freely available from Hesperian) — they are written precisely for "care when there is no professional available".
  • Learn: recovery position · CPR · choking · bleeding control and tourniquet use · splinting · recognising dehydration, sepsis and hypoxia · fever management in children · wound cleaning and closure · when NOT to close a wound.

Dental — the badly neglected one

Toothache with no dentist is one of the most miserable and morale-destroying things that can happen in a long crisis, and a spreading dental infection can be dangerous.

  • Get all outstanding dental work done in GREEN. This is free preparation.
  • Stock: temporary filling material (Cavit/zinc oxide, €10), clove oil (eugenol, a genuine topical anaesthetic), dental wax, floss, extra toothbrushes, bicarbonate of soda, a dental mirror and probe, and chlorhexidine mouthwash.
  • Brush and floss religiously — in a high-sugar storage diet with no dentist, oral hygiene is preventive medicine.

Antibiotics — a realistic note

Do not obtain or self-prescribe antibiotics. Misuse causes resistance, masks serious illness, and the wrong drug for the wrong infection is useless or harmful. What you can do: talk to your doctor about whether a standby course is appropriate for anyone with a specific recurrent condition (some people with COPD, recurrent UTIs, or who travel are legitimately prescribed rescue packs); make sure any existing rescue medication (asthma inhalers, steroid packs, EpiPens) is in date and duplicated; and focus your effort on the prevention side — wound hygiene, clean water, and handwashing — which is where a household can genuinely reduce its need for antibiotics.

4. Keeping the disease out — the household protocol

The layers

No single measure works. Stack them, and accept that each is imperfect:

Distance → Ventilation → Filtration → Masks → Hygiene → Quarantine → Isolation

Reduce contacts to the minimum

The most effective control is the number of people whose air you share. At ORANGE and RED: no visitors indoors, one designated shopper (if any), deliveries left at the door, outdoor meetings only.

Ventilation is the biggest lever for an airborne pathogen

  • Open windows. Cross-ventilate for 5–10 minutes every hour rather than leaving a window cracked all day (less heat loss, better air exchange).
  • Use the CO₂ meter to make it concrete: ventilate until it drops below 800 ppm. It turns an invisible risk into a number everyone in the household can see and act on.
  • Run a HEPA cleaner or a Corsi–Rosenthal box in the room the household shares, and in any sick room.
  • Ventilation fights heating. In winter, ventilate hard and briefly, wear layers, and accept the fuel cost — this is a trade you should consciously make in favour of air.

The decontamination threshold (set this up at ORANGE)

Create an airlock zone just inside your entrance — a boot tray, a chair, hooks, a bin, a bucket of 1:100 bleach, and hand sanitiser.

Entering the house:

  1. Shoes off in the tray, outside the line.
  2. Outer layer off and hung in the airlock (or into a laundry bin).
  3. Mask off last, by the straps, into a bin or a dedicated paper bag.
  4. Hands washed with soap for 20 seconds before touching anything else.
  5. Shower and change if you have been somewhere high-risk.

Incoming goods: the realistic evidence is that surface transmission is a minor route for respiratory viruses compared with air — so do not exhaust yourself disinfecting groceries. A proportionate protocol: leave non-urgent deliveries in a holding area (garage, porch, balcony) for 24 hours, unpack outdoors or in the airlock, discard outer packaging, and wash your hands. That is the step that matters. Wipe down only high-touch items if you wish.

People coming in (a returning family member, a helper): if the pathogen's incubation allows, quarantine in a separate room for the recommended period with daily symptom and temperature checks, masks when sharing space, and a separate bathroom or a disinfect-after-use rule.

5. If someone in the household gets ill

Set up the sick room in advance (do it at ORANGE, not when someone is coughing)

  • Choose a room with a window that opens and, ideally, its own bathroom or the bathroom nearest to it.
  • Put in: a bed, a chair, a bin with a liner, a jug of water and a cup, a basin, a thermometer, a pulse oximeter, tissues, ORS, paracetamol, a phone or a bell, and an air cleaner if you have one.
  • Tape a symptom and observation chart to the door.
  • Everything that goes in stays in until it is cleaned.

Care protocol

  • One designated carer, ideally the youngest healthy adult at lowest risk, and not anyone in a high-risk group. Minimise the number of people exposed.
  • Carer wears: FFP2/FFP3 (fitted), eye protection, gloves, apron. On leaving: gloves off, apron off, eye protection cleaned, mask off last, hands washed.
  • The sick person wears a surgical mask whenever the carer is in the room, if they can tolerate it.
  • Window open, air cleaner running, door closed.
  • Separate crockery, washed hot with a little bleach in the rinse. Separate towels.
  • Laundry: handle minimally, don't shake it, wash hot (60 °C if possible).
  • Bin: double-bagged, tied, held 72 h before joining the household waste.
  • Everything the sick person touches stays in the room.

Monitor and record — twice a day, written down

Time · Temperature · Pulse · Breaths per minute · SpO₂ (oximeter) ·
Fluids in (mL) · Urine out (rough) · Symptoms · Medication given

A written chart lets you see a trend instead of guessing, and it is exactly what a medical professional will ask for if you do get through to one.

Supportive care that actually helps

Fluids, fluids, fluids (ORS if there is diarrhoea, vomiting or high fever) · rest · paracetamol/ibuprofen for fever and discomfort (follow the dosing on the packet; watch total daily paracetamol carefully — overdose is easy and is serious) · keeping warm but not overheated · sitting up or prone positioning if breathing is difficult · food when they want it, calories over nutrition.

🚨 Seek professional help — break isolation if you must — for:

  • SpO₂ below 92% (or a sustained drop from their baseline), or severe breathlessness, or breathing so fast they cannot complete a sentence
  • Blue lips or face · chest pain or pressure · coughing blood
  • Confusion, unable to be roused, unresponsive
  • Signs of severe dehydration: no urine for 8–12 hours, sunken eyes, no tears (children), dizziness on standing, skin that stays tented when pinched
  • Fever with a non-blanching rash (glass test), a stiff neck, or a severe headache with light sensitivity — possible meningitis/sepsis
  • Any fever in an infant under 3 months
  • Seizures · severe abdominal pain (especially lower right — appendicitis) · uncontrollable bleeding · a suspected fracture or head injury
  • Any symptom that frightens you and is getting worse

An overwhelmed hospital is still vastly better than a kitchen table. The plan is not "never get medical care" — it is "reduce the number of times you need it, and know exactly when you do."

6. Staying healthy for a year indoors

The chronic risks of a long confinement are as real as the acute ones.

  • Move every day. Muscle and cardiovascular loss is fast and it is a mortality risk. Bodyweight exercise, a skipping rope (€5), stairs, garden work, a 20-minute daily routine that everyone does. Physical work in a garden is both food and fitness.
  • Get daylight. 20–30 minutes outdoors, even overcast, for circadian rhythm, mood and vitamin D. A balcony or a doorstep counts. Supplement vitamin D in winter regardless.
  • Sleep on a schedule. Confinement destroys sleep timing, and poor sleep degrades immunity, judgement and mood.
  • Eat enough, and eat vegetables. See Ch. 03 §5 — the nutrition trap is real and its symptoms (fatigue, poor healing, low mood, frequent infection) will be blamed on stress when the cause is diet.
  • Alcohol discipline. Consumption reliably rises under confinement and stress, and it damages sleep, immunity, judgement, and relationships. Decide the household rule now, while it is an abstract question.
  • Protect your eyes, back and hands. Unfamiliar manual work causes injuries; gloves, good tools and taking it slowly prevent them. An injury when there is no help is a category-changing event.

Mental health — plan for it as seriously as for calories

Months of fear, confinement, boredom, grief and enforced proximity break people and break families. This is not a soft topic; it is the reason plans fail.

  • Structure is the medicine. A daily routine with fixed times for waking, meals, work, exercise, chores and a shared evening is what holds a household together. See Ch. 12.
  • Everyone gets a job. Purpose and competence are protective. Children especially need real responsibilities, not just entertainment.
  • Everyone gets privacy, even if it is only an hour and a corner with headphones. Enforce it.
  • Ration the news. Once or twice a day, from a fixed source, for a fixed time. Continuous doom-scrolling is corrosive and changes nothing.
  • Stay connected. Scheduled calls, radio check-ins with the neighbours, letters. Isolation is the risk factor, not the virus.
  • Plan entertainment deliberately: books (paper), board games, cards, instruments, craft supplies, seeds, puzzles, projects. A crate of these costs €100 and is worth more than another sack of rice.
  • Mark time. Birthdays, festivals, a special meal every week. When days are identical, time becomes formless and morale collapses. Deliberate celebration is a survival technique.
  • Watch for: withdrawal, not eating, not sleeping, drinking, irritability escalating to violence, hopelessness. Talk about it early and explicitly, agree now that raising it is allowed, and know your local crisis line numbers — write them on paper.
  • Children: honest, age-appropriate information beats overheard fragments. Keep schooling going in some form. Keep play sacred.

7. Build sequence

LevelDo this
Now, €0Book a first aid course · book overdue dental and medical check-ups · order a spare pair of glasses · ask your GP about 90-day prescriptions · print the medication list for every household member · download and print the Hesperian books

L1, €350Full medical chest as listed above · pulse oximeter · 50 FFP2 · gloves · 1-month prescription buffer · first aid manual on paper
L2, €7003-month prescription buffer · 100+ FFP2/FFP3 fit-tested per person · goggles, aprons · CO₂ meter · HEPA/Corsi–Rosenthal box · dental kit · sick room kit boxed and labelled · 12 months of vitamins
