🔨 Build it: Corsi–Rosenthal air cleaner — a €60 box fan and four filters that outperforms most commercial purifiers

Previous: 05 — Sanitation & hygiene · Next: 07 — Land & garden

↑ Contents

07 — Land & Garden 🏡

Growing food is the only preparation that renews itself. It is also slow, skill-dependent, and unforgiving of being started late. Plant the first bed this month, whatever month it is.

1. The honest expectations

Read this before you plan anything.

  1. Year one will disappoint you. Your soil is poor, your timing is wrong, your slugs are numerous, and you don't yet know your site. Expect 25–40% of the yield an experienced gardener gets from the same ground. This is normal and it is not a reason to stop — it is the reason to start now, while failure costs nothing but a season.
  2. Vegetables are not calories. A gorgeous bed of lettuce, tomatoes and courgettes is worth maybe 200–600 kcal/m²/year. It will not feed you. It provides the vitamins, minerals, flavour and morale that make your stored staples survivable. Storage feeds you; the garden keeps you healthy and sane. Design around that division of labour.
  3. The garden is a supplement in year one, a substantial contributor in year three, and a foundation in year five. Perennials and soil take that long.
  4. Full self-sufficiency needs land most people don't have. See the table below. Aim for meaningful contribution, not autarky.
  5. Preservation capacity, not growing capacity, is the real limit. Anyone can grow 60 kg of tomatoes in August. Having them in February requires jars, a canner, a dehydrator and a cellar. Build the preserving side in parallel (Ch. 03 §6).

2. How much land do you actually need?

GoalArea per personWhat it looks like
Fresh vegetables and herbs in season, big morale/vitamin value30 – 50 m²Four raised beds and some pots
Above, plus enough to preserve for winter vegetables80 – 150 m²A serious kitchen garden
Above, plus a meaningful calorie contribution (30–50% of calories)250 – 400 m²Kitchen garden + a potato/squash/bean plot
Most calories, most of the year, still buying oil/grain/salt600 – 1,000 m²A smallholding plot, plus a cellar and a polytunnel
Genuine full food self-sufficiency incl. grain, oil, and animal feed1,500 – 4,000 m² (0.15–0.4 ha)A small farm, with animals, and it is a full-time job

Add for animals (Ch. 08): chickens need 10–20 m² of run for a small flock but far more land than that to feed — a hectare of grain feeds a lot of chickens and 6 hens eat ~300 kg of grain a year. Pasture for a goat: 1,000–2,000 m².

Plan your actual site:

Total plot                 ______ m²
  minus house/paths/shed   ______ m²
  minus too shaded (<5 h summer sun) ______ m²
  minus too wet/steep/rocky          ______ m²
= USABLE GROWING AREA      ______ m²
  of which: annual beds ____   perennial/fruit ____   animals ____   wood/coppice ____

3. Calorie-dense crops — what to actually grow

Ranked by realistic home-garden calories per square metre per year, with storage behaviour, which matters just as much.

Cropkg/m²kcal/m²Stores how longNotes
Potato3 – 52,300 – 3,8005–8 months in a dark cellar at 4–8 °CThe single best survival crop in this climate. Easy, fast, calorie-dense, stores well, and you replant from your own tubers. Watch for blight — grow resistant varieties (Sarpo Mira/Axona are outstanding).
Winter squash / pumpkin3 – 51,200 – 2,0004–8 months at 10–15 °C, no cellar neededEnormous value: it stores at room temperature on a shelf. Also a fine animal feed. Needs space and a long season.
Jerusalem artichoke2 – 41,500 – 2,900In the ground all winter — dig as neededBombproof, perennial, near-zero effort, thrives in poor soil. Two warnings: it is invasive (site it where you don't mind), and inulin causes serious flatulence until the gut adapts.
Maize (grain/flour corn)0.5 – 0.81,800 – 2,900Years, dried on the cobNeeds sun and a long season; dries and stores beautifully; feeds people and poultry. Grow a flour/flint variety, not sweetcorn.
Beetroot / mangel-wurzel4 – 61,700 – 2,5004–6 months in sandMangels are a classic winter animal fodder — a critical link in the livestock chain.
Sweet potato2 – 31,700 – 2,6004–6 months, warm and dryNeeds warmth; increasingly viable in Central Europe with fleece and a warm site.
Carrot / parsnip3 – 51,300 – 2,0004–6 months in damp sand; parsnips overwinter in the groundParsnip is a hungry-gap hero and sweetens after frost
Onion / garlic2 – 4800 – 1,6006–9 months, hanging, dry and coolLow calories, high value: flavour is what makes a storage diet edible. Garlic is also medicinal and near-effortless.
Cabbage (incl. for sauerkraut)4 – 61,000 – 1,5003–5 months whole; 12 months as sauerkrautVitamin C through winter. Grow a lot.
Kale / chard / leaf beet2 – 4400 – 900Standing crop, harvested through winterStands in the ground at −10 °C and feeds you in January. Essential.
Dry beans0.15 – 0.3500 – 1,000YearsLow yield per m² but protein + stores indefinitely + fixes nitrogen + easy to save seed. Grow climbing varieties for yield per m².
Peas (dry)0.15 – 0.25500 – 850YearsAs above, and earlier in the season
Sunflower0.2 – 0.3 seed~1,500 (as oil/seed)Seed: 1 yr; oil: 1 yrThe realistic home oil crop, but pressing needs a press (a hand oil press is €250–600 and low-yielding). Also excellent chicken feed.
Wheat / rye / barley0.3 – 0.51,000 – 1,700Years as whole grainPoor use of a small garden (you'd need ~500 m² per person for bread alone), but worth growing on any spare field area, and rye will grow on poor soil. Threshing by hand is laborious.
Turnip / swede / kohlrabi3 – 5900 – 1,5004–5 monthsFast, cold-hardy, doubles as fodder
Tomato, courgette, cucumber, pepper, salad3 – 10200 – 600Fresh only; preserveLow calories, very high vitamin, flavour and morale value. Grow them — just don't count them as food security.

The allocation rule for a survival garden

50–60%  calorie crops    (potatoes, squash, maize, roots, beans)
20–25%  storage/winter   (cabbage, kale, leeks, onions, garlic, parsnips)
15–20%  fresh/vitamin    (tomatoes, salad, cucumbers, peppers, herbs)
 5%     experiments      (you must keep learning what works on YOUR site)

4. Laying out the garden

Beds

  • 1.2 m wide, any length, with 45–60 cm paths. You can reach the middle from either side, so you never step on the soil. Compacted soil is the most common cause of poor yields.
  • Orient rows north–south where possible so plants don't shade each other.
  • Raised beds (15–25 cm of soil above path level, edged or not) warm earlier in spring, drain better, and are easier on the back. On dry sites, sunken beds conserve water instead.
  • No-dig / no-till: cover the ground with cardboard, then 5–10 cm of compost on top, and plant into it. It suppresses weeds, feeds soil life, halves the labour, and after 2–3 years gives better yields than digging. This is the method to use — especially when you have no fuel, no machinery and limited calories to spend on labour.

Rotation — 4 beds, 4 years

Rotation prevents the build-up of soil-borne disease and balances nutrient demand. Move each group one bed along each year:

Bed 1  LEGUMES      peas, beans           → fix nitrogen, leave it behind
Bed 2  BRASSICAS    cabbage, kale, swede  → use that nitrogen; lime if needed
Bed 3  ROOTS/ALLIUM carrot, parsnip, beet, onion, leek → low nitrogen demand
Bed 4  POTATOES/SOLANACEAE + squash       → heavy feeders, get the compost

Never grow potatoes or tomatoes in the same ground two years running (blight and eelworm). Onions and brassicas both benefit from a 3–4 year gap.

Protected growing — the highest-value structure you can build

A polytunnel or greenhouse changes your calendar by 6–8 weeks at each end and makes winter salad possible.

  • A 3 × 6 m polytunnel costs €400–900 and is the best value; a glasshouse is prettier, more fragile, and more expensive.
  • Cold frames (a glazed lid on a box, €40 or free from old windows) do most of the same job at 1/20th of the cost, for hardening off, winter salad, early sowing and overwintering.
  • Fleece and cloches (€30) buy 2–4 weeks at each end of the season and protect against late frosts — the cheapest season extension there is.
  • Aim for: seedlings in a cold frame from February, tomatoes/peppers/cucumbers in the tunnel from May, and winter salad/spinach/claytonia in the tunnel November–March.

5. The hungry gap — the thing that catches everyone out

March, April and May are the hardest months, not winter. Stores are exhausted, last year's roots are sprouting, and this year's crops are barely seedlings. Historically this is when people starved.

Design for it explicitly:

StrategyWhat to do
Overwintering standing cropsKale, leeks, parsnips, swede, Brussels sprouts, chard, claytonia, corn salad, land cress, sprouting broccoli (purple sprouting broccoli crops in exactly April — plant it)
Long-storing rootsOnions and garlic (into May/June), potatoes (to May if cellared properly), carrots in sand, beetroot
Autumn sowingsBroad beans and garlic sown October–November crop far earlier than spring sowings
The polytunnelWinter salad, spinach, radish, early carrots
PreservesThis is what the jars and sauerkraut are for — the gap, not the autumn glut
Sprouts and microgreens5–14 days to fresh food, indoors, any month. The hungry gap insurance policy (Ch. 09)
PerennialsRhubarb (April), asparagus (April–June), sorrel, Welsh onion, wild garlic, nettle tops, dandelion — most of the earliest food in the year is perennial
Stored caloriesKeep your grain and bean store as the explicit March–May buffer, and ration to it

6. Seed — the true renewable

Seed is what turns one garden into an infinite number of gardens. Without saved seed you are running a countdown timer, not a system.

Buy the right kind

  • Open-pollinated (OP) or heirloom varieties ONLY for anything you intend to save. F1 hybrids do not come true from saved seed — the offspring are a variable mess. F1s are often more vigorous and disease-resistant, so keep a few, but your strategic seed store must be OP.
  • Buy locally-adapted varieties where you can; they are already suited to your climate and diseases.
  • Store a 3–5 year seed bank. Seed is tiny, cheap and light. A comprehensive seed store for a 300 m² garden costs €150–300 and fits in a shoebox. There is no better value per gram in this whole plan.
  • Buy extra of the things you cannot easily save: F1-only varieties you rely on, and biennials.

Seed viability (years, stored cool, dry and dark)

1–2 yrs3 yrs4–6 yrs
Onion, leek, parsnip, salsify, sweetcornCarrot, pea, bean, pepper, spinach, chivesTomato, cabbage/brassicas, beetroot, cucumber, squash, melon, lettuce, radish, turnip

Store seed in paper envelopes inside a sealed glass jar with a silica gel sachet or a spoonful of dry rice, in the fridge or a cold cellar. Cold + dry roughly doubles viability. Label with variety and year. Test germination annually: 10 seeds on a damp paper towel in a bag — count how many sprout in 10–14 days.

Saving seed — start with the easy ones

Easy (self-pollinating, minimal isolation, save from day one): tomato, French/runner bean, pea, lettuce, pepper (mostly), and any self-fertile herb. Save from these in your first year. Take seed from your best plants, not your leftovers — the ones that were healthiest, earliest and tastiest. That is how you breed a strain adapted to your own garden.

  • Tomato: scoop seeds + pulp into a jar of water, ferment 2–3 days until it moults, rinse, dry on a plate (not paper).
  • Beans and peas: leave the pods on the plant until they rattle and are papery.
  • Lettuce: let it bolt and flower, collect the fluffy seed heads.

Harder (cross-pollinating — needs isolation distance or hand-pollination):

  • Squash/pumpkin: cross freely within the same species. Grow only one variety per species, or hand-pollinate and tape the flowers.
  • Maize: wind-pollinated, needs 300 m+ separation or different flowering times. Save from at least 100–200 plants or the strain degrades from inbreeding.
  • Cucumber, melon: 400 m+ or hand-pollinate.

Hardest (biennial — they flower in their second year, so you must overwinter the plant): carrot, beetroot, cabbage and all brassicas, onion, leek, parsnip (parsnip is actually easy — it self-seeds readily), turnip, swede. Brassicas also cross-pollinate wildly with each other and need 800 m+ or cages. Plan to buy these in, or pick just one biennial to master per year.

Also store

Seed potatoes (save your own from healthy, disease-free plants — after a few years buy in certified stock to reset virus load), garlic cloves, onion sets, and root cuttings from your perennials.

7. Fertility without a supply chain

The moment fertiliser deliveries stop, your garden lives on what you can close the loop on. This is the difference between a garden and a mine.

A vegetable garden needs roughly 10–20 g of nitrogen per m² per year, plus potassium, phosphorus, calcium and trace elements, plus organic matter to hold water and feed soil life.

SourceWhat it givesHow to use it
CompostEverything, slowly, plus organic matterThe foundation. Build two or three bays (1 m³ each): fill one, turn into the next, use the third. Mix ~2:1 browns (straw, leaves, card, wood chip) to greens (kitchen waste, weeds, grass, manure). Keep damp and turn it. A hot pile finishes in 3 months; a cold one in 12. You cannot have too much compost.
UrineNitrogen, phosphorus, potassium — a genuinely balanced fertiliserDilute 1:8–1:10, water onto soil around growing plants. One adult's output ≈ enough N for 200–300 m²/year. Free, sterile, and the biggest single fertility input available to most households (Ch. 05).
Humanure compostEverything + organic matterTwo-year composting, then fruit trees and non-food crops only (Ch. 05)
Animal manureN, P, K + organic matterChicken manure is hot — compost it 6+ months or it burns plants. Rabbit manure is cold and can go straight on beds — a real advantage. Horse/cow manure: compost, and beware aminopyralid herbicide residues that destroy tomatoes and beans (test a sample on a bean seedling before spreading a load).
Green manures / cover cropsNitrogen, organic matter, weed suppression, erosion controlSow on any bare bed. Field beans, vetch, clover, lupin fix 50–200 kg N/ha. Phacelia, mustard and rye add bulk and protect soil over winter. Chop down before they seed and leave on the surface or dig in shallowly. Never leave soil bare over winter.
Comfrey (Bocking 14)Potassium, especially — perfect for tomatoes, potatoes and fruitPlant a dozen roots in a corner. Cut 3–4 times a year; use as mulch, in the compost, or as a liquid feed (leaves in a barrel of water for 4 weeks — it smells appalling and works brilliantly). Sterile Bocking 14 doesn't seed and take over.
Wood ashPotassium + calcium, raises pHFrom your wood stove. A light dusting only — it is strongly alkaline. Never on potatoes (encourages scab) or on acid-loving plants. Keep it dry until used.
NettlesNitrogenLiquid feed, same method as comfrey. Free, everywhere.
BiocharNothing itself — a permanent habitat for microbes and a water/nutrient spongeCharcoal from a smothered fire, crushed, charged by soaking in urine or compost tea before use. A genuine long-term soil improvement, and a way to use woody waste.
Leaf mouldOrganic matter, water retentionPile autumn leaves in a wire cage, wait 1–2 years. Free, effortless.
LimeCalcium, raises pHBuy a few sacks now. Brassicas want pH 6.5–7.5 and clubroot is suppressed by lime. It does not perish.
Rock dust, bone meal, seaweedTrace elements, phosphorusWorth stocking a sack of each — they store indefinitely and cover what the loop can't.

Test your soil pH now (€10 kit). Most vegetables want 6.0–7.0. Getting pH wrong locks out nutrients no matter how much fertility you add.

Mulch everything. Straw, leaves, grass clippings, cardboard, compost. Mulch halves your watering, suppresses weeds, feeds the soil and prevents erosion. It is free labour-saving and the single most useful habit in a low-input garden.

8. Water in the garden

  • Mulch first — it saves more water than any irrigation system.
  • Water deeply and infrequently (once or twice a week, thoroughly), not a little every day. Deep water grows deep roots that survive drought.
  • Water at the base, in the morning or evening. Overhead watering in sun wastes most of it and spreads fungal disease.
  • Drip line or soaker hose from a rainwater tank on a stand — gravity gives enough pressure for soaker hose at 1 m of head. €60 for a garden's worth.
  • Ollas (unglazed clay pots buried to the neck, filled weekly) water roots directly with near-zero loss. Ancient, effective, and free if you have old pots.
  • Swales and shaped beds on a slope slow and sink runoff instead of losing it.
  • Prioritise ruthlessly in a drought: water what you cannot replace (fruit trees, perennials, seedlings, and the crops you eat in winter). Let salad die. Squash and potatoes need water at flowering and tuber-set; mature roots and brassicas will survive a lot of neglect.
  • Rainwater, greywater and stored water — never drinking water on the garden. See Ch. 01.

9. Pests and disease without sprays

  • Physical barriers are 90% of the answer. Insect mesh over brassicas (against cabbage white and root fly), fleece over carrots (carrot fly flies low — a 60 cm barrier works), netting over fruit, collars around brassica stems. Buy plenty of mesh and netting now — it lasts years and there is no substitute.
  • Slugs are the number one garden enemy in a wet Central European spring. Beer traps, night-time hand collection (genuinely the most effective method), a duck or two (superb slug hunters — Indian Runners), removing hiding places, copper tape on pots, coarse wool pellets, and starting plants big in modules rather than direct sowing.
  • Blight (potato/tomato) arrives in warm wet July weather. Grow resistant varieties, space for airflow, avoid overhead watering, earth up potatoes, and cut and remove the haulm the moment blight appears — the tubers keep growing and stay clean underground.
  • Rotation and hygiene: 4-year rotation, remove diseased material (burn it, don't compost it), clean tools, don't work among wet plants.
  • Encourage predators: a pond (frogs eat slugs), flowers for hoverflies and lacewings (poached egg plant, phacelia, calendula, umbellifers), hedges and log piles for hedgehogs and beetles, bird boxes. A garden with a healthy predator population has far fewer pest explosions.
  • Companion planting and interplanting: partly folklore, but strong-smelling plants (onions among carrots, marigolds, herbs) genuinely confuse some pests, and mixed plantings limit how fast an outbreak spreads.
  • Larger pests: fence against deer (1.8 m) and rabbits (buried 30 cm, 1 m high); net against birds; trap rodents around the store. Fencing is a capital item — buy and install it before you need it.

10. Perennials — plant them NOW, they take years

This is the most time-sensitive item in the entire plan, because there is no way to shortcut it. A tree planted today fruits in 3–8 years. A tree planted at AMBER fruits never.

PlantFirst cropNotes
StrawberriesYear 1Fastest fruit there is. Multiply free from runners.
Raspberries (summer + autumn varieties)Year 2Very productive, spreads itself, freezes/jams well
Currants (black/red/white), gooseberries, jostaberryYear 2–3Tough, reliable, high in vitamin C, propagate free from hardwood cuttings
RhubarbYear 2The earliest garden food of the year; near-immortal
AsparagusYear 320+ year crop once established
Apples, pears, plums, cherries (on semi-dwarfing rootstock)Year 3–6The backbone of a food-secure property. Choose storing varieties — some apples keep to March in a cellar. Plant several varieties for pollination and to spread the season.
GrapesYear 3Fresh, dried (raisins store for a year), juice, vinegar, wine
Elderberry, rosehip, sea buckthornYear 2–4Huge vitamin C, medicinal, thrive on neglect. Sea buckthorn is one of the richest vitamin C sources that grows here.
HazelnutsYear 4–6Fat and protein that stores — the most valuable nut for this climate, and it coppices for firewood
WalnutsYear 8–15Plant anyway. Fat, protein, stores in the shell for a year, and superb timber.
Herbs (rosemary, thyme, sage, oregano, mint, bay)Year 1Flavour, tea, and medicine, from almost no space
Comfrey, sorrel, Welsh/walking onion, wild garlic, Good King Henry, sea kaleYear 1–2Perennial vegetables — they crop in the hungry gap and need almost no work
Willow / hazel coppiceYear 3–7Renewable firewood, poles, bean sticks, basketry. A coppice on 500 m² gives a sustainable annual cut forever.

Do this in the next planting season, whatever else is unfinished. Bare-root trees and bushes in November–March are cheap (€10–25 each); the whole list above for a garden costs €300–600 and is the highest-compounding investment here.

11. Tools for a garden that has to work

Hand tools first, and buy quality — these should last 30 years: spade · digging fork · rake · hoe (Dutch/push hoe and a draw hoe) · trowel and hand fork · secateurs (+ a spare) · loppers · pruning saw · garden line · wheelbarrow · watering cans (2 × 10 L) · buckets · sharpening stone and a file · a good knife.

Then: seed trays and modules · pots · propagator or a warm windowsill · labels and a pencil (ink fades) · fleece, mesh, netting · canes and string · compost bins · hose and soaker line · a scythe (genuinely practical for grass, green manures and fodder, and needs no fuel — but needs learning and peening) · a broadfork · a garden cart · a soil thermometer · a moisture meter · a notebook.

Spares and consumables: twine, wire, staples, spare mesh, spare fleece, replacement tool handles, tool oil, glass or polythene for the frames/tunnel (polytunnel plastic lasts 5–7 years — buy the replacement sheet now).

The garden notebook is a tool. Record what you sowed, when, which variety, what happened, what the weather did, what you harvested and when. Three years of notes is worth more than any book, because it is about your site.

12. Year one — a realistic first-season plan

Don't try to do everything. Do this:

Autumn/winter (start here whenever you're reading this)

  1. Mark out four beds of 1.2 m × 5 m (24 m² of growing space). That is enough to learn on and not enough to defeat you.
  2. Cardboard + 10 cm of compost on top. No digging.
  3. Order seed (OP varieties), compost, and bare-root fruit.
  4. Plant the fruit trees, bushes, rhubarb and asparagus now — they are on the longest clock.
  5. Build a compost bay. Start filling it.
  6. Plant garlic and broad beans (October–November) for an early crop.

Spring

  1. Sow in modules on a windowsill or in a cold frame: brassicas, leeks, lettuce, tomatoes, squash, and start the potato chitting.
  2. Plant potatoes at the frost-free date (mid-April in most of Central Europe); earth up as they grow.
  3. Direct sow: carrots, beetroot, peas, beans (after last frost), radish.
  4. Mesh over the brassicas and carrots on day one, not after the damage.

Summer

  1. Water deeply, mulch heavily, weed weekly (little and often — an hour a week beats a day a month).
  2. Start saving seed from beans, peas, lettuce and tomatoes.
  3. Preserve as you go — don't wait for a glut you can't process.
  4. Sow the autumn/winter crops in July–August: kale, chard, leeks, spring cabbage, winter salad, purple sprouting broccoli.

Autumn

  1. Harvest, cure and store: potatoes (dry in the dark for 2 weeks first), onions, squash (cure 10 days in sun), roots into sand.
  2. Sow green manure on every empty bed.
  3. Write down what worked and what failed. Plan year two from your own data.
  4. Add two more beds.

13. Garden checklist

[ ] Usable growing area measured, sun-mapped and written down
[ ] Soil pH tested
[ ] First beds built (no-dig) and planted, whatever the season
[ ] 4-year rotation plan drawn on paper
[ ] Calorie crops are 50%+ of the planned area, not just salad
[ ] Open-pollinated seed bank for 3–5 years bought and stored cold and dry
[ ] Seed germination test done this year
[ ] Seed saved from at least beans, peas, tomatoes and lettuce
[ ] Seed potatoes and garlic for next year set aside
[ ] Compost bays built and filling
[ ] Urine diverted and used as fertiliser
[ ] Green manure sown on every bare bed
[ ] Comfrey patch planted
[ ] Lime, rock dust and bone meal stocked
[ ] Mesh, netting and fleece bought — enough to cover everything
[ ] Slug strategy in place before spring
[ ] Blight-resistant potato varieties chosen
[ ] Fruit trees, bushes, rhubarb and asparagus PLANTED (this is the urgent one)
[ ] Nut trees planted
[ ] Perennial vegetables and herbs established
[ ] Rainwater irrigation from tanks, with mulch everywhere
[ ] Cold frame or polytunnel up; spare polythene in store
[ ] Full set of quality hand tools + spares + sharpening kit
[ ] Root cellar or cool store ready before the first harvest
[ ] Preservation equipment ready BEFORE the glut (jars, canner, dehydrator)
[ ] Hungry-gap crops (kale, leeks, parsnips, PSB, overwintered beans) planted
[ ] Garden notebook started

🔨 Build it: Garden builds — no-dig beds · three-bay compost · insect mesh cage · cold frame · low tunnel · walk-in polytunnel · seed-starting rack · liquid feed barrel · leaf mould cage

08 — Animals & Protein 🏡

Animals are the only part of a homestead that can turn things you cannot eat — grass, weeds, kitchen scraps, insects, garden waste — into things you can. That is their entire strategic purpose. Understand it, or you will build a system that consumes more food than it produces.

1. The feed problem — read this before buying a single chicken

Almost every plan for keeping animals in a crisis fails on the same arithmetic.

Six laying hens:

Feed:   6 hens × 130 g/day × 365 days   = 285 kg of grain per year
        285 kg × ~3,400 kcal/kg          ≈ 970,000 kcal IN

Eggs:   ~1,200 eggs/year × 70 kcal       ≈   84,000 kcal OUT
        plus spent hens and cockerels    ≈   30,000 kcal
                                           ------------
        Return on grain fed              ≈ 12%

If you feed your chickens grain you could have eaten, you destroy about 88% of those calories. Six hens eat roughly what one adult eats. That is not a food strategy — that is a luxury paid for in your own rations.

Animals only make sense when they eat what you cannot:

Feed sourceWhat it isWho eats it
Kitchen and garden wastePeelings, trimmings, spoiled produce, wheyChickens, pigs, rabbits (greens only)
Forage and pastureGrass, weeds, insects, worms, seedsChickens (free-range), geese, goats, rabbits
Crop residuesBean haulm, brassica leaves, carrot tops, beet leavesRabbits, goats, chickens
Fodder crops grown deliberatelyMangel-wurzel, fodder beet, turnip, kale, pumpkin, comfrey, lucerne, cloverAll of them — this is the key link, and almost nobody plans it
HayCut and dried grass/cloverRabbits, goats
InsectsBlack soldier fly larvae or mealworms raised on food wasteChickens, quail, fish — 40% protein, essentially free
GleaningsWindfall fruit, spilled grain, spoiled stores, mill sweepingsAll
Milk by-productsWhey, skimmed milk, buttermilkPigs, chickens

The rules that make livestock work:

  1. Store feed for the animals as deliberately as for yourself. If you keep chickens, store 6–12 months of feed (300–600 kg for six hens). Otherwise you will be eating your flock in month four.
  2. Grow fodder. Mangel-wurzel yields 6–10 kg/m² and stores all winter in a clamp. 100 m² of mangels and fodder beet is a winter's feed for a flock and a couple of rabbits. Pumpkins store on a shelf and chickens love them. This single practice is what separates a renewable system from a countdown.
  3. Size the flock to the forage, not to your ambition. In a real shortage, cull down to a breeding core. Six hens and a rooster can rebuild a flock of thirty in one season; thirty starving hens produce nothing.
  4. Maximise free-ranging. A hen with genuine range for insects and greens can get 30–60% of her diet herself in summer, and the eggs are better.
  5. Composting and manure are part of the return. Chicken manure fertilises the garden that feeds you. Count it.

2. Choosing your animals

ChickensRabbitsQuailGoatsPigsBees
Space needed0.4 m²/bird coop + 2–10 m² run0.7 m² per doe + kits0.1 m²/bird1,000+ m² browse20–50 m² + shelter1 m² per hive
NoiseRooster = loudSilentQuietLoud when in seasonLoud at feedingSilent
Output/year180–300 eggs/hen + meat80–120 kg meat (3 does)250–300 tiny eggs/bird500–1,000 L milk70–90 kg carcass + 15 kg fat15–30 kg honey + wax
Feed demandHighLow — forage/hayLow but must be high-proteinVery high (browse)Very highNone
Breeds itself?✅ if broody breed + rooster✅✅ prolifically❌ needs an incubator✅✅✅
Time to first output5–6 months3 months6–8 weeks18 months6–8 months1 year
Skill neededLowLowLowHighMediumMedium–high
Apartment possible?❌⚠️ (a single pet-scale doe)⚠️ Yes, realistically❌❌⚠️ rooftop, with permission
VerdictThe default. Eggs are the best protein return there is.The best meat converter for a small plot. Underrated.Apartment/small-space proteinOnly with real pasture and commitmentBest fat + calorie converter, if you can feed itBest effort-to-value ratio on a homestead

3. Chickens — the household default

Breed choice (this decides whether your flock renews)

  • Do NOT buy commercial hybrid layers (ISA Brown, Lohmann, etc.) as your only stock. They lay superbly for two years and then collapse, and they have had broodiness bred out of them — they will never hatch a chick. A flock of hybrids is a consumable, not a system.
  • Buy dual-purpose heritage breeds that go broody: Orpington, Sussex, Wyandotte, Marans, Australorp, Barnevelder, Plymouth Rock, or a good local landrace. Fewer eggs (180–220/yr), better meat on the cockerels, and — the critical part — a hen that will sit on eggs and raise chicks for you, for free, without electricity.
  • A broody hen is a solar-powered incubator that also raises the chicks. This is the single most important livestock decision in this chapter.
  • Keep a rooster if the law and your neighbours allow. Without one, there are no fertile eggs and no next generation. If a rooster is impossible, keep an incubator + a source of fertile eggs, and accept the power dependency.

Numbers

  • 4–6 hens per 4-person household for eggs. 6 hens ≈ 3–5 eggs/day in season.
  • Add 2–4 more if you want meat as well as eggs.
  • Winter laying drops to near zero in December–January without supplementary light. 14 hours of daylight keeps them laying — a 5 W LED on a timer in the coop (a trivial electrical load) restores winter production. Whether to do this is a real trade-off: it shortens the hen's productive life, but it puts protein on the table in the hardest months.

Housing

  • Coop: 0.4 m²/bird minimum, dry, draught-free but ventilated at the top (damp, not cold, is what kills chickens), with perches (20 cm/bird) and one nest box per 4 hens.
  • Run: 2 m²/bird absolute minimum, 10 m²+ if you want real forage value. A bare, overgrazed run is a mud-and-parasite problem; rotating two runs, or a movable tractor on grass, keeps it healthy.
  • Predator-proof it properly. Fox, marten, rat, hawk, dog. Weld mesh (not chicken wire — a fox goes through chicken wire), buried or skirted 30 cm, a secure roof, and a door that closes every single night. Losing the flock to a fox in month two is the classic, avoidable disaster. An automatic door is fine, but keep a manual latch.
  • Deep litter (wood shavings/straw built up over months) composts in place, keeps the coop warm, and produces excellent garden compost. Clean out twice a year.

Feeding

  • Layer feed: 120–150 g/bird/day. Store 6–12 months (see §1).
  • Grit (insoluble, for the gizzard) and oyster shell/crushed eggshell (calcium, for shells) — both essential, both store indefinitely, both cheap. Stock several years' worth; a hen without calcium lays soft-shelled eggs and then stops.
  • Supplement with: kitchen scraps, garden waste, comfrey, cut grass, windfall fruit, spent brewing grain, mangels and pumpkins in winter, and insect larvae.
  • Fodder sprouting (barley sprouted in trays for 7 days, 1 kg of grain → 6–8 kg of green fodder) — be clear about what this does: it does not create calories (dry matter actually falls slightly), but it converts stored dry grain into fresh green feed with vitamins in winter, which is exactly what a confined flock lacks. Useful, not magic.
  • Black soldier fly larvae: a simple bucket bioreactor turns kitchen waste into 40%-protein larvae that self-harvest by crawling up a ramp. One of the best closed-loop tricks available.
  • Water: 0.25–0.5 L/bird/day, and it must not freeze. A frozen drinker is the most common winter chicken killer. Keep a spare and rotate it from the house.

The renewal cycle — how a flock becomes permanent

March–June   Broody hen sits on 8–12 fertile eggs → 21 days → chicks
             (or set eggs under her from your best layers)
Weeks 0–8    Hen raises chicks; ~50% will be cockerels
Weeks 16–20  Cockerels reach 1.5–2.5 kg → slaughter all but one or two
Month 5–6    Pullets start laying
Autumn       Cull the oldest third of the hens (years 3+, laying declines) — into
             the pot, the stockpot, or the canner
Result       Flock size stable, age profile young, genetics refreshed,
             meat on the table twice a year, and NO purchases required

Replace about one third of your layers each year. Hens lay best in years 1–2, decline from year 3, and are still perfectly good stewing birds (old hens make the best stock there is — long, slow cooking, or pressure-canned).

Health

Common problems: red mite (check perches weekly — treat with diatomaceous earth and a thorough clean), worms, scaly leg mite, coccidiosis in chicks, respiratory infection from damp housing. Keep the coop dry and ventilated, rotate the ground, quarantine any new bird for 3 weeks, and cull sick birds early rather than letting disease run through the flock. Stock diatomaceous earth, poultry tonic and a sharp knife.

4. Rabbits — the most underrated survival animal

Rabbits deserve far more attention than they get in most plans.

Why: silent (no rooster problem), small footprint, breed prolifically, eat grass, weeds, hay and garden waste rather than grain, and reach eating size in 10–12 weeks. A trio of does and one buck occupies about 4 m² and produces 80–120 kg of meat a year.

Gestation 31 days · 6–8 kits per litter · 4–5 litters/doe/year
3 does × 4 litters × 7 kits × 1.25 kg meat each ≈ 105 kg meat/year
  • Feed: hay (the staple — they need it constantly for gut health), fresh greens, garden waste, carrots and roots, comfrey, dandelion, plantain, bramble leaves. Pellets are convenient but not essential — which is exactly the point. Introduce fresh greens gradually; a sudden change causes fatal gut problems.
  • Housing: hutches off the ground, dry, shaded in summer (rabbits tolerate cold far better than heat — above 28 °C is dangerous), with wire floors over a dropping tray, or colony housing on deep litter. Does need a nest box before kindling.
  • Manure is "cold" — it can go straight onto beds with no composting. It is excellent, and a genuine convenience advantage over poultry.
  • Breeding: take the doe to the buck (never the reverse — she'll defend her territory). Keep records: mating date, kindling date, litter size, weaning. Rest each doe properly; don't breed her to death.
  • ⚠️ Rabbit is extremely lean (~1,300 kcal/kg, almost no fat). A diet of rabbit alone causes protein poisoning ("rabbit starvation") — fatal within weeks. Always eat rabbit with fat and carbohydrate: lard, oil, potatoes, grain. This is a real historical cause of death and it must be designed around.
  • Slaughter is quick and requires no equipment beyond a sharp knife and a hook — the easiest livestock for a beginner to process humanely. Learn it properly from someone experienced before you need to.

5. Quail — the small-space option

  • Coturnix (Japanese) quail lay at 6–8 weeks old — the fastest protein of any livestock — and produce 250–300 eggs a year each.
  • 5 quail eggs ≈ 1 chicken egg. A dozen birds gives a family a real egg supply.
  • 0.1 m²/bird: 10 birds live comfortably in 1 m², indoors or on a balcony.
  • Quiet (the male's call is soft and far less carrying than a cockerel's).
  • ⚠️ They do not go broody. Domesticated coturnix have lost the instinct — so renewal requires an incubator (a small one is €60 and about 40 W for 17 days, well within a modest solar system, but it is a dependency).
  • Need higher protein feed than chickens (game-bird crumb, ~20–24% protein); insects and sprouted seed help.
  • Meat birds at 8–10 weeks, ~200 g dressed. Small but fast.
  • The realistic apartment livestock. See Ch. 09.

6. Larger animals — only with land and commitment

Goats (dairy): 2 does give 2–4 L of milk a day for 250–300 days a year — milk, cheese, butter, yoghurt, and whey for the pigs and chickens. But: they need real browse or a lot of hay (1–2 kg/day each in winter), fencing that actually holds them (they are escape artists), annual breeding to keep milking, and twice-daily milking every single day without exception. That last point defeats most people. Sheep are easier but give less milk; a house cow gives far more milk and eats far more.

Pigs: the best calorie and fat converter there is, and fat is the hardest thing to produce at home. A pig eats 2–3 kg/day and reaches 100 kg live in 6–8 months, yielding ~70 kg of carcass including 15 kg of lard — which solves the cooking-fat problem that nothing else on a homestead solves. They will eat almost anything: whey, spoiled produce, windfalls, acorns, waste. Two pigs raised over a summer on surplus and waste, slaughtered in autumn, then cured and canned, is the classic peasant economy for good reason. Check the law: feeding household/catering food waste to pigs is illegal in the EU and UK (disease control), so plan feed you can legally use.

Ducks: better foragers than chickens, lay well, eat slugs voraciously, are hardier in wet weather, and don't destroy a garden the way chickens do. They need water to dip their heads in (not necessarily a pond) and they make mud. Muscovies are quiet, broody and excellent mothers. Seriously consider ducks over chickens if your site is wet.

Geese: live almost entirely on grass, guard the yard, and provide a large carcass and a lot of fat — but they are loud and aggressive, which is either a security feature or a neighbour problem.

Bees: 15–30 kg of honey per hive per year, plus wax for candles and salves, plus pollination that lifts fruit yields across the whole plot. Low daily effort once established, but real skill is needed and there is a €300–600 setup cost (hive, suit, smoker, tools, extractor — or share an extractor with your group). Honey never spoils. The best effort-to-value ratio on a homestead — start learning from a local association now, because beekeeping is not a thing you can learn from a book in a crisis.

Fish / aquaponics: high complexity and it fails hard. Fish need constant aeration (a pump = continuous power, and a failure kills everything in hours), stable temperature, and feed that is usually bought in. A low-tech farm pond stocked with carp or tench, fed on kitchen waste and duckweed, is a far more robust proposition than an aquaponics rig — much lower yield per m², but it survives a blackout.

7. Slaughter, butchery and preservation

This is a skill, and it must be learned before you need it. Find a local smallholder, a hunting club, or a course. Doing it badly is cruel, unsafe and wasteful; doing it well is quick, calm and respectful.

  • Learn the humane methods for your species and follow the legal requirements in your country (most permit home slaughter of your own poultry and rabbits for your own household; larger animals usually require a licensed abattoir or a licensed slaughterman — check).
  • Equipment: sharp knives (and a steel and a whetstone — a blunt knife is the main cause of both cruelty and injury), a killing cone for poultry, a gambrel or hooks, a scalding pot (poultry plucking: 60–65 °C for 30–60 s), a plucking table, buckets, and a clean cool space.
  • Use everything: meat, offal (liver and heart are the most nutrient-dense food available to you — do not waste them), fat (render it: lard and tallow are precious), bones (long-simmered stock, then to the compost or the dogs), blood (black pudding), skin, feathers (pillows and insulation), and the intestines if you are making sausage.
  • Preserve immediately (Ch. 03 §6): pressure canning is the best power-independent option for meat and stock — jars of chicken and stock on a shelf need no freezer and no electricity. Then salting, curing, smoking, drying, and confit.
  • Timing: the traditional autumn slaughter exists because the animal is fat from the summer, the weather is cold enough to work safely, and you are not feeding it through the winter. Keep breeding stock; eat the rest.

8. Legal and neighbourly

  • Check municipal rules on poultry and livestock in residential areas — roosters are the usual sticking point and are banned or restricted in many urban areas.
  • Registration requirements (many countries require a holding number for livestock, and movement/medicine records).
  • Slaughter law, waste and carcass disposal law, manure storage rules.
  • Talk to your neighbours before, not after. Noise, smell and flies are manageable with good husbandry, and a neighbour who gets a dozen eggs a week is an ally rather than a complainant. This is also the beginning of your mutual-aid group (Ch. 10).

9. Build sequence

LevelLand track
Now, €0Find a local keeper and visit · join a poultry/beekeeping association · read one good book per species · check your local rules
L1, €400–700Coop + secure run built · 4–6 heritage hens · feeders, drinkers, grit, oyster shell · 3 months of feed
L2, €800–1,500+ a rooster · 6–12 months of feed stored · fodder crops (mangels, pumpkins, kale) planted · rabbit trio + hutches · black soldier fly bucket · first broody hatch completed
L3, €2,000++ bees · ducks or geese · pigs raised over a summer · goats if you have pasture · pressure canner and full butchery kit · a second breeding line, or a chick/kit exchange agreement with your group (genetics matter — a closed flock degrades)

10. Animals checklist

[ ] I have calculated the annual feed requirement in kg — and where it comes from
[ ] 6–12 months of feed stored, plus grit and oyster shell for years
[ ] Fodder crops (mangel, fodder beet, pumpkin, kale, comfrey) planted for winter
[ ] Breeds chosen that go broody / breed without an incubator
[ ] A rooster (or a fertile-egg plan) — the flock can actually renew
[ ] Housing is predator-proof with weld mesh, buried skirt, and a nightly door
[ ] Ventilation without draughts; dry bedding; deep litter started
[ ] Water that will not freeze in January, plus a spare drinker
[ ] Free-range or rotational area to cut the feed bill
[ ] Black soldier fly or mealworm bucket running
[ ] Rabbits considered seriously (quiet, forage-fed, high output)
[ ] Fat source planned — rabbit alone is a trap
[ ] Slaughter and butchery learned from a real person, with sharp tools ready
[ ] Preservation ready before the first slaughter (canner, jars, salt, smoker)
[ ] Local law checked: roosters, registration, slaughter, waste
[ ] Neighbours spoken to; eggs shared
[ ] Cull-down plan written for a genuine feed shortage (breeding core defined)

09 — The Apartment Playbook 🏢

Everything in chapters 01–06 applies to you. This chapter covers what is different, what is harder, and — the part that surprises people — what a flat can actually produce.

1. The honest limits, stated up front

You cannot feed yourself from a flat. Not from a balcony, not with grow lights, not with a vertical garden. A 6 m² balcony in full sun, worked expertly, might produce 15–30 kg of vegetables a season — perhaps 3–5 days' worth of calories over a whole year. Anyone who tells you otherwise is selling something.

So the apartment strategy is different in kind, not just in scale:

🏡 LAND:      store food  +  PRODUCE food        → aims at self-sufficiency
🏢 APARTMENT: store food  +  PRODUCE NUTRITION   → aims at endurance

Your stored calories are the plan. What you grow indoors is vitamin C, fresh greens, live enzymes, variety, and morale — the things that stored grain lacks and that stop a 9-month confinement turning into scurvy, depression and illness.

That is not a consolation prize. It is the exact gap that kills people on storage diets, and it is entirely solvable in a flat, in any season, in a few square metres, with no soil and almost no power.

The second apartment truth: your building is your land. A block of forty flats has a roof, a courtyard, a basement, a stairwell, and — most importantly — forty households of skills, tools and labour. The single highest-value thing an apartment dweller can do is organise the building (§10).

2. What a flat CAN produce — ranked by value

MethodCycleSpaceLight neededYieldValue
Sprouts3–5 daysA shelfNone100 g seed → 400–800 g⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ The single most important apartment food technique
Microgreens7–14 daysWindowsill or a €20 LED shelfYes, modest10 g seed → 150–250 g/tray⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ Fresh greens, fast, all year
Fermentation (kraut, kimchi, pickles)1–3 weeks, keeps 6–12 monthsA cupboardNoneTurns 5 kg of cabbage into a winter's vitamin C⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ Zero power, zero skill barrier

3. Sprouting — the apartment keystone

Learn this technique this week. It is the most important thing in this chapter.

Sprouting takes stored dry seed — which you already have, which keeps for years, which weighs nothing — and converts it in 3–5 days into fresh food with vitamin C that the dry seed did not have (vitamin C is synthesised during germination), plus greatly increased B vitamins, enzymes and digestibility. It needs no soil, no light, no power, no garden, and no season.

Be clear about what it does: sprouting does not create calories — dry matter actually falls slightly. It converts stored calories into fresh nutrition. That is exactly the gap in a long-term storage diet, and nothing else fills it so cheaply.

Equipment (€20 total)

  • 3–4 wide-mouth 1 L glass jars
  • Mesh lids (or muslin and a rubber band)
  • A rack or bowl to stand them at 45° to drain
  • That's it.

Method

  1. Rinse 2–4 tablespoons (30–60 g) of seed, put it in the jar.
  2. Soak 6–12 hours in plenty of cool water.
  3. Drain thoroughly and invert the jar at 45° so it keeps draining.
  4. Rinse and drain twice a day (three times in hot weather). Drainage is everything — standing water is what causes spoilage.
  5. Harvest at 2–5 days, when the shoot is 1–3× the seed length.
  6. Rinse, drain well, refrigerate or keep somewhere cool; eat within 3–5 days.
  7. Start a new jar every day. Four jars on rotation = a continuous daily harvest, forever, from a bucket of seed.

What to sprout, and expected yields (by weight)

SeedDaysYield multipleNotes
Mung bean3–54–6×The workhorse. Bulky, mild, filling, cheap.
Lentil (whole brown/green)2–43–4×Fast, reliable, nutty. Ordinary supermarket lentils work.
Chickpea3–43×Hearty, good in salads and hummus
Alfalfa4–68–10×Highest yield by weight, very high vitamin
Radish / mustard / cress3–55–6×Peppery, high vitamin C, fast
Broccoli3–55–6×High in sulforaphane
Fenugreek3–54–5×Medicinal, distinctive
Wheat / barley / oat groats2–32–3×Sprouted grain for bread, or grow on for wheatgrass
Sunflower (in shell)2 days then grow on as shoots—Best as microgreens
⚠️ Never sprout——Kidney/red beans (toxic raw — must be boiled), tomato, potato, aubergine, pepper or rhubarb seed (nightshade/oxalate toxicity)

Food safety (take this seriously)

Sprouts are grown warm and damp — which is also ideal for Salmonella and E. coli, and sprouts have caused real outbreaks.

  • Buy seed intended for sprouting where you can, and keep it dry and sealed.
  • Rinse thoroughly and drain completely, twice a day, every day. Most failures are drainage failures.
  • Keep jars at room temperature, not hot. Discard anything that smells sour, slimy or musty — trust your nose absolutely.
  • Clean jars and lids between batches (boiling water).
  • If anyone in the household is pregnant, elderly, very young or immunocompromised, cook the sprouts (stir-fried mung sprouts are excellent) rather than eating them raw.

How much seed to store

5 kg of mixed sprouting seed per person covers a year of daily fresh food and costs about €25–40. It stores 4+ years in sealed jars. Nothing else in this entire plan delivers as much nutritional security per euro or per litre of storage space. Buy it this week.

4. Microgreens — fresh greens in 10 days

Seedlings harvested at the first true-leaf stage. More substantial than sprouts, genuinely delicious, and about as fast as food gets.

  • Tray: any shallow container, 20 × 30 cm, with drainage. Stack of 4–6 trays on a shelf = a continuous supply.
  • Medium: 2–3 cm of compost, coir, or even a folded damp paper towel or a hemp mat (no soil needed for most).
  • Seed: sown thickly — 10–15 g per tray. Cover for the first 2–3 days (darkness encourages an even, leggy start), then uncover and give light.
  • Light: a bright windowsill works; a €20–30 LED strip at 20–40 W works better and works in winter, running 12–16 h/day (≈ 300–600 Wh/day — plan for it in your power budget, or use the windowsill and accept slower growth).
  • Harvest at 7–14 days with scissors, just above the medium. 150–250 g per tray.
  • Best for volume and substance: pea shoots and sunflower shoots — these are the most filling and the closest to a real vegetable. Then radish, broccoli, mustard, kale, beet, coriander, basil.
  • Reuse the compost by adding it to a wormery or a balcony pot and refreshing it.
  • Pea shoots deserve special mention: ordinary dried marrowfat/whole peas from the supermarket, soaked overnight and sown thickly, give a big tray of sweet shoots in 10 days, and will regrow a second cut. Cheapest fresh green there is.

5. Mushrooms — protein and texture from waste

Oyster mushrooms (Pleurotus ostreatus) are the easiest and the most useful.

  • Substrate: used coffee grounds, shredded cardboard, straw, sawdust, or paper. Pasteurise straw by soaking in hot (65–75 °C) water for an hour, or use fresh coffee grounds within 24 hours (already effectively pasteurised).
  • Spawn: buy grain spawn (€10–20/kg). Mix ~5–10% spawn by weight into the substrate, pack into a bucket or bag with small holes.
  • Colonise in a dark, warm-ish cupboard for 2–4 weeks (it goes white).
  • Fruit: move to light and humidity (mist twice a day, or a bag with holes). Mushrooms appear over 5–10 days.
  • Yield: 1 kg of substrate gives 200–400 g of mushrooms, usually over 2–3 flushes.
  • Spent substrate goes to the wormery or the balcony pots.
  • You can propagate your own spawn from a piece of a mushroom stem on damp cardboard — this is what makes it a cycle rather than a purchase.
  • ⚠️ Only grow species you bought as spawn from a reputable supplier. Never eat a wild or unknown mushroom, and never eat anything growing on your substrate that doesn't look like what you inoculated. Contaminant moulds (green, black, pink) mean discard the batch outdoors.

6. Fermentation — the zero-power vitamin store

This is how Central and Eastern Europe survived winters for centuries, and it needs nothing but vegetables, salt, a jar and a cupboard.

Sauerkraut, the complete method:

  1. Shred 5 kg of cabbage.
  2. Add 2% salt by weight (100 g of salt for 5 kg of cabbage).
  3. Massage/pound for 10 minutes until it releases its own brine.
  4. Pack tightly into a crock or jars; everything must stay below the brine (use a weight, a cabbage leaf, or a water-filled bag).
  5. Ferment at 18–20 °C for 1–3 weeks, burping jars or using an airlock.
  6. Move to somewhere cold (cellar, unheated room, fridge). Keeps 6–12 months.

Why it matters: sauerkraut retains most of the cabbage's vitamin C and becomes more digestible. Historically it is the anti-scurvy food of this region. A 10 L crock is cheap, lasts forever, and is entirely power-independent.

Also ferment: cucumbers, carrots, beetroot, turnip, garlic, peppers, green tomatoes, and hot sauce. And for the cupboard: sourdough starter (bread from flour and water alone, no yeast purchase), vinegar (from wine, cider or scraps — and vinegar is a preservative in its own right), and yoghurt/kefir if you have milk (kefir grains multiply themselves indefinitely — a true renewable).

Stock: a 5–10 L fermenting crock or a dozen 1.5 L jars with airlocks, several kg of non-iodised salt, and a weight for each vessel.

7. Balcony and window growing

Accept the constraint and grow high-value, not high-calorie.

CropContainerYield per seasonWorth it?
Cherry tomatoes20–30 L pot2–4 kg per plant✅ Best value on a sunny balcony
Chillies & peppers10 L pot0.5–2 kg✅ Dry them; they keep forever and transform dull food
Cut-and-come-again saladWindow boxWeekly cuts for months✅
Bush beans15 L pot0.3–0.5 kg✅ And they fix nitrogen
RadishWindow box4 week cycles✅ Fast morale
Potatoes40 L sack1–2 kg✅ The only real calories available
HerbsSmall potsContinuous✅✅ Best value per cm²
StrawberriesHanging basket0.5 kg✅ Perennial, propagates itself
Courgette30 L pot2–5 kg⚠️ Takes a lot of space
Dwarf/patio fruit tree40 L pot2–5 kg from year 3✅ If you'll be there

Practical points

  • Sun is the limit. South/southwest facing, 6+ hours. A north-facing balcony will do salad, herbs and mushrooms and not much else — don't fight it, grow indoors instead.
  • Wind dries pots brutally on a high balcony. Screen with mesh; use bigger pots (they dry more slowly); mulch the surface.
  • Weight: wet compost is ~700 kg/m³. A row of large pots is a real structural load — keep them over the edge beams, near the wall, and check your building's rules. Never overload a balcony.
  • Self-watering containers (a reservoir below the soil) halve your watering and survive a missed day. Easy to make from stacked boxes.
  • Reuse compost by mixing in wormery castings, a handful of lime, and some fresh material each season. Store a couple of bags of compost — it is not something you can improvise in a flat.
  • Vertical: railing planters, wall pockets, tiered shelves, hanging baskets. Trellis climbing beans and cucumbers up a string to the ceiling — vertical space is the one dimension a flat has plenty of.

8. Quail on a balcony — the realistic livestock

Reread Ch. 08 §5 for the details. In summary:

  • 10 coturnix quail in 1 m² (a 3-tier rabbit hutch or a purpose-built cage) give 8–9 eggs a day — the protein equivalent of ~1.7 chicken eggs daily.
  • Laying from 6–8 weeks old. Fastest protein of any livestock.
  • Quiet, small, and legal in far more places than chickens — but check your lease and local rules, and talk to your neighbours first.
  • Feed: game-bird crumb (20–24% protein), plus your microgreen trimmings, sprout surplus and mealworms. ~25–30 g/bird/day → 10 birds ≈ 100 kg of feed a year. Store it.
  • They do not go broody — renewal needs a small incubator (€60, ~40 W for 17 days) and a heat plate for the chicks. That is a power dependency; plan for it.
  • Droppings go to the wormery/balcony pots. Manage bedding well or the smell will end your neighbourly relations and your quail project together.

9. Apartment-specific failure modes

These are the things that make a flat different, and each needs a decision now.

ProblemWhat happensWhat to do about it
Water pressure lossBuildings above ~5–8 floors are fed by electric booster pumps. No power = no water above the low floors, immediately.Store aggressively (Ch. 01). Deploy the bath bladder at AMBER. Know the route to the ground-floor tap or the building's tank.
No lift20 L of water = 20 kg. A family needs 4 trips a day, up 8 floors. Groceries, waste, everything.Store so you need fewer trips. A rope and a bucket from the balcony is not a joke — it is the practical answer. A folding sack truck helps on the flat.
Sewer backupIf mains pumping stations fail, or lower flats keep flushing, sewage backs up into the lowest flats.Stop flushing at the first sign. Go to the bucket system (Ch. 05). Lower-floor residents: consider a drain plug/non-return valve.
Waste with no collectionA family generates ~1 m³ of rubbish a month, plus 100+ L of sanitation waste. It cannot stay in a flat.The balcony drum, the building agreement, or the courtyard. Decide in GREEN. (Ch. 05 §3)
Heating lossDistrict heating and gas boilers both fail without power. A flat cools more slowly than a house (neighbours insulate you) but reaches the same place.Warm room → warm tent inside the room. Layers, hot water bottles, duvets. Never a flueless heater overnight. (Ch. 04)
No safe combustionYou cannot run a generator, a charcoal stove or an unflued heater in a flat. Ever. CO kills in minutes in a sealed space.Alcohol and gas stoves with ventilation, thermal cooker, and battery/solar power only. CO alarm mandatory.
FireCrisis cooking + candles + crowded rooms + no fire brigade. A flat fire spreads to neighbours.Extinguisher + fire blanket + smoke alarm. Two exit routes known. Never leave a flame unattended. This is a higher risk in a flat than in a house.
Smell, noise, light, smokeYour neighbours will know exactly what you have. Cooking smells travel through a stairwell; a generator or a lit window advertises.Cook when others do. Blackout curtains. Discretion. (Ch. 10)
Nowhere to storeA year of food for four is ~1 tonne and 1.5–2 m³.Under beds, wardrobe bases, above cupboards, behind the sofa, hallway cupboard, a rented storage unit, a cellar locker if the building has one. Dense, calorie-rich foods first.
You cannot modify anythingNo wood stove, no well, no solar on the roof, no compost heap.Plug-in and portable solutions only: power stations, folding/balcony panels, bucket systems, indoor growing. And organise the building (below).
Everyone is very closeDisease spreads through a block via shared stairwells, lifts, handrails and ventilation.Mask in shared spaces, use the stairs not the lift, disinfect your own door handle, ventilate your flat, HEPA box in the shared room. (Ch. 06)

10. The building is your land

This is the apartment track's equivalent of a hectare, and it is the highest-value thing you can do. A block of flats has, collectively:

  • A roof — rainwater catchment for the whole building, and possibly solar.
  • A courtyard, lawn or verge — a communal garden, compost bays, a waste drum.
  • A basement or bike store — bulk storage, a shared freezer, a workshop.
  • A stairwell — a communication network.
  • Dozens of households — with a nurse, an electrician, a plumber, a gardener, a carpenter and a lot of labour among them.

Do this now, in GREEN:

  1. Meet your immediate neighbours. Names, numbers, who lives alone, who is elderly, who has medical needs, who has skills. This costs nothing and is worth more than any purchase in this plan.
  2. Propose the boring, popular things first: a communal herb planter, a building WhatsApp/Signal group, a shared tool cupboard, a bulk-buying group. Nobody objects to these, and they build the relationships that matter later.
  3. Then the resilience items: a rainwater butt on a downpipe, a compost bin, a shared generator or battery for the stairwell lights and phone charging, an agreed plan for waste if collections stop, a list of who checks on whom.
  4. Get it minuted. A building committee decision in GREEN is worth ten arguments in RED.
  5. Share what you learn. A neighbour with two weeks of food is a neighbour who is not knocking on your door in week three — and who might have the medicine you run out of in week nine.

11. The apartment kit list

L0 — this weekend (€250)

6 × 10 L water bottles filled · NaDCC tablets · Sawyer Mini · 4 headlamps · 16 rechargeable AA + charger · 20,000 mAh power bank + 20 W folding panel · wind-up radio · 2 weeks of food you eat · butane stove + 8 canisters · 2 buckets + toilet seat + 2 coir bricks · first aid kit · CO alarm · fire blanket · cash · documents in one bag

L1 — this month (€700–1,200)

Bathtub bladder · 6 × 20 L jerry cans · gravity filter · 1 month of food · 5 kg of sprouting seed + 4 jars + mesh lids · fermenting crock + 5 kg salt · 500 Wh–1 kWh LiFePO₄ power station + 200 W of folding/balcony panels · thermal cooker · pressure cooker · full medical chest + FFP2s · urine-diverting insert · plunger washer · menstrual cups · window film · heavy curtains · board games and books

L2 — three months (€2,500–4,000)

3 months of food, densely packed and inventoried · 10 jerry cans (200 L) · balcony rain catch · 1.5–2 kWh power station + 400 W balcony solar (check local rules) · 12 V compressor cool box · microgreen shelf with LED (4–6 trays on rotation) · mushroom kit + spawn · wormery · 3 months of cover material (8–10 coir bricks) · sealed 120 L waste drum · 3-month medicine buffer · CO₂ meter · Corsi–Rosenthal box · balcony containers, compost and seed · the building agreement, in writing

L3 — twelve months (where space allows)

12 months of staples (needs a cellar locker or storage unit) · quail with feed stored + incubator · continuous sprout/microgreen/mushroom rotation running as a daily habit · shared building rainwater and solar · a mutual-aid group of 3–8 households with complementary skills · a rural contact — a relative, friend or allotment — as a fallback and a source of bulk vegetables, eggs and firewood

12. Apartment checklist

[ ] I accept that stored food is the plan, and indoor growing is the nutrition
[ ] 5 kg of sprouting seed per person, plus jars — and I have sprouted a jar
[ ] Microgreen trays and seed in stock; one crop grown as practice
[ ] Fermenting crock + salt; one batch of sauerkraut made
[ ] Mushroom spawn and substrate plan
[ ] Bathtub bladder ready to deploy at AMBER
[ ] 200 L+ of water in jerry cans, stored where I can actually lift them
[ ] Hot water cylinder drain valve located and understood
[ ] Bucket toilet built and tested; 3 months of cover material stored
[ ] The destination for human waste is decided and agreed with the building
[ ] Power station + panels: enough for phones, lights, radio and medical devices
[ ] NO combustion device that could run unflued; CO alarm fitted
[ ] Fire extinguisher, fire blanket, smoke alarm; two exit routes known
[ ] Cooking possible with ventilation only: gas/alcohol + thermal cooker
[ ] 3 months of food stored in dense, calorie-rich form, inventoried
[ ] Balcony containers, compost and seed ready (if there is sun)
[ ] Quail considered, lease and neighbours checked
[ ] Neighbours' names and numbers known; building group exists
[ ] Building-level plan for water, waste and power discussed and minuted
[ ] A rural fallback contact identified
[ ] Blackout weekend drill completed in the flat, in winter

10 — Security, Community & Communications

The fortress mentality is the most seductive and most wrong idea in preparedness. Isolated households fail. Connected neighbourhoods endure. This chapter is about being un-noticed, being well-connected, and being informed.

1. The realistic threat picture

In a pandemic, the security risks are — in order of actual likelihood:

  1. Opportunistic theft — an unlocked shed, a bicycle, a generator left out, a delivery on a doorstep, fuel siphoned from a car. Common, low-drama, and almost entirely preventable with locks and habits.
  2. Desperate neighbours — people who did not prepare, in week six, who know or suspect you did. This is a social problem, not a tactical one, and it is by far the most likely difficulty you will face.
  3. Burglary of empty or apparently empty properties — rises in any economic crisis.
  4. Disputes — over water, fuel, boundaries, noise, animals, or somebody's behaviour during a stressful confinement. More likely to harm you than any stranger.
  5. Fire and accident with no emergency services available.
  6. Organised violence — very unlikely in a pandemic scenario in a stable European country, and if it does occur, no household plan defends against it. Community, distance and leaving are the only real answers.

Calibrate your effort accordingly. Good locks, good lighting discipline, good neighbours and a fire extinguisher will do more for your safety than anything tactical. Preparing for scenario 6 while neglecting scenarios 1–5 is a misallocation that many people make.

2. OPSEC — the discipline of not being interesting

The best security is that nobody thinks you have anything. Everything below is free.

Before the crisis

  • Don't talk about your preparations. Not at work, not at parties, not on social media, not in a Facebook group, not "just to a friend". Every person who knows is a person who will remember your address in week six — and who will mention it to someone else.
  • Take deliveries discreetly; break down and dispose of packaging that advertises (a stack of 20 L bucket boxes at the kerb is a billboard).
  • Keep the shed, garage and greenhouse locked, and the good tools out of sight.
  • Be careful with photos: a picture of your pantry, your solar array or your chickens, geotagged, is an inventory with a map.

During the crisis

  • Light discipline. In a dark street, a brightly lit window is visible for a kilometre. Blackout curtains or blankets over windows after dark. Use low, warm, directed light — headlamps rather than room lights.
  • Sound discipline. A generator is audible for hundreds of metres and says "this house has fuel and things worth powering". Run it in daylight, when other noise exists, for short batch-charging sessions (Ch. 02), inside a baffle box or behind a wall, and never at night.
  • Smell discipline. Cooking smells carry astonishingly far when nobody else is cooking. Cook at conventional mealtimes; use enclosed stoves; be careful with baking bread and roasting meat when others are hungry.
  • Sight discipline. Don't unload supplies in view. Don't let a visible garden full of food face a public road without a hedge or a fence. Keep the front of the house looking as unremarkable and as occupied-but-ordinary as everyone else's.
  • Routine discipline. Vary times if you must go out. Don't establish a visible pattern.
  • Conversation discipline. "We're managing, same as everyone" is the answer. Not "we're fine, we've got a year of food".

3. Layered physical security — in order of value

1. RELATIONSHIPS  — neighbours who look out for you   (free, best)
2. APPEARANCE     — nothing worth taking, nothing unusual  (free)
3. DETERRENCE     — lighting, dogs, visible locks, a lived-in look  (cheap)
4. BARRIERS       — doors, locks, windows, fences, gates  (moderate)
5. DETECTION      — alarms, sensors, a dog, a watch rota  (moderate)
6. DELAY & REFUGE — a securable inner room, a plan, a way out  (last)

Practical, legal, and worth doing:

  • Upgrade the doors and locks. Most break-ins are the front or back door forced in seconds. A solid door, a good multi-point lock or a deadbolt with a long strike screwed into the frame studs, hinge bolts, and a door chain cost a few hundred euros and are the single most effective physical measure.
  • Window locks and laminated security film on accessible glass (film keeps a broken window in one piece — cheap and very effective).
  • Motion-sensor lights (solar ones need no wiring and work in a blackout).
  • A dog. An ordinary, noisy family dog is the best intruder detection there is, and good company through a long confinement. Factor in a year of dog food.
  • Fences, gates and a thorny hedge — hawthorn, blackthorn, berberis, rugosa rose. A thorn hedge is a fence that grows itself, feeds birds, gives you fruit, and looks like gardening rather than fortification.
  • Secure the outbuildings — this is where most theft actually happens.
  • Don't store all your supplies in one place. Split the store across at least two locations (cellar + loft + a neighbour's garage). A single loss should never be total.
  • A refuge room — one room with a solid door and a lock where the family can gather, with phones, light and water, while you wait for a situation to pass or for help. This is the cheap, sane version of a "safe room".

On firearms: laws vary enormously by country and this plan makes no recommendation. If you already own firearms lawfully, secure storage, safe handling, training and ammunition rotation are your responsibility. If you do not, a crisis is the worst possible time to acquire and learn. Far more households are harmed by a badly stored weapon than are saved by one, and no weapon solves the problems in §1.

4. Community — the actual survival strategy

Build a mutual-aid group of 3–8 households. Start this month.

One household cannot: keep a 24-hour watch, contain a fire, treat a serious injury, cover every skill, share the cost of a well or a generator, care for children while an adult is ill, or look after two people when both adults are sick at once. A group of five households can do all of that.

How to build one, starting from nothing

  1. Start with something ordinary. A street WhatsApp/Signal group. A tool library. A bulk-buy order. A shared skip. A seed swap. Helping an elderly neighbour with shopping. None of this requires mentioning "prepping" at all.
  2. Notice who is capable and who is reliable. Over a few months you will learn who shows up. Those are your people.
  3. Have the conversation gently, one household at a time, framed around things everyone remembers: "after the last few years, I've been thinking we should have a plan as a street for if the power goes out for a week."
  4. Agree small, concrete things: a check-in signal, who has a chainsaw, who is medically trained, who has a generator, who lives alone and needs checking on, where you'd meet.
  5. Grow it into: shared bulk purchasing (cheaper for everyone, less conspicuous than one household buying a tonne of rice), shared expensive kit (a chainsaw, a log splitter, a pressure canner, a generator, a freezer), a skill exchange, childcare, and a watch rota if it ever comes to that.

What a good group has

  • Complementary skills: medical (a nurse or paramedic is worth more than any amount of kit), electrical, mechanical, building, growing, animal husbandry, cooking and preserving, childcare, and at least one person everyone trusts to mediate an argument.
  • Complementary resources: someone with land, someone with a well, someone with a workshop, someone with a vehicle, someone with a big freezer.
  • Agreed decision-making. How do you decide something, and who decides when you disagree? Sort this out while it is theoretical.
  • Explicit reciprocity. Generosity works between people who both give. Agree what is shared, what is lent, and what is private — before scarcity makes it emotional.

The rural/urban link

If you are in a flat, cultivate a rural contact: a relative, a friend with land, an allotment, a farm you buy from directly. If you have land, cultivate urban contacts who have skills, tools and hands. This link runs both ways in a crisis and both sides need it.

5. The charity plan — decide this now, in calm

In week six, someone you know will knock on your door and say their children are hungry. Decide today what you will do, because deciding in the moment, at the door, with your own children behind you, is how people end up making choices they cannot live with.

The reasoning:

  • Turning everyone away hardens you, poisons your relationships with the community you depend on, and — practically — creates the exact resentment that makes you a target.
  • Giving without limit empties your stores and helps nobody, including the person you gave to.
  • So set a policy, in advance, that you can sustain and explain.

A workable approach:

  1. Set aside a "charity shelf" as a deliberate line item — perhaps 10% of your store, bought and packed separately. Cheap, bulky, high-calorie items: rice, oats, flour, beans, pasta, oil, salt. This is not "spare" food; it is budgeted, and giving it does not touch your family's rations.
  2. Give at the gate, not in the house. Nobody needs to see inside.
  3. Give food, not information. Never confirm how much you have.
  4. Prefer giving through the group or a community point — a church, a neighbour who is organising, a street food table. It is more effective, more dignified for the recipient, and it does not mark your door.
  5. Prefer giving work where you can: "I can't give much, but there's a day's work here and you'll eat with us." Dignity matters, and labour is a thing you will need.
  6. Prioritise: children, the elderly, the ill, and the people in your mutual-aid group — in that order.
  7. Have a line, and hold it kindly. "This is what we can give. I'm sorry it isn't more." No lectures, no justifications, no arguments at the door.
  8. Help people prepare NOW instead. Every neighbour who has two weeks of food is one who will not knock in week three. Sharing this plan is a security measure, and the cheapest one available.

6. Communications

Getting information in

  • Battery/wind-up/solar radio with FM + AM + shortwave. €40–80. FM gives you local and national broadcasting; shortwave gives you the international picture when domestic media is disrupted or unreliable. Buy one now, and learn to use it — listening to shortwave is a skill.
  • A second radio in the go-bag. Radios are cheap; being uninformed is not.
  • DAB dies with the transmitter network's power; FM and AM are far more robust. Don't rely on DAB alone.
  • NOAA/weather band or your national weather service — weather matters a lot more when your food is outdoors and your heat is wood.
  • Note official emergency broadcast frequencies for your country on paper.

Talking to each other

  • Mobile networks fail — cells have 2–8 hours of battery, and networks congest instantly in a crisis. SMS often gets through when calls do not.
  • PMR446 / FRS radios (licence-free in the EU): €50 a pair, 1–3 km in real terrain. Perfect for the street, the garden, a shopping trip, and the mutual-aid group. Buy 4, plus rechargeable batteries.
  • Amateur radio (ham) is the serious option: real range, repeater networks, emergency traffic handling, and a community of skilled operators. It requires a licence (a few weeks of study and an exam — do it now, it's genuinely interesting) and €50–200 for a handheld. A licensed operator in your mutual-aid group is a major asset.
  • ⚠️ Transmitting on amateur frequencies without a licence is illegal, and "it's an emergency" only covers genuine immediate danger to life. Get licensed in GREEN.
  • Agreed radio schedule: the group checks in at a fixed time on a fixed channel, e.g. 09:00 and 19:00 daily. A schedule saves batteries and means a missed check-in is itself information.
  • Low-tech signals, agreed in advance: a coloured cloth in a window (green = all well, red = help needed), a whistle code, a torch signal at night, a chalk mark. Free, silent, and they work when everything else doesn't.

The family contact plan (write it, print it, everyone carries a copy)

- Out-of-area contact person (often reachable when local networks are congested)
- Everyone's number, address, workplace, school — ON PAPER
  (nobody memorises numbers any more; a dead phone is total memory loss)
- Primary rally point (home) and secondary rally point (if home is unreachable)
- Check-in times and the order to try: SMS → call → radio → in person
- What each person does if separated, per scenario, per location
- Where the spare key, the documents, the cash and the go-bag are
- Medical information for each person

Practise it with the children. Twice a year.

Offline information — build a paper library

The internet will not always be there, and a phone with a dead battery contains nothing. Print what matters.

  • Medical: Where There Is No Doctor, Where There Is No Dentist, a first aid manual
  • Food: a preserving book with tested canning times, a bread book, a fermentation book, a foraging guide with good photographs for your region
  • Growing: a vegetable growing guide for your climate, a seed-saving guide, a fruit pruning guide
  • Practical: basic electrics and plumbing, engine and bicycle maintenance, knots, sewing, soap making, butchery
  • Local: paper maps of your region (road and topographic), a compass
  • Your own plan, printed, in a binder, with the checklists filled in
  • An e-reader with thousands of books uses about 1 Wh per day and is an outstanding use of a small solar system — but keep the critical references on paper as well. Electronics fail; paper doesn't.

7. Security & communications checklist

[ ] Nobody outside the household knows the scale of our preparations
[ ] No photos, posts or geotagged evidence of supplies exist
[ ] Blackout curtains or blankets for every window
[ ] Generator siting, timing and baffling planned for noise
[ ] Doors and locks upgraded; hinge bolts and long strike plates fitted
[ ] Window locks; security film on accessible glass
[ ] Outbuildings and tools secured
[ ] Motion-sensor (solar) lighting fitted
[ ] Supplies split across at least two locations
[ ] Refuge room identified
[ ] Fire extinguishers, blanket and smoke alarms — no fire brigade coming
[ ] 3–8 household mutual-aid group forming, with names and numbers
[ ] Group skills mapped; gaps identified
[ ] Shared kit and bulk-buying arrangements agreed
[ ] Charity shelf bought, packed separately, and its policy agreed in writing
[ ] Neighbours encouraged and helped to prepare themselves
[ ] FM/AM/shortwave radio + spare, with batteries
[ ] PMR446 radios ×4 with rechargeables; ham licence being pursued
[ ] Radio check-in schedule agreed with the group
[ ] Low-tech signals agreed (window cloth, whistle, torch)
[ ] Family contact plan written, printed, carried, and practised
[ ] Paper library printed and shelved
[ ] Paper maps and a compass
[ ] Rural↔urban contact established
