11 — Tools, Spares, Money & Documents

The unglamorous chapter. It is about the things that break, the things you cannot buy, and the pieces of paper that prove who you are.

1. The single-point-of-failure audit

Walk through your whole plan and ask one question: what breaks, and what happens when it does?

SystemThe single point of failureThe fix
WaterThe filter element2 spare elements + chemical disinfection + a pot to boil in
WaterThe pumpA hand pump, or a bucket and rope
PowerThe charge controllerA spare (€60). It is the most likely electronic failure.
PowerThe inverter12 V DC lighting and USB that works without it
PowerCables and connectorsSpare MC4 connectors, cable, fuses, crimps
CookingThe stoveA second, different-fuel stove + a thermal cooker
HeatThe chimneyOwn rods, sweep it annually
FoodThe can openerThree. (The classic.)
FoodThe grain millThe hand version, plus spare burrs
LightBatteriesRechargeables + solar charger, one standard cell size
HealthGlassesA spare pair per person + prescription copy
HealthPrescription medicineA buffer, built legally over months
GrowingSeedA 3–5 year seed bank + saved seed + OP varieties
LivestockThe rooster / breeding stockKeep two, or an exchange agreement with the group
SecurityThe one person who knows how it all worksWrite it down and teach someone else
EverythingThe house burning downSplit the store; fire extinguishers; insurance documents offsite

That last one is the most commonly overlooked. If only one adult understands the power system, the water treatment, the stove and where everything is, then that person getting ill — which is precisely what a pandemic does — disables the whole household. Write the manual. Teach the family. Run a drill where that person says nothing all weekend.

2. Tools

Buy quality once. Cheap tools fail at the worst moment, and there will be no replacement.

Hand tools — the non-negotiable core

Hammer (claw + club) · screwdrivers (or a good ratchet set with bits) · spanners (metric set) + adjustable · pliers (combination, long-nose, water pump) · wire cutters · hacksaw + spare blades · wood saw (panel + tenon) · chisels · hand drill or brace + bits (works with no power) · files and rasps · sharpening stone + file + honing steel · measuring tape · spirit level · square · utility knife + blades · vice or clamps · crowbar · bolt cutters · shovel, spade, pick · axe, splitting maul, wedges · bow saw · sledgehammer · wheelbarrow.

Powered tools (while there is power)

Cordless drill/driver + 2 batteries · angle grinder + cutting/grinding discs · circular saw or jigsaw · chainsaw + full PPE · multimeter · soldering iron · inspection lamp. Standardise on one battery platform so packs and chargers are interchangeable, and buy a 12 V DC charger for that platform so you can charge from the solar system directly.

Specialist but high-value

Pipe wrench and plumbing fittings · PTFE tape · pipe freeze kit · drain rods · soldering/compression fittings · wire strippers and crimps · a multimeter (you cannot diagnose a power system without one) · a clamp meter · a tyre pump and puncture kit · a bicycle tool set · a sewing kit and an awl · a manual sewing machine (treadle or hand-crank — genuinely useful and completely power-free) · leatherwork awl and waxed thread · a scythe and peening kit · a hand-cranked drill · a soil auger.

3. The spares and consumables box

These are the things you cannot make, cannot substitute, and will not be able to buy. All of it is cheap, small, and stores indefinitely. This box is one of the highest-value €300 in the whole plan.

Fixings and fastening: screws and nails (assorted, in quantity) · bolts, nuts and washers · wall plugs · zip ties (500) · picture wire · galvanised wire (a roll) · staples · split pins · hose clamps (assorted, a box) · jubilee clips · O-rings and washers (tap, hose, plumbing) · rivets.

Tapes and adhesives: duct/gaffer tape (5 rolls) · electrical tape · self-amalgamating tape (fixes leaking pipes under pressure) · PTFE tape · two-part epoxy · superglue · wood glue · contact adhesive · silicone sealant · plumber's putty · self-fusing repair tape.

Sealing and waterproofing: plastic sheeting and heavy polythene · tarpaulins (2–3, various sizes — endlessly useful) · roofing felt and nails · flashing tape · window film · expanding foam · bitumen paint.

Electrical: fuses (every rating you use — car blade, ceramic, MCBs) · cable (mains and DC, several gauges) · connectors and terminal blocks · crimps · MC4 solar connectors · a spare MPPT charge controller · a spare inverter if budget allows · bulbs and LED strips · switches · a 12 V socket set · batteries (rechargeable + a sealed pack of alkalines for the go-bag).

Fluids: engine oil · 2-stroke oil · chain oil · grease · WD-40/penetrating oil · brake fluid · antifreeze · fuel stabiliser · methylated spirits · white spirit · lamp oil.

Cordage and fabric: rope (10 m each of 6 mm, 10 mm, plus paracord) · baler twine · bungee cords · sewing thread, needles, buttons, zips, elastic · fabric patches and denim offcuts · leather scraps · wool blankets · a roll of canvas.

Consumables you will run out of: matches (thousands, stored dry) and ferro rods (3) · lighters (20) · candles · batteries · water filter elements · canning lids (several hundred) · pressure canner gasket and safety plug · salt · soap · bleach/NaDCC · sandpaper · saw blades · drill bits · grinding discs · chainsaw chains (2 spare) and files · spark plugs and a spare carburettor for each small engine · air and fuel filters · mower/strimmer line · sharpening stones · pencils and paper (ink and printers fail; you will want to write things down for a year).

Personal: boot laces · boot repair glue · spare work gloves (6 pairs — you will destroy them) · spare wellies · thermal base layers · wool socks (lots) · a good hat.

4. Money and barter

Cash

  • ATMs and card terminals fail early in every crisis — a power cut, a network outage, a bank holiday, or a run on cash. Cash works when nothing else does, and only for a while.
  • Hold enough cash for 4–8 weeks of essential spending, in small denominations (nobody will have change for a €200 note, and handing one over tells people exactly the wrong thing about you).
  • Store it split across at least two secure places, not in an obvious box.
  • Keep some smaller coins and notes in the go-bag and the car.
  • Insurance: keep the policy documents where you can reach them, and photograph everything of value now for a future claim.

Beyond cash

  • Hyperinflation and currency stress are realistic in a multi-year crisis. Diversification — some savings in a different form (precious metal, foreign currency, real assets) — is a personal financial decision, not a survival tactic, and this plan makes no investment recommendation. If you do hold physical metal, small denominations only; nobody can make change for a 1 oz coin with potatoes.

Barter goods — the practical list

The best barter goods are cheap now, impossible later, small, non-perishable, and consumed continuously. Buy an extra box of these purely for trade:

TierGoods
BestCoffee · salt · sugar · soap · lighters and matches · batteries · painkillers · toilet paper · candles · yeast · spices · seeds · water filters · NaDCC tablets · sewing supplies · tampons/pads · condoms · alcohol (spirits — also a disinfectant and a preservative)
GoodTea · cooking oil · honey · tinned meat · rope · tape · fuel · nails and screws · socks · gloves · tools · rechargeable batteries · reading glasses · bicycle inner tubes
Best of allSkills. Medical care, dentistry, repair, welding, sewing, butchery, midwifery, teaching. They cannot be stolen, they weigh nothing, and they do not run out.
⚠️ AvoidTrading weapons, ammunition, or prescription medication — it is illegal, it creates danger for you, and it makes you a known source

Barter discipline: trade through the group and at the gate, never from your store, never revealing quantity. A reputation as a trader attracts attention you do not want.

5. The document pack

If you ever have to leave, or if you need to prove anything to anyone, this is what matters. Assemble it this weekend — it costs an afternoon and nothing else.

One waterproof folder, grab-and-go, plus a duplicate elsewhere:

IDENTITY        Passports · ID cards · driving licences · birth certificates
                Marriage certificate · residence permits · citizenship papers
PROPERTY        Deeds or lease · mortgage papers · land registry extract
                Vehicle registration and insurance
FINANCIAL       Bank and account details · insurance policies (home, health,
                life, vehicle) · pension details · recent statements · tax number
MEDICAL         For each person: prescription list (generic names + doses),
                allergies, conditions, blood group, vaccination records,
                GP and specialist contact details, EHIC/health card
                Spare glasses prescription
FAMILY          The contact plan (Ch. 10) · school records · pet vaccination
                and chip records
PRACTICAL       Photos of every room and every valuable (for insurance claims)
                Serial numbers of tools, appliances, generator, bicycles
                Your own system diagrams: power, water, stopcocks, consumer unit
                This plan, printed, with checklists filled in
CASH            Some, in the folder

Three copies, three forms:

  1. Paper in the waterproof folder at home, where anyone in the family can find it.
  2. Encrypted USB (VeraCrypt or similar) in the go-bag, with the passphrase known to two people.
  3. Encrypted cloud backup and/or a copy with a trusted relative in another town. Fire, flood and theft are more likely than any pandemic scenario.

6. Skills — the things that take longest to acquire

Rank your skill gaps and learn one per month. They are free, they weigh nothing, they cannot be stolen, and they are the difference between owning equipment and being capable.

Tier 1 — everyone in the household should have these First aid and CPR · lighting a fire in the wet · using a fire extinguisher · turning off water, gas and electricity at the main · basic hygiene discipline · cooking a meal from dry staples · hand-washing clothes · using the radio · where everything is and how it works.

Tier 2 — at least one adult Cooking from raw ingredients including bread, beans and grains · food preservation: canning, fermenting, drying · vegetable growing and seed saving · sharpening tools · basic sewing and mending · basic plumbing (isolate, drain, fix a leak, replace a washer) · basic electrics and 12 V DC wiring · using a multimeter · small-engine maintenance · bicycle repair · safe axe and saw use · navigation with a map and compass · water treatment · butchering a chicken or a rabbit.

Tier 3 — high value, worth someone in the group having Advanced first aid / wilderness first responder · beekeeping · dairying and cheesemaking · larger-animal husbandry and slaughter · welding · carpentry and building repair · soap making · brewing and distilling (for vinegar and disinfectant — check your local law) · amateur radio · midwifery · dentistry basics · gunsmithing/reloading where lawful · chainsaw certification · tree felling.

How to learn them: local courses, a smallholding association, a beekeeping club, a repair café, the person down the road who already does it. Every one of these skills is more fun to learn than it is to read about, and every one of them is useful in normal times too. That is the test of a good preparation: it makes your ordinary life better as well.

7. Tools, spares & money checklist

[ ] Single-point-of-failure audit done; each one has an answer
[ ] The system manual is WRITTEN, and a second person has been taught
[ ] Full hand-tool set, quality, with sharpening kit
[ ] Cordless platform standardised, with a 12 V charger
[ ] Multimeter owned and used
[ ] The spares box assembled (fixings, tape, epoxy, fuses, cable, cordage)
[ ] Spare charge controller, fuses, connectors and cable
[ ] Spare water filter elements ×2
[ ] Spare glasses per person
[ ] Spare canning lids, canner gasket, chainsaw chains, spark plugs, carburettor
[ ] Matches, ferro rods, lighters, candles — in quantity
[ ] Paper and pencils for a year
[ ] 4–8 weeks of cash in small denominations, split across two places
[ ] Barter box bought separately from the family store
[ ] Document pack assembled: paper + encrypted USB + offsite copy
[ ] Photos of home contents taken for insurance
[ ] System diagrams drawn and filed with the documents
[ ] Skills gap list written; one skill being learned each month
[ ] At least one adult can do every Tier 2 skill

12 — Operating Cycles & Calendar

A stockpile is a countdown. A cycle is a system. This chapter is what turns the first into the second — the routines that keep stores rotated, gardens producing, animals breeding, equipment working and people sane.

This is the chapter that answers "how do I make it repeat?"

1. The four loops

Everything in this plan sits in one of four nested loops. If any loop stops, the whole system quietly degrades until you notice it has failed.

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ANNUAL LOOP   Seed → plant → harvest → preserve → save seed    │
│                Breed → raise → cull → keep breeding stock       │
│                Cut → season → burn wood → cut again             │
│  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  SEASONAL LOOP   Sow, tend, harvest, store, rest          │  │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
│  │  │  WEEKLY LOOP   Bake, launder, clean, inventory,     │  │  │
│  │  │                maintain, plan, rest                  │  │  │
│  │  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │  │
│  │  │  │  DAILY LOOP   Water, power, animals, food,    │  │  │  │
│  │  │  │               sanitation, health, comms       │  │  │  │
│  │  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │  │
│  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │
│  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Plus the continuous indoor loops that run in any season, in any dwelling: sprouts (3–5 days) · microgreens (7–14 days) · mushrooms (3–5 weeks) · ferments (1–3 weeks) · sourdough (daily) · compost/wormery (continuous). These are the loops an apartment can run, and they are the reason a flat is survivable for a year.

2. The daily routine in a crisis

Structure is not bureaucracy — it is the thing that holds a household together over months of confinement. Write this on a card and put it on the wall.

Morning

07:00Wake at a fixed time. Everyone. Drifting sleep is the first sign of a household coming apart.
Health check: anyone symptomatic? Temperature and pulse ox if so. Log it.
Light the stove, put the kettle and the big pot on. One fire does breakfast, hot water and the room.
Water: check levels, run the filter (it should be filtering continuously), top up the day's clean supply, fill the hand-wash station.
Power: check battery state of charge, clear snow/leaves off the panels, plan the day's loads around the forecast. Charge everything while the sun is up.
Animals: feed, water (break ice in winter), collect eggs, check every animal is well and the housing is secure.
08:00Breakfast together. Eat as a household at fixed times — it anchors the day.
Rinse the sprout jars (morning rinse). Check the microgreens and mushrooms.

Day

09:00Radio check-in with the mutual-aid group.
The day's main work, assigned the night before: garden, wood, repairs, preserving, building, laundry, teaching. Everyone has a job.
12:30Lunch. Usually the previous night's leftovers or something from the thermal cooker.
13:30Second work block. Children: school work. Adults: rotate the heavy job.
15:00Start the evening meal early — get it to the boil and into the thermal cooker, so it finishes itself with no more fuel.
Daylight tasks finish: anything needing light gets done before dusk, always.

Evening

17:00Secure for the night: animals shut in, doors locked, tools in, blackout curtains closed, water containers refilled.
Rinse the sprout jars (evening rinse).
18:00Dinner together. No radio, no crisis talk.
19:00Radio check-in. News window: 20 minutes, one source, then off.
Household meeting (10 min): what happened today, what's the plan tomorrow, who is doing what, is anyone struggling.
20:00Non-productive time, deliberately. Cards, music, reading aloud, games, talking. This is not a luxury — it is the maintenance schedule for the people.
22:00Lights out. Log the day in the household journal: weather, stores used, work done, anything that changed.

Rotate the unpleasant jobs (emptying the toilet bucket, hauling water, laundry) on a written rota. Nothing corrodes a household faster than one person silently doing all the worst work.

3. The weekly cycle

DayTask
MondayLaundry. One big batch — water heated once, washed, rinsed, hung. Reuse the water on the garden or for flushing.
TuesdayBake day. Bread for the week; the oven/stove is hot, so also roast, dry herbs, render fat, heat wash water. One fire, many jobs.
WednesdayMaintenance. Sharpen tools, fix what broke, check the stove and flue, service one system properly. Something breaks every week; deal with it before it accumulates.
ThursdayGarden/production day. The big session: planting, weeding, harvesting, preserving. Start the next ferment.
FridayInventory and stores. Count what was used, update the written inventory, check for damp/pests/spoilage, rotate stock forward, set next week's ration.
SaturdayProjects and improvements. Build the thing you've been meaning to build. Also: proper wash day for people.
SundayRest, and celebrate something. A better meal. A game. Contact with people outside the household. Take the day off deliberately — burnout is a survival risk.

Also weekly: test the CO and smoke alarms · check the animals for mites and parasites · clean the water filter housing · check battery water if you have lead-acid · full handover briefing so everyone knows the state of every system.

4. The monthly cycle

  • Full inventory count against the written record. Reconcile. Calculate days of endurance remaining for water, food, fuel, medicine and cover material. Write the number down and track the trend — this single number tells you whether your rationing is right, months before you would otherwise notice.
  • Run the generator for 30 minutes under load (unused engines seize; fuel goes stale in carburettors). Check oil.
  • Rotate fuel. Use the oldest petrol in the car, refill the can.
  • Rotate water. Empty, clean and refill a share of the storage.
  • Check and rotate medicines. Anything within 3 months of expiry moves to the front for use.
  • Test the radios, charge every battery bank, verify the group's comms plan.
  • Deep clean one system: the water filter elements, the stove and flue, the coop, the food store.
  • Group meeting with the mutual-aid households — in person if possible.
  • Skills hour: teach one household member one thing they can't yet do.
  • Review the plan. What changed? What did we learn? Update this document.

5. The garden year — Central Europe, month by month

Adjust to your own last frost (assumed mid-April to early May) and first frost (assumed mid-October).

MonthSow / plantHarvestOther
JanuaryUnder cover: nothing much. Order seed now — good varieties sell out.Leeks, parsnips, kale, Brussels sprouts, winter salad (tunnel), stored rootsPlan the rotation · prune apples and pears · sharpen and oil all tools · force rhubarb · repair structures · check the store for rot weekly
FebruaryIndoors/heated: onions, leeks, chillies, aubergines, early tomatoes. Cold frame: broad beans, early peasAs January + forced rhubarbChit seed potatoes in a light, cool place · turn compost · finish pruning · lime beds that need it
MarchIndoors: tomatoes, brassicas, celery. Direct under fleece: broad beans, peas, spinach, radish, early carrots, parsnips. MayAfter the last frost: plant out tomatoes, squash, courgettes, cucumbers, sweetcorn, French and runner beans, celeriac, leeksAsparagus, rhubarb, salads, spinach, first broad beans, early potatoes (late month)Earth up potatoes · first comfrey cut · mulch everything · slug patrol nightly · succession-sow salad every 2 weeks from now

JulySow the winter crops now: kale, chard, spring cabbage, winter salads, turnips, oriental greens. Plant out leeks and PSBEverything begins: courgettes, beans, cucumbers, onions, berries, currants, early applesBlight watch in warm wet weather — cut potato haulm at the first sign · preserve continuously · save seed from peas and beans
AugustWinter salads, spinach, corn salad, land cress, oriental greens, overwintering onions (late)Peak harvest: tomatoes, beans, cucumbers, sweetcorn, plums, apples, onions, garlicPeak preserving month — canning, drying, fermenting daily · cure onions and garlic · save seed from tomatoes and lettuce · order autumn bulbs and bare-root stock
SeptemberGreen manure on every bare bed · overwintering broad beans (late) · plant strawberry runnersMaincrop potatoes, squash, apples, pears, beans (dry), tomatoes, rootsCure squash 10 days in the sun · dry potatoes in the dark 2 weeks before storing · load the root cellar · final seed saving · take hardwood cuttings
OctoberPlant garlic and overwintering broad beans · green manure on everything bareRoots, cabbage, leeks, kale, late apples, Jerusalem artichokesFirst frost mid-month — lift the tender crops before it · clear and mulch spent beds · protect the tunnel · check all winter housing for animals
NovemberBare-root planting season: fruit trees, bushes, hedging. Do this now.Leeks, parsnips (sweeter after frost), kale, sprouts, stored everythingPrune apples and pears (not stone fruit) · build leaf mould cages · turn compost · drain and lag outdoor pipes · stack next year's wood
DecemberRest.Winter greens, stored roots, tunnel saladsPlan next year from the notebook · order seed · service tools · full inventory · repair · rest properly — this is the recovery month and you need it

6. The livestock year

PeriodPoultryRabbitsGeneral
Jan–FebLowest laying. Extra light if you use it. Keep water ice-free. Deep litter keeps them warm.Breed for spring litters if housing is warm enoughFeed stores at their lowest — check the maths against the calendar
Mar–AprLaying picks up strongly. Watch for the first broody hen. Set 8–12 fertile eggs under her.Main breeding season begins; 4–5 litters aheadSpring clean all housing; treat for mites; worm if needed
May–JunChicks hatch and grow. Peak laying — preserve the surplus (water glassing, Ch. 03). Move to fresh range.Litters every 5–6 weeks; weaning at 4–6 weeksRotate range/pasture; cut and dry hay and comfrey for winter
Jul–AugGrowers on range, eating insects and garden waste — cheapest month of the year to keep poultryPeak production; growers to freezer/jar at 10–12 weeksSow and tend the fodder crops (mangels, fodder beet, kale, pumpkins)
Sep–OctCull the cockerels (16–20 weeks) and the oldest third of the hens. Preserve immediately. Moult begins; laying drops.Reduce breeding; keep the best does and one or two bucksLift and clamp the mangels and fodder beet. Store the pumpkins. Buy in and store the winter feed — this is the month the whole system depends on.
Nov–DecWinter housing checked and draught-proofed (but ventilated). Feed mangels and pumpkins. Minimal laying.Winter rest; feed hay and rootsTraditional pig slaughter and curing month · full feed audit: do I have enough to reach March? If not, cull now, not in February.

7. Maintenance and rotation schedule

IntervalTask
DailyFilter water · check battery SoC · animals fed, watered and secure · sprouts rinsed ×2 · health check · stove ash and air control
WeeklyTest CO/smoke alarms · clean the filter housing · check stores for damp and pests · sharpen what was used · mite check on poultry · update the inventory
MonthlyGenerator run under load · fuel rotated · a share of water storage emptied and refilled · medicines checked for expiry · radios tested · all batteries cycled · full inventory count and endurance calculation
QuarterlyEat-the-store week · deep-clean a system · full first aid kit audit · drill (blackout, water-off, or isolation) · group meeting · review and update the plan
Every 6 monthsFull inventory reconciliation · rotate stored water fully · rotate petrol · chimney check · panel tilt changed (spring/autumn) · test the whole power system under load · replace anything expired
AnnuallyChimney swept · water filter elements replaced or deep-cleaned · water source lab-tested (well/spring) · fire extinguishers serviced · seed germination test · seed bank topped up · CO alarm test with a test gas · insurance and documents reviewed · wood: burn this year's, season next year's, cut the year after's · lime/pH test · rotate the medicine buffer · replace bleach
Every 2–5 yearsReplace NaDCC tablets and O₂ absorber stock · replace vitamins · replace CO alarms (5–7 yrs) · replace polytunnel polythene (5–7 yrs) · refresh seed potatoes with certified stock · battery health check · refresh breeding stock genetics

8. The rotation ledger

Keep one page, on the wall or in the binder, that answers "what expires next?"

ITEM                    QTY     PACKED/BOUGHT   REVIEW BY     ACTION
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Stored water (barrels)  600 L   2026-03         2026-09       Empty/refill
Petrol (jerry cans)     40 L    2026-05         2026-11       Into the car
Bleach                  5 L     2026-02         2027-02       Replace
Vitamins                12 mo   2026-01         2028-01       Replace
Seed bank               —       2026-01         2027-01       Germination test
Filter elements         2       2025-11         2027-11       Replace
Medicine buffer         3 mo    rolling         monthly       Use oldest first
Canning lids            500     2026-04         —             Count after season
Chicken feed            400 kg  2026-10         2027-04       Must last to March
Cover material          250 L   2026-09         —             Top up in autumn
Wood (this winter)      4 m³    seasoned 2025   burn 2026-27  Cut 2027-28's now

Update it every month. This one page is the difference between a system and a pile of stuff.

9. The annual review

Once a year — December is ideal — sit down as a household and do this properly. It takes an afternoon.

  1. Re-run the household audit (worksheets/household-audit.md). What changed? New baby, new condition, new medication, someone moved out, a child now old enough to have real jobs?
  2. Recalculate endurance in days for every category. What is the lowest number? That is next year's priority, regardless of what you feel like buying.
  3. Read the garden notebook. What actually grew well here? What failed twice? Stop growing what fails; plant more of what works.
  4. Read the drill log. What broke? What did we not know how to do?
  5. Review the skills list. What did we learn this year? What's next?
  6. Check the group. Who joined, who left, what gaps opened?
  7. Set next year's targets: one level up in the weakest category, two new skills, one new perennial planting, one new production cycle.
  8. Update this plan and print it again. A plan that doesn't change is a plan nobody is using.

10. Cycles checklist

[ ] A written daily routine is on the wall, and everyone knows their jobs
[ ] Job rotation agreed in writing — nobody silently does all the worst work
[ ] Weekly rhythm established (laundry / bake / maintain / grow / inventory / rest)
[ ] Monthly inventory count and endurance-in-days figure being tracked
[ ] Rotation ledger written and updated monthly
[ ] Garden calendar adapted to OUR frost dates and pinned up
[ ] Livestock breeding calendar written; autumn feed audit scheduled
[ ] Fodder crops in the planting plan, not an afterthought
[ ] Wood cycle is two years ahead
[ ] Seed saved every year; germination tested every year
[ ] Continuous indoor cycles running NOW: sprouts, ferments, compost
[ ] Quarterly drill scheduled in the calendar
[ ] Annual review booked for December
[ ] A rest day every week, defended

🔨 Build it: All build plans — construction plans for everything in this plan, in priority order

13 — Offline, Paper & Keeping the Plan Readable

A survival plan stored on a website you cannot reach, or a phone that is dead, is not a plan. This chapter makes the whole thing work with no internet, no power, and no functioning device.

1. The failure this prevents

Think through the actual sequence. The grid goes down, or the network congests, or your phone dies on day three and the charger is useless. At that exact moment you want to know: how many drops of bleach per litre? what is the oral rehydration recipe? what temperature kills botulism spores? which vent goes high and which goes low?

If the answer lives in a browser tab, a cloud document, a bookmark, or a GitHub repository, you do not have it.

Website        → needs internet + power + a working device
Cloud document → needs internet + an account + power + a device
Phone PDF      → needs power + that specific device
Laptop file    → needs power + that specific device
E-reader       → needs occasional power (weeks per charge)
USB stick      → needs SOME working computer
★ PAPER        → needs daylight, or a candle

Every layer above paper can fail simultaneously, and in a bad winter they will. Paper is the floor you build up from.

2. The rule: three copies, three formats, one off-site

Adapted from the standard backup rule, for a household:

#CopyFormatWhere
1Printed binderPaperOn the shelf, in the house, findable by anyone
2Laminated cardsPaperBy the water store, the stove, the medical kit, the toilet
3E-reader or old phoneDigital, offlineIn the kit, charged, with the solar charger
4USB stick ×2DigitalOne in the document pack, one with a relative in another town
5Repository cloneDigitalOn the family computer, so you can reprint and update

One copy must be off-site. Fire, flood and theft are more likely than any pandemic scenario, and they take the house, the binder and the USB stick in the document drawer together.

3. What to print, in priority order

Printing 5,500 lines is about 150–200 A4 pages double-sided. That is one ream and an afternoon. But if you only print some of it, print it in this order.

Tier 1 — print today, laminate, and post on the wall (8 pages)

worksheets/quick-reference-cards.md — the numbers you need under stress, by torchlight, without reading a chapter: water treatment doses · oral rehydration recipe · bleach dilutions · carbon monoxide rules · when to seek medical help · the trigger ladder · the Amber 72-hour list · the daily routine · your shut-off locations · your contacts.

These ten cards are the single highest-value pages in the entire plan. Print two sets — one for the kitchen, one for the kit.

Tier 2 — the working core (about 60 pages)

  • 01 Water · 04 Heating, cooking & fuel · 05 Sanitation · 06 Health & pandemic protocol
  • worksheets/household-audit.md — print it and fill it in by hand. A filled-in audit is worth more than the whole rest of the binder.
  • worksheets/shopping-lists.md
  • 12 Operating cycles — the daily, weekly and seasonal routines

Tier 3 — the rest of the plan (about 80 pages)

Chapters 00, 02, 03, 07–11, the drill log, and the inventory template.

Tier 4 — the builds (about 50 pages)

builds/. Print the ones you intend to build, when you intend to build them — they are working drawings, so they will get wet, muddy and annotated. Print the chicken coop and the bucket filter regardless; those two get built by most households.

How to print the whole thing in one go

Open offline/survival-plan.html in any browser and press Print. It is formatted for A4: each chapter starts on a new page, tables and diagrams are kept whole, dark backgrounds are dropped, and external web addresses are printed in full after their link text so a paper reader can still see where something came from.

Print double-sided, punch it, and put it in a ring binder with dividers. A ring binder beats a stapled stack: you can reprint one chapter, you can add your own notes, and you can pull out the page you need and take it to the job.

4. The offline bundle

Two files in offline/, both generated from the markdown sources:

FileWhat it isUse it when
survival-plan.htmlThe complete plan as one self-contained page. No network, no fonts, no images, no server, no CDN. Opens from a USB stick by double-clicking. Readable on a phone, works in dark mode, prints properly.Any device with a browser
survival-plan.txtThe complete plan as plain UTF-8 text, wrapped to 78 columns, tables rendered as aligned columns.E-readers, old phones, terminals, anything that chokes on HTML. Prints on anything.

Both are committed to the repository, so downloading the repo as a ZIP from a browser gives you the ready-to-use files with nothing to build or install.

Rebuilding them

If you edit the plan — and you should, it is your plan — regenerate with:

python3 tools/build_offline.py      # or: make offline

The build script uses only the Python standard library: no pip install, no internet, no pandoc, no Node. It runs on any machine with Python 3, today or in ten years. It also self-checks the output and fails if the page would try to fetch anything at all from the network.

make offline    build both files
make check      verify the build fetches nothing, without writing
make links      verify every internal link and anchor resolves
make print      print instructions
make clean      remove generated files

5. Devices that actually work when the power is uncertain

DeviceBattery lifeVerdict
E-reader (e-ink)Weeks per charge, ~1 Wh/day⭐ The best offline library there is. Holds thousands of books, sips power, readable in bright sun, charges from a tiny solar panel. Load it with this plan plus the reference library below.
Old phone, wiped, airplane modeDays, as a dedicated reader⭐ Excellent second copy. No SIM, no apps, WiFi off, screen dim. It becomes a library, not a phone.
Current phone1–2 daysFine, but it is doing ten other jobs and it will be flat
Tablet1–2 daysGood screen for build drawings; power-hungry
LaptopHoursOnly for editing and printing, not for reading in a crisis
PaperInfinite⭐ The one that cannot fail

Charging all of these is covered by the P0 tier in Chapter 02 — a 20 W folding panel and a power bank, about €150, which is the cheapest meaningful preparation in the whole plan.

Honest note on digital media longevity

  • USB flash and SD cards: reliable for a few years, then increasingly not. Unpowered charge leakage is real. Copy the data to a fresh stick every 2 years, keep two sticks, and never treat one as an archive.
  • Hard drives: fine while used, unreliable after years on a shelf.
  • Optical discs: ordinary burned DVDs degrade in 5–10 years; archival M-DISC is rated far longer but you need a drive to read it.
  • Paper in a dry box: decades to centuries, readable by anyone, no format obsolescence, no reader required.

Conclusion: paper is the archive. Digital is the convenience. Keep both, and refresh the digital copies on the annual review (Chapter 12 §9).

6. The offline reference library — download and print BEFORE you need it

This plan is deliberately self-contained: everything you need to act is in these pages. But a few outside references are genuinely worth having, and every one of them must be obtained while you still have a connection.

Get these now

ReferenceWhat it gives youNote
Where There Is No Doctor (Hesperian)Diagnosis and treatment when no professional is available. Written for exactly this situation.Free digital editions from the publisher. Print it, or at least the chapters on dehydration, wounds, infection and childbirth.
Where There Is No Dentist (Hesperian)Toothache, abscess, temporary fillings, extractionFree digital editions. Toothache with no dentist is one of the most demoralising things in a long crisis.
Tested home-canning times and pressures (a national food-preservation extension service, or a current edition preserving book)⚠️ Safety-critical. Pressure and time for every low-acid food.Print the tables. Do not improvise, and do not rely on family memory — this is the one place in the plan where getting it wrong is fatal.
A regional vegetable growing guide and a seed-saving guideSowing dates, spacings, isolation distances for your climateLocal beats generic
A foraging guide with good photographs, for your regionWild food and medicine⚠️ Photographs matter. Never eat anything you have not positively identified.
Paper maps — road and topographic, your regionNavigation with no GPSPlus a compass, and know how to use them
A first aid manualThe basics, on paperTake the course too; the book is the reminder
Repair referencesBicycle, small engine, plumbing, electrics, sewingWhatever matches the things you own
Offline Wikipedia (e.g. a Kiwix .zim archive)An enormous general reference, fully offlineA medical or general subset fits on a phone; the full thing needs ~100 GB
Offline maps on the phoneDownloaded map regions that work in airplane modeDownload your region and the surrounding ones
Seed catalogue, tool manuals, appliance manualsPart numbers, specificationsSave the PDFs, print the critical pages

The rule

Anything that exists only as a bookmark does not exist. Download it, print the parts you would need in the dark, and put the files on the e-reader and both USB sticks.

Do a download day once a year, at the annual review. It takes two hours and it is the cheapest preparation on the list.

7. Make it findable and readable by someone else

This is the single-point-of-failure rule applied to the plan itself. If only you can find it and only you understand it, then you getting ill — which is exactly what a pandemic does — takes the whole household's plan out with you.

[ ] The binder lives in ONE obvious, named place. Everyone knows where.
[ ] It is labelled on the spine, in plain language, not "prep stuff".
[ ] The quick-reference cards are ON THE WALL, not in the binder.
[ ] The household audit is filled in, in pen, at the front.
[ ] The system notes are written: how the power system starts, where the
    stopcock is, how the filter is backflushed, what the alarms mean.
[ ] At least two people have read the binder and run a drill from it.
[ ] The contacts card is filled in, including the out-of-area contact.
[ ] A relative in another town has a copy.

Write in the binder. Annotate it, correct it, add your own frost dates, your own well depth, your own meter readings. A marked-up binder is a working document; a pristine one has never been used.

8. Keeping it current

On the annual review (Chapter 12 §9):

  1. Re-run the household audit — people, medicines and endurance all change.
  2. Update the plan with what you learned from the year's drills.
  3. make offline to regenerate the HTML and text files.
  4. Reprint the chapters that changed and swap the pages in the binder.
  5. Refresh both USB sticks onto new sticks; post one to the off-site holder.
  6. Re-sync the e-reader.
  7. Do the download day (§6).
  8. Check the printed cards are still legible and still on the wall.

9. Offline checklist

[ ] offline/survival-plan.html opens with the network switched off
[ ] It prints correctly to A4 (chapters start on new pages)
[ ] Tier 1 quick-reference cards PRINTED and LAMINATED
[ ] Cards posted: kitchen, water store, stove, medical kit, toilet
[ ] Full plan printed and in a labelled ring binder
[ ] Household audit filled in BY HAND at the front of the binder
[ ] System notes written (power, water, stove, shut-offs)
[ ] E-reader or wiped old phone loaded, charged, in the kit
[ ] Solar panel + power bank that can charge it
[ ] 2 × USB sticks written; one held off-site by a relative
[ ] Repository cloned to the family computer so it can be reprinted
[ ] Reference library downloaded AND the critical parts printed
[ ] Canning time/pressure tables printed (safety-critical)
[ ] Paper maps of the region + a compass
[ ] Two people have read the binder and run a drill from it
[ ] Annual refresh scheduled in the December review

Before you start: the fundamentals

The tool kit that builds everything here

Absolute minimum (€120): tape measure · pencil · combination square · handsaw · hammer · cordless drill/driver + bits · adjustable spanner · utility knife · tin snips · 2 clamps · spirit level · safety glasses · work gloves.

Makes it much faster (€200 more): circular saw or mitre saw · jigsaw · impact driver · staple gun · long spirit level (1.2 m) · workbench or two trestles · a chalk line · a spade and a shovel.

Chapter 11 covers the full tool and spares list.

Timber sizes used in these plans

European metric sawn timber. If yours differs, adapt — none of these designs are fussy about a few millimetres.

Name hereActual sizeUsed for
45 × 4545 × 45 mm (2×2)Frames, studs, coop structure, small builds
45 × 9545 × 95 mm (2×4)Load-bearing frames, bed edges, stands, run posts
70 × 7070 × 70 mm (3×3)Legs, posts
22 × 10022 × 100 mm boardCladding, bed edges, shelving
Batten25 × 38 mmRoofing battens, glazing bars, trim
Sheet2440 × 1220 mmOSB3, exterior ply, MDF

Sheet goods: OSB3 (moisture-resistant, cheap, ugly) for anything that will be painted or covered · exterior/marine plywood for floors, doors and anything that gets wet or scrubbed · 18 mm for structure, 12 mm for panels, 6 mm for linings.

Timber preservation and food safety

  • Never use CCA-treated, creosoted or old railway-sleeper timber where animals live, where food grows, or where water is collected. The preservatives leach.
  • Modern tanalised/pressure-treated (ACQ, copper azole) timber is generally considered safe for raised beds and animal housing, but line the inside face of raised beds with plastic sheeting if you want to be conservative.
  • Best untreated options that last outdoors: larch, Douglas fir, sweet chestnut, oak, western red cedar, black locust. Larch is usually the cheapest durable option in Central Europe and needs no treatment at all.
  • Raw linseed oil (2–3 coats, thinned) is a non-toxic finish for coops, beds and tool handles.
  • Keep wood off the ground. A post in soil rots in 5–8 years; the same post on a concrete pad or a metal post shoe lasts 25.

Fixings

  • Screws, not nails, for almost everything — they can be undone, they hold better, and you can build alone. Exterior-grade (coated or stainless) outdoors.
  • Rule of thumb: the screw should go at least twice as deep into the second piece as the first piece is thick.
  • Pre-drill near ends to stop splitting (a pilot hole ≈ the screw's core diameter).
  • Galvanised or stainless outdoors. Plain steel bleeds rust stains within a season and fails within five years.
  • Structural connections: use a bolt (M8/M10 with washers), not a screw.
  • Coach screws for post-to-rail, angle brackets for anything you'd otherwise butt-joint badly.

Mesh — the one you must not get wrong

MeshUse⚠️
12.5 mm (½") galvanised weld mesh, 19 gEverything predator-related: coop vents, run walls, aprons, feed storesThe correct answer
25 mm (1") weld meshRun tops, upper sections, rabbit runsAcceptable above 1.2 m
Chicken wire (hexagonal netting)Keeping chickens in. Nothing else.❌ A fox tears through it and a marten walks through it. Never use it as predator protection.
Insect mesh (0.6–1 mm)Brassica cages, window screens
Bird nettingFruit cages, run tops against hawks
Hardware cloth 6 mmRodent-proofing feed stores, vents

Safety

  • Eye protection whenever cutting, drilling or hammering. Every time.
  • Dust mask (FFP2) when cutting OSB, MDF or treated timber — the dust is genuinely harmful.
  • Never cut towards yourself. Clamp the work; don't hold it.
  • Ladders: 1-in-4 angle, three points of contact, someone footing it.
  • Chainsaw: full PPE and training, never alone, never above shoulder height.
  • Anything burning: read the CO warning in Chapter 04 §6. It applies to every stove in these plans.
  • Anything electrical touching house wiring: a qualified electrician. Not you.
  • Anything holding water above head height: 1,000 L weighs 1,000 kg. Build the stand to hold three times what you think it needs.
  • Excavations over 1.2 m deep can collapse and kill. Batter the sides or shore them. This is the most dangerous build in this whole folder.

Adapt, scavenge, substitute

Almost everything here can be built from salvage: pallets (heat-treated "HT" stamped only — never MB/methyl bromide), old windows, scaffold boards, IBC totes, blue barrels, bread crates, scrap timber, offcuts from a building site, a demolished shed. The dimensions in these plans are a starting point, not a law. Build to the materials you can actually get.

Keep every offcut. In a crisis the timber pile is a resource.

Back to: main README

↑ Contents

01 — Water Builds

Read Chapter 01 first. The three layers are stored water, a source you control, and treatment. These builds cover all three.

Contents: Bucket gravity filter · Tippy-tap · First-flush diverter · IBC stand & manifold · Slow sand filter · Solar water heater · Ollas & bucket drip

1. Bucket gravity filter

30 min · €50 · Skill ⬤○○ · Build this first. Today if possible.

Two stacked buckets and a hollow-fibre filter give you a continuous, hands-free supply of safe water at 1–2 litres a minute, with no power and no pumping. It does the same job as a €300 commercial gravity filter for a sixth of the price.

        ┌───────────────────┐ ← lid (keeps dust and insects out)
        │  ░░░ RAW WATER ░░ │
        │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │  TOP BUCKET — 20 L, drilled for the filter
        └────────╥──────────┘
                 ║ ← Sawyer/hollow-fibre filter, gasket + washer both sides
        ┌────────╨──────────┐
        │                   │
        │   CLEAN WATER     │  BOTTOM BUCKET — 20 L, with a tap 50 mm up
        │                   │
        └──────────────[TAP]┘
             ═══════════════   stand on a stable shelf at working height

Materials

ItemQty~€
Food-grade 20 L buckets with lids216
Sawyer-type hollow-fibre filter (0.1 µm) with gaskets1–230
Plastic bucket tap (spigot) + washers16
Silicone sealant (food-safe)—4

Build

  1. Drill a hole in the centre of the base of the top bucket to suit your filter's threaded fitting (usually 19–20 mm).
  2. Drill a matching hole in the lid of the bottom bucket — or omit the lid and let the filter hang straight into the bottom bucket.
  3. Fit the filter through both, with a rubber gasket and washer on each side, and tighten the nut hand-tight plus a quarter turn. Do not over-tighten — cracked plastic is the usual failure.
  4. Fit the tap into the bottom bucket, 50 mm up from the base, with washers inside and out. The gap leaves any sediment behind.
  5. Run a bead of silicone around both fittings; let it cure 24 h.
  6. Test with clean water first, and look for drips. Any leak at the filter seal means untreated water is bypassing the filter.

Use it

  • Two filters double the flow rate. For a family of four, fit two.
  • Pre-filter cloudy water through 4–8 layers of cotton, or settle it first — silt clogs the fibres fast.
  • Backflush weekly with the syringe that comes with the filter, and whenever flow drops.
  • ⚠️ Filtration is not disinfection. Hollow-fibre removes bacteria and protozoa but not viruses. After filtering, add a NaDCC tablet or 2 drops of bleach per litre, or boil, if the source was surface water. From your own rainwater tank, filter + chlorine residual is the sensible standard.
  • ⚠️ Never let the filter freeze. Freezing ruptures the fibres invisibly and it will then pass everything without any change you can see. Bring it indoors in winter.

2. Tippy-tap handwashing station

20 min · €5 · Skill ⬤○○ · Highest disease-prevention per euro in the whole folder

Handwashing with soap cuts diarrhoeal disease by 30–50%. A tippy-tap uses 0.2 litres per wash, and — the important part — you never touch it with dirty hands.

        ┌───────┐          Two uprights + a crossbar
       ╱│ 5 L   │╲         (or hang it from a branch or a nail)
      ╱ │  jug  │ ╲
     │  └───┬───┘  │        Jug pivots on a string through two
     │      │      │        holes near the neck
     │     ╱ ╲     │
     │    ╱   ╲    │  ← 3 mm hole in the cap or upper shoulder
     │   soap on   │    lets water out ONLY when tilted
     │   a string  │
     │      │      │
     │   ───┴───   │  ← foot lever, string to the jug's handle
    ═╧═════════════╧═
      gravel or a soakaway below, or a bucket to catch greywater

Build

  1. Take a clean 5 L jug or bottle with a handle.
  2. Drill a 3 mm hole in the cap (or in the shoulder, 30 mm below the neck).
  3. Drill two small holes either side of the neck and thread a string through as a pivot; hang it from a crossbar so it swings freely.
  4. Tie a second string from the handle down to a foot lever (a stick pivoted on a peg) or simply a loop you pull with your foot.
  5. Hang a bar of soap in a net or on a nail beside it — soap is what does the work; running water alone does very little.
  6. Put gravel or a bucket underneath. Catch the greywater for the garden.

Site one at the toilet and one at the kitchen. Refill them as a daily job. In winter, bring the jug indoors at night or it splits.

3. First-flush diverter

1 h · €25 · Skill ⬤○○ · Do more for water quality than any filter

The first rain off a roof carries the bird droppings, dust, pollen, moss and insects that accumulated since the last shower. A first-flush diverter throws that away and sends only the clean water to the tank.

             roof gutter
                 │
                 ▼
            ┌────┴────┐
            │  tee    ├──────────► to TANK (once the chamber is full)
            └────┬────┘
                 │
            ┌────┴────┐  ← ball floats up as the chamber fills
            │    ●    │     and seals the tee, diverting flow onward
            │         │
            │ 110 mm  │     CHAMBER — volume = roof area × 0.5 L/m²
            │  pipe   │
            │         │     110 mm pipe holds ~8.5 L per metre
            │         │     160 mm pipe holds ~18 L per metre
            └────┬────┘
              ┌──┴──┐  ← end cap with a 3 mm hole:
              │  •  │     drains slowly between rain events so the
              └─────┘     chamber is empty and ready for the next shower

Sizing. Aim for 0.5 litres per m² of roof area:

Roof areaFirst flush volume110 mm pipe160 mm pipe
40 m²20 L2.4 m1.1 m
60 m²30 L3.5 m1.7 m
100 m²50 L5.9 m2.8 m

Most DIY diverters are undersized because a 6 m pipe is impractical. An undersized diverter is still far better than none — 20 L takes the worst of it. Use 160 mm pipe, or split the roof across two downpipes with a diverter on each, or accept a smaller chamber and clean your gutters more often.

Materials: 110 or 160 mm soil pipe to the length above · a tee · an end cap · 2 pipe clips · a floating ball that fits the pipe loosely (a hard plastic ball, or a sealed empty bottle) · solvent cement or rubber seals.

Build

  1. Cut the downpipe and fit the tee so the side outlet goes to the tank and the straight run goes down into the chamber.
  2. Glue the chamber pipe below the tee, clipped to the wall.
  3. Drop the ball in. As the chamber fills, the ball rises and seals the opening, so further water passes out of the side outlet to the tank.
  4. Fit the end cap and drill a 3 mm hole in it. The chamber empties over a few hours, ready for the next shower.
  5. Add a leaf screen / 1 mm mesh basket at the gutter outlet above the tee. It keeps out leaves, and — critically — mosquitoes and rodents, which is a disease issue, not a tidiness one.

Maintenance: clear the mesh monthly and after storms. Flush the chamber and check the drain hole twice a year. Disconnect and drain it before a hard frost.

4. IBC tank stand and linked manifold

4 h · €80 · Skill ⬤⬤○

⚠️ A full 1,000 L IBC weighs 1,000 kg. Build the stand for three times that. A collapsing tank will destroy a wall, a car or a person.

    TWO LINKED IBCs, plan view                SIDE VIEW
   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐              ┌────────────┐
   │  IBC 1   │   │  IBC 2   │              │    IBC     │
   │  1000 L  │   │  1000 L  │              │            │
   └────╥─────┘   └─────╥────┘              └──╥─────────┘
        ╚════ 50 mm ════╝  ← linked at the      ║ outlet
             manifold        BASE: they fill  ══╬══════  ← 45 × 95 bearers
                             and draw as one   ███  ███  ← concrete blocks
                  │                            ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓  ← compacted hardcore
              ┌───┴───┐                                     on level ground
              │  TAP  │ ← 500–600 mm above ground
              └───────┘   gives enough head for a hose and to fill a can

Source the tanks carefully. Buy only food-grade IBCs that previously held food (syrup, juice, glycerine). Ask what was in it and do not accept "not sure". A tank that held a chemical can never be made safe for drinking water. Rinse thoroughly before use regardless.

Build the stand

  1. Level and compact the ground. Everything depends on this. Dig out 150 mm, fill with hardcore/gravel, compact it hard, and check level in both directions.
  2. Lay concrete blocks on edge (or paving slabs on a full bed) to 400–500 mm high, supporting the full perimeter of the IBC pallet base, not just the corners.
  3. Lay 45 × 95 bearers across the blocks, running the same way as the pallet's own bearers, so the load goes straight down into the blocks.
  4. Check level again with the tank empty, and again after the first fill.

Link the tanks

  1. Connect the bottom outlets together with 50 mm pipe and the standard IBC camlock/S60x6 adaptors (cheap, available online). Linked at the base, the tanks equalise — they fill and empty together and you draw from one tap.
  2. Link the tops too with a short overflow pipe, and take a final overflow away from the house foundations to a soakaway, a ditch or a pond.
  3. Fit an isolating valve at each tank so you can take one offline to clean it.

Finish

  1. Block the light — paint the tanks, wrap them in tarpaulin, or build a simple timber surround. Sunlight grows algae, and algae fouls filters and makes the water taste foul.
  2. Fit a tap 100 mm above the tank floor so sediment stays behind, and a separate bottom drain to flush the sludge out twice a year.
  3. Winter: drain the pipework and the tap, leave the tanks no more than ¾ full, or lag them. Water expands as it freezes and will split a tank. Partially burying a tank, or surrounding it with straw bales, keeps it liquid.

Insulating one tank (straw bales or 50 mm foam board in a timber box) means you still have liquid water in January, which is when you need it most.

5. Slow sand (biosand) filter

4 h to build + 2–3 weeks to mature · €40 · Skill ⬤⬤○

A genuinely effective, permanent, zero-consumable filter. Once the biolayer (a living film of micro-organisms on the sand surface) has matured, it removes 95–99% of bacteria, over 99% of protozoa, and most turbidity — indefinitely, with no cartridges to buy. This is a real L3 renewable.

        ┌─────────────────────────┐
        │   ▓▓ diffuser plate ▓▓  │ ← drilled plate, 50 mm above the sand:
        │═════════════════════════│   pouring water straight onto the sand
        │  ~~ 50 mm standing ~~   │   destroys the biolayer
        │  ~~~~~ water ~~~~~~~~~  │
        │▪▪▪▪ BIOLAYER ▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▪ │ ← the living filter, top 10 mm
        │░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
        │░ FILTRATION SAND        │   500 mm of 0.15–0.7 mm washed sand
        │░ 500 mm                 │
        │░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
        │▒▒ coarse sand 50 mm ▒▒▒ │   1–6 mm
        │●● drainage gravel 50 ●● │   6–12 mm
        └──┬──────────────────────┘
           │ outlet pipe rises from the base...
           └────────────┐
                        └──► outlet at the level of the standing water
                             (this is what keeps 50 mm of water permanently
                              above the sand, keeping the biolayer alive)

Materials: a 60–120 L food-grade barrel or a purpose-built concrete body · 20 mm pipe and elbows · washed sand, graded · gravel · a drilled plastic plate for the diffuser.

Build

  1. Fit the outlet pipe: from the very bottom of the barrel, rising outside (or inside) to discharge at a height that leaves 50 mm of water standing above the sand surface. Getting this height right is the whole design — it is what keeps the biolayer permanently wet.
  2. Wash the sand until the rinse water runs completely clear. This takes longer than you think — an hour of work, several changes of water. Unwashed sand clogs the filter and it never recovers. Do not skip it.
  3. Layer in: 50 mm of 6–12 mm gravel, 50 mm of 1–6 mm coarse sand, then 500 mm of 0.15–0.7 mm filtration sand.
  4. Fit the diffuser plate 50 mm above the sand.
  5. Charge it daily with water for 2–3 weeks before trusting it. The biolayer has to establish. Flow will slow as it matures — that is the filter working.

Operate it

  • Pour a batch (10–20 L) through, once or twice a day. It is a batch filter, not a continuous one. The pause between batches is when the biolayer feeds.
  • Flow rate of roughly 0.4–0.6 L/min is right.
  • ⚠️ Never let it dry out. A dry biolayer is a dead biolayer and you start the three weeks again.
  • ⚠️ Never let it freeze.
  • When flow drops too far, do the "swirl and dump": stir the top 20 mm of sand gently, let the dirt suspend, scoop the dirty water out. Expect 1–2 weeks of reduced performance while the biolayer recovers.
  • ⚠️ Still disinfect afterwards. A biosand filter is excellent but it is not a virus barrier. Chlorinate or boil the output for drinking.

6. Solar batch water heater

1 day · €60 · Skill ⬤⬤○ · Free hot water, April–September

6.1 The simple version — solar shower bag

A 20 L black PVC bag (€15) hung on a sunny south wall reaches 40 °C in 3–4 hours on a clear day. Zero build. Buy two. This is the sensible starting point and it is genuinely enough for washing.

6.2 The batch heater — 100–200 L of hot water

             ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
       glass │  ╔════════════════════════════════╗  │ ← old window or
        lid  │  ║   BLACK-PAINTED TANK / BARREL  ║  │   twin-wall polycarbonate
       (30°) │  ╚════════════════════════════════╝  │
             │  ▓▓▓ 50 mm insulation all round ▓▓▓  │ ← except the glazed face
             └──────────────────────────────────────┘
              cold in at the BOTTOM · hot out at the TOP
              (hot water rises — never plumb it the other way)

Build: a plywood box with 50 mm of insulation on the base and all sides, a barrel or steel tank painted matt black inside it, and an old double-glazed window or twin-wall polycarbonate as the lid, tilted to face the sun at about your latitude. Line the inside of the box with foil or white paint to bounce light back onto the tank.

Plumb it: cold feed into the bottom, hot draw-off from the top. On a clear Central European summer day expect 45–60 °C; in April and September, 30–40 °C — still ample for washing, dishes and laundry.

⚠️ Drain it completely before the first frost. A frozen batch heater splits the tank and the pipework. ⚠️ Never seal it as a pressure vessel. Always leave a vent or an open feed. Heated water expands, and a sealed tank is a bomb.

7. Ollas and bucket drip irrigation

1 h · €15 · Skill ⬤○○ · Cuts garden watering by 50–70%

7.1 Ollas — buried clay pots

Ancient, silent, and the most water-efficient irrigation there is. Water seeps through the unglazed clay directly to the roots as the soil dries, with almost zero evaporation.

Build: take two unglazed terracotta pots, plug the drainage hole of the lower one with silicone or a cork, and glue the two rim-to-rim with silicone to make a sealed vessel with a filling neck at the top. Or simply use a single large unglazed pot with the hole plugged.

Use: bury to the neck, leaving 30 mm proud, between plants. One 3–4 L olla waters roughly 1 m². Fill weekly, cover with a stone or a saucer to stop evaporation and mosquitoes. Lift them before winter or they crack.

7.2 Bucket drip line

Build: a 20 L bucket on a stand 500 mm above bed level, with a tap at the base feeding 16 mm poly tube laid along the bed, with 4 mm drippers or simply a 1 mm hole every 300 mm. Gravity does the rest.

Details: a fine mesh or a piece of cloth over the bucket outlet stops the holes blocking · run it under mulch for maximum effect · 500 mm of head is enough for a soaker line but not a sprinkler · connect it to the IBC manifold above for a garden-wide system. Feeding it with comfrey or nettle tea (strained!) makes it a fertigation system too.

The rule from Chapter 07: mulch saves more water than any irrigation system. Do the mulching first, then add drip for what mulch alone cannot cover.

Related: Chapter 01 — Water · Sanitation builds · Build index

02 — Power Builds

Read Chapter 02 first — especially the load audit. Never size a system before you have measured your loads. These builds assume you have done that and know your Wh/day figure.

Contents: 12 V solar system · Cable sizing · Fusing · DC lighting circuit · Battery box · Panel tilt frame · Generator noise screen

⚠️ The line you must not cross. Everything in this file is extra-low-voltage DC, isolated from your house wiring. That is legal, safe and DIY-able everywhere. Anything that connects to your mains wiring — a transfer switch, an interlock, a hybrid inverter feeding house circuits — must be done by a qualified electrician. Not because of bureaucracy: a backfed generator kills line workers, and a badly earthed inverter kills you.

1. The 12 V solar system — complete build

1 day · €400–1,200 depending on size · Skill ⬤⬤○

This is the P1 "Essentials" system from Chapter 02: lights, phones, laptop, radio, and a fridge if you size it up. It is the system every household should have, and it is genuinely a one-day job.

1.1 The wiring diagram

  ┌────────────┐
  │   PANELS   │  400–600 W, wired in SERIES for MPPT
  │ ▒▒▒▒ ▒▒▒▒  │  (higher voltage = thinner cable, better dull-day harvest)
  └─────╥──────┘
        ║  PV cable (4 or 6 mm², MC4 connectors)
   ┌────╨────┐
   │ PV DC   │ ← isolator switch: turn the array off before touching anything
   │ ISOLATOR│
   └────╥────┘
   ┌────╨─────────┐
   │ MPPT CHARGE  │  30–50 A. Buy a SPARE — this is the most likely failure.
   │  CONTROLLER  │
   └──╥────────╥──┘
      ║        ║
      ║   ┌────╨─────────────────────────────────┐
      ║   │  ⚡ MAIN FUSE — within 300 mm of the │
      ║   │     battery POSITIVE terminal        │
      ║   └────╥─────────────────────────────────┘
      ║   ┌────╨────┐
      ╚═══╡ BATTERY │  100–200 Ah LiFePO₄ (1.2–2.4 kWh)
          │  12 V   │  with an integrated BMS
          └────╥────┘
       ┌───────╨────────┐
       │  BATTERY       │ ← main isolator switch you can reach IN THE DARK
       │  ISOLATOR      │
       └───┬────────┬───┘
           │        │
    ┌──────┴──┐  ┌──┴──────────┐
    │ DC FUSE │  │ ⚡fuse      │
    │  BLOCK  │  │  INVERTER   │ 1000–1500 W pure sine
    └──┬───┬──┘  │  (switch it │ ⚠️ modified sine damages motors,
       │   │     │   OFF when  │    chargers and medical devices
       │   │     │   not used) │
       │   │     └──────╥──────┘
       │   │            ║
   lights USB      230 V AC sockets (STANDALONE — not wired to the house)

1.2 Parts list (a 600 W / 1.2 kWh example)

ItemSpec~€
Solar panels2 × 300 W mono, wired in series220
Mounting rails / framesee §480
PV cable + MC4 connectors6 mm², 20 m + 4 pairs45
MPPT charge controller30 A, 12/24 V auto110
Spare MPPT controllersame110
PV DC isolator600 V, 25 A20
LiFePO₄ battery12 V 100 Ah with BMS320
Main fuse + holder125 A Class T or MEGA25
Battery isolator switch300 A marine type20
Battery cable25 mm², lugs, heat shrink40
DC fuse block6-way, blade fuses15
Pure sine inverter1000 W, 12 V130
Cable, crimps, glands, trunking, labels—60
Battery monitor (shunt-based)optional but excellent60
Total~€1,255

Halve this for a 300 W / 50 Ah starter system (~€500), or scale up to 24 V for anything over 1.5 kW of load.

1.3 Cable sizing — the table that prevents fires

Undersized DC cable is the classic off-grid fire cause. At 12 V the currents are large and the voltage drop is brutal. Size for both voltage drop and current-carrying capacity, and use whichever is bigger.

Minimum cable size (mm²) for 12 V, 3% voltage drop, for the ONE-WAY run length:

Current1 m2 m3 m5 m8 m
5 A1.51.51.52.54
10 A1.52.54610
20 A2.5461016
30 A46101625
50 A1010162550
100 A2525355095

(Values in bold are set by ampacity, not voltage drop.)

Rules:

  • At 24 V you can use half the area for the same power; at 48 V, a quarter. This is the main reason to go to a higher voltage as systems grow.
  • Keep the battery-to-inverter run as short as physically possible. Under 1.5 m, ideally under 1 m.
  • Use flexible fine-strand cable (welding or marine cable), not solid house wire — it survives vibration and tight bends.
  • Crimp lugs properly with a proper crimping tool, then heat-shrink. A bad crimp is a resistance heater. Do not twist-and-tape anything on the DC side.
  • Colour code red positive / black negative and label every cable at both ends.

1.4 Fusing — non-negotiable

A fuse protects the CABLE, not the device. Its job is to blow before the wire melts.

LocationFuseWhy
Within 300 mm of the battery positive100–150 A Class T or MEGA⚠️ The most important fuse in the system. A shorted battery cable delivers thousands of amps and will start a fire in seconds. Class T has the high interrupt rating lithium needs.
Inverter feedSized to the inverter1000 W ÷ (12 V × 0.85) ≈ 98 A → 125 A fuse, 25 mm² cable
Each DC branch circuit5–20 A blade fuseLights, USB, pumps, fridge
PV array to controllerDC isolator + controller's own protectionFit the isolator so you can safely work on the system

Sizing a fuse: rate it at roughly 125% of the expected continuous current, and never above the cable's ampacity.

1.5 Build sequence

  1. Plan the layout on paper first. Battery, controller and inverter close together; panels as far away as necessary. Draw it, label it, keep the drawing.
  2. Mount the panels (§4). Wire them in series for an MPPT controller — higher voltage means thinner cable and better output in dull conditions. Check the controller's maximum PV voltage and remember open-circuit voltage rises in the cold: allow 25% headroom.
  3. Run the PV cable into the building through a proper gland or a drip loop, with the DC isolator at the entry point.
  4. Mount the controller on a non-combustible surface with 100 mm of clear air all round (they get hot).
  5. Connect in this order — it matters: `` BATTERY first → then the CONTROLLER → then the PANELS → then the LOADS `` Most MPPT controllers need to see the battery voltage first to detect the system voltage. Connecting panels first can damage them.
  6. Fit the main fuse and the isolator as you connect the battery, not afterwards.
  7. Set the controller to the right battery chemistry — LiFePO₄, not "lithium" generically, and definitely not the lead-acid default. Wrong charge profile is the fastest way to kill a €320 battery.
  8. Wire the DC fuse block off the isolator, and run the lighting and USB circuits from it (§2).
  9. Mount the inverter on its own short heavy cable with its own fuse. Put it on a switched circuit — idle draw of 10–30 W is 240–700 Wh/day wasted on nothing.
  10. Label everything, pin the diagram on the wall beside the equipment, and write the operating notes — how to check state of charge, what the alarms mean, what to do if it stops. Chapter 11's single-point-of-failure rule applies: someone else must be able to run this.
  11. Test under load. Run the fridge off it for 48 hours and watch the numbers before you trust it.

1.6 Common mistakes

❌ Cable too thin                     → voltage drop, heat, fire
❌ No main battery fuse               → the one that burns houses down
❌ Modified sine inverter             → damages motors, chargers, medical devices
❌ Inverter left on 24/7              → 240–700 Wh/day of pure waste
❌ Battery in an unheated garage      → LiFePO₄ refuses to charge below 0 °C
❌ Controller set to the wrong chemistry → a dead battery in months
❌ Sized on annual average sun        → works in June, fails in December
❌ Shading "only a little"            → one shaded cell can halve a string
❌ No spare charge controller         → €60 part strands a €1,200 system
❌ Only one person understands it     → they get ill, the house goes dark

2. 12 V DC lighting and USB circuit

2 h · €40 · Skill ⬤○○ · Do this even on the smallest system

Running lights and charging directly from the battery at 12 V avoids the inverter entirely — which saves the 10–15% conversion loss and, far more importantly, lets you switch the inverter off and stop its idle drain.

Materials: 12 V LED strip (warm white, 4.8–9 W/m) or 12 V LED bulbs with fittings · 12 V USB charging sockets (dual, 3 A) · 12 V cigarette sockets for accessories · switches · 1.5–2.5 mm² twin cable · blade fuses.

Build

  1. Run a 2.5 mm² pair from the DC fuse block to each room.
  2. Lights: LED strip along a ceiling cornice or under a shelf, on a switch. 5 W lights a room adequately; 10 W lights it well.
  3. USB points: one per room, plus two at the "charging station" where phones, headlamps, radios and battery packs live.
  4. Fuse each circuit at the block (5 A for lights, 10 A for USB/accessories).
  5. Keep polarity consistent everywhere — reversed polarity destroys most 12 V devices instantly.

Energy reality: a whole house on 12 V LED is 30–60 Wh per evening. Your phone is 15 Wh. You can light and communicate for weeks on a 1 kWh battery. This is why the P0 tier removes the panic from a blackout for €200.

3. Insulated battery box

2 h · €50 · Skill ⬤○○

LiFePO₄ must not be charged below 0 °C — the BMS will simply refuse, and you will think the system is broken. In a Central European winter an outbuilding regularly sits below freezing.

        ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
        │ ░░░ 50 mm insulation, lid ░░░░░░░░░ │
        ├─────────────────────────────────────┤
        │ ░│                             │░   │
        │ ░│      BATTERY                │░   │  Cable exits through a
        │ ░│                             │░   │  sealed gland, LOW down
        │ ░│                             │░─────►
        │ ░└─────────────────────────────┘░   │
        │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │
        └─────────────────────────────────────┘
          18 mm ply box · 50 mm PIR/foam lining · battery strapped down

Build: an 18 mm ply box, 50 mm larger than the battery all round, lined with 50 mm PIR foam board (taped joints), with a lifting lid. Strap the battery down so it cannot move. Take the cable out through a cable gland at low level and seal it.

Details:

  • LiFePO₄: seal it up. The battery's own charge/discharge losses keep it a few degrees above ambient inside an insulated box. Add a thermostatically controlled 10–20 W heat pad for a cold shed (that is 240–480 Wh/day — budget for it, or just keep the battery indoors, which is free).
  • ⚠️ Lead-acid: NEVER seal it. Flooded and AGM batteries vent hydrogen, which is explosive. Vent the box to the outside at the top, and keep all sparks and switches outside the box.
  • Put a thermometer inside and check it on the coldest nights.
  • Best answer of all: put the battery inside the heated envelope of the house. LiFePO₄ is safe indoors and it needs no ventilation. This costs nothing.

4. Adjustable panel tilt frame

4 h · €80 · Skill ⬤⬤○ · Gains 20–30% in December

At 46 °N, panels at a fixed 35° lose a lot in winter. A frame you can re-tilt twice a year to latitude + 15° in winter (≈ 60°) and latitude − 10° in summer (≈ 35°) gains 20–30% in the months that matter — and a steep winter angle sheds snow, which is worth more than the angle itself.

    SUMMER ~35°                  WINTER ~60°
         ╱▒▒▒▒▒▒▒                    ╱▒
       ╱▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒                   ╱▒▒
     ╱▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒                   ╱▒▒▒
   ╱─────○────                 ╱──○         ○ = pivot bolt (M10) at the
  ╱  strut in   ╲             ╱ strut in        bottom rail
 ╱   hole A      ╲           ╱  hole B
═╧════════════════╧═════════╧═══════════   base frame, weighted or anchored

Materials: 45 × 95 treated timber or 40 × 40 steel angle · M10 bolts and washers · panel clamps or 45 × 45 rails · ground anchors, or concrete blocks as ballast.

Build: a rectangular base frame sized to the panels · a hinged panel frame bolted to the base at the front edge with M10 pivot bolts · a rear strut with two or three drilled holes for the seasonal angles.

Critical details:

  • ⚠️ Wind load is the killer. A 2 m² panel at 60° in an 80 km/h gust carries a load of several hundred kilos. Anchor the frame to the ground or ballast it with 80–150 kg of concrete blocks. Panels that fly are lethal and they take the roof with them.
  • Keep the bottom edge at least 300 mm above ground/snow level or winter drifts bury the array exactly when you need it.
  • Leave 100 mm of air behind the panels — they lose about 0.4%/°C of output as they heat, so ventilation matters in summer.
  • Survey the winter sun path before siting. The sun is very low from November to February; trees and buildings that cast no shadow in June will shade you completely in December.
  • Ground mounting beats roof mounting for anyone who can: you can clean it, clear snow, re-tilt it, and repair it without a ladder.

5. Generator noise screen

4 h · €100 · Skill ⬤⬤○

A generator is audible for hundreds of metres and announces that your house has fuel and things worth powering (Chapter 10). You can cut that substantially — but not by putting it in a box.

⚠️⚠️ NEVER enclose a running generator. Enclosure causes overheating, engine fire, and — above all — carbon monoxide accumulation that kills. Generators need enormous airflow for cooling and combustion. Every year people die building "generator sheds". Do not build one.

What actually works, safely:

  1. Distance and position first. Every doubling of distance cuts perceived noise. Site it ≥ 5 m from any door, window or vent, downwind, with the exhaust pointed away from the house and away from neighbours.
  2. A three-sided screen, open at the top and open at the exhaust end. A mass barrier between the generator and the listener: 18 mm ply or two layers of board, with a dense core (mineral wool, or a rubber/mass-loaded vinyl layer). Mass blocks sound; foam does not.
    • Three panels, 1.2 m high, forming a U around the sides and back
    • Completely open above and at the front for airflow
    • At least 1 m of clear space on all open sides
    • Line the inside faces of the screen with 50 mm mineral wool behind a

perforated board — this absorbs the reflections rather than bouncing them.

  1. Put it on a rubber mat or paving slabs on sand, not on a hollow wooden deck (which acts as a soundboard) and not on bare resonant ground.
  2. Exhaust extension: a flexible steel exhaust pipe routed away from the house and screened separately makes a big difference — most of the noise is exhaust. ⚠️ Keep it clear of anything combustible; it glows.
  3. The bigger win is operational: run it in batch-charging mode (Chapter 02 §5) — 1–2 hours in daylight, at 60–80% load, while other noise exists, instead of all day. That cuts noise exposure, fuel and engine wear together.

CO rules, every time, no exceptions:

⚠️ Outdoors only. Never in a garage, shed, porch, cellar or under an overhang.
⚠️ ≥ 5 m from any opening, exhaust pointing away.
⚠️ CO alarms in every sleeping room and on the nearest wall.
⚠️ Never refuel a hot engine.
⚠️ Never backfeed into a socket — use a transfer switch fitted by an electrician.

Related: Chapter 02 — Electricity · Apartment builds · Build index
