03 — Cooking & Heat Builds

Read Chapter 04 first, and read §6 on carbon monoxide before you light anything. Every stove here is outdoor or properly flued only.

Contents: Brick rocket stove · Tin-can rocket stove · Haybox / thermal cooker · Solar box oven · Solar dehydrator · Wood store · Warm-room tent

1. 16-brick rocket stove

20 minutes · €15 · Skill ⬤○○ · Outdoors only

The highest fuel-efficiency-per-euro object in this entire folder. It boils 5 litres of water on a double handful of twigs, burns almost smokelessly, and costs the price of sixteen bricks. Every household with any outdoor space should have one.

            SECTION — the L-shaped rocket
                     ┌───┐
                     │ ▲ │  ← pot sits on the top, on 3 spacers,
                     │ ▲ │     leaving a gap for exhaust all round
        ┌────────────┤ ▲ ├─────┐
        │            │ ▲ │     │   RISER — the chimney.
        │            │ ▲ │     │   Height = 2–3 × the feed opening.
        │            │ ▲ │     │   This is the engine of the stove:
        │   ┌────────┘ ▲ └──┐  │   hot gas rising pulls air in hard,
        │   │  ═══════════  │  │   which is why it burns so cleanly.
      ══╪═══╡ FUEL SHELF    │  │
        │   │  ↑ air under  │  │ ← air MUST flow UNDER the sticks.
        └───┴───────────────┴──┘   Remove the shelf and it becomes a
              FEED                 smoky, useless fire.

The three rules that make it work:

  1. Equal cross-section throughout. Feed opening, burn tunnel and riser should all be about the same area — roughly 90 × 90 mm for a cooking stove. A pinch anywhere kills the draw.
  2. Riser height = 2–3 × the feed opening height. The taller the riser, the harder it pulls.
  3. A fuel shelf, so primary air flows under the burning sticks. This one detail is the difference between a rocket stove and a smoky hole.

Materials: 16–20 standard bricks (firebricks are better and last far longer; ordinary bricks will spall and crack after a season or two of hard use) · 3 small metal spacers or a cast-iron trivet · optionally wood ash, perlite or vermiculite to insulate around the riser.

Build

  1. Lay 4 bricks as a base pad on level ground.
  2. Build an L-shaped channel: a horizontal feed tunnel about 2 bricks long, turning up into a vertical riser 3–4 bricks tall. Dry-stack, no mortar — so you can adjust it and take it apart.
  3. Slide a flat brick or a piece of steel plate into the feed tunnel as the fuel shelf, leaving an air gap of 25–30 mm beneath it.
  4. Pack wood ash, perlite or vermiculite into the gaps around the riser if you have a double-skin design. Insulation makes the riser hotter, which makes it burn cleaner and hotter still.
  5. Put 3 spacers on top so the pot sits 20–25 mm above the riser, letting exhaust escape all round. Sitting the pot flat on the riser smothers it.

Use it: feed small, dry sticks (finger-thick) in horizontally, pushing them forward as they burn. Only the tips burn — this is what makes it so efficient. Use a windshield. Expect to boil 5 L in 10–15 minutes on about 300 g of wood.

⚠️ Outdoors or in an open-sided shelter only. It produces CO like any fire.

2. Tin-can rocket stove

45 min · €0–5 · Skill ⬤○○ · Portable, outdoors only

The same principle in scrap steel. Genuinely useful, and buildable from a recycling bin.

Materials: one large can (4–5 L catering size) · one medium can (~1 L) for the riser · one small can (soup size) for the feed · wood ash, perlite, vermiculite or dry sand for insulation · tin snips · gloves.

Build

  1. Cut a hole in the side of the large can, near the bottom, the diameter of the small can.
  2. Cut a matching hole in the side of the medium can (the riser), near its bottom.
  3. Push the small can through the large can's hole into the medium can's hole, forming an L. Seal the joints with foil or tuck the cut tabs in.
  4. Stand the riser upright inside the large can and pack the void between them with ash, perlite or vermiculite. Insulation is not optional — it is what makes it burn hot and clean.
  5. Cut a small rectangle of steel as a fuel shelf for the feed tube.
  6. Make a pot stand from three bent tabs or a wire trivet, giving the same 20–25 mm exhaust gap.

Lasts 1–3 seasons before the steel burns through. Build two.

3. Haybox / thermal cooker

1 h · €0–20 · Skill ⬤○○ · Cuts cooking fuel by 50–80%

If you build one thing from this folder, consider this one. Bring food to the boil for 10–15 minutes, then seal it in heavy insulation. It keeps cooking on stored heat for 3–6 hours with zero further fuel. Beans, grains, stews, soups, stocks, porridge — everything that needs a long simmer, which in a storage-food diet is nearly everything.

        ┌───────────────────────────────────┐
        │ ░░░░ insulated lid / cushion ░░░░ │ ← must press down onto the pot
        ├───────────────────────────────────┤
        │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
        │ ░░░  ┌───────────────────┐   ░░░  │
        │ ░░░  │  POT — lid on,    │   ░░░  │   100 mm+ of insulation
        │ ░░░  │  FULL, straight   │   ░░░  │   on ALL SIDES and
        │ ░░░  │  from a hard boil │   ░░░  │   underneath
        │ ░░░  └───────────────────┘   ░░░  │
        │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │
        └───────────────────────────────────┘

Build: a wooden box, a cardboard box, a cool-box, or a laundry basket — at least 100 mm larger than the pot on every side and underneath. Pack the void with straw, hay, wool, shredded paper, polystyrene, old duvets, blankets or sleeping bags. Make a thick insulated cushion for the top that presses down onto the pot lid. Line it with foil or an old emergency blanket for a few extra percent.

Use it

  1. Bring the food to a hard rolling boil and hold it there 10–15 minutes (20 for dry beans, 5 for rice). The pot must be genuinely hot all through.
  2. Lid on, transfer immediately — every minute on the counter is heat lost.
  3. Pack it in, cushion on top, close.
  4. Do not open it. Every peek costs an hour of cooking.
  5. Leave 3–6 hours. Dry beans that need 2 hours of simmering need 15 minutes of boil plus 4 hours in the box.

Why it matters: it turns your cooking fuel store into 2–4× as much cooking, it works with any stove, it cannot burn food, and it keeps food hot for serving. Combined with a pressure cooker (which cuts the boil phase too) it is the biggest fuel saving available to any household.

⚠️ Food safety: the food must go in boiling hot and stay above 60 °C. A well-built box holds a full pot above 70 °C for 5–6 hours. A half-empty pot in a thin box does not — use a full pot (thermal mass is the point) and don't stretch it past 6 hours.

4. Solar box oven

4 h · €25 · Skill ⬤○○ · Free cooking, April–September

Reaches 120–150 °C on a clear Central European summer day — enough to bake bread, cook rice, stew vegetables, and pasteurise water. No fuel, no smoke, no CO, no noise, and it cannot burn the house down.

              reflector lid (foil-covered, propped to bounce
             ╱  extra sun in — roughly doubles the temperature)
            ╱
         ┌─╱──────────────────────────┐
         │╱  GLASS or twin-wall lid   │ ← sealed, this is the greenhouse
         ├────────────────────────────┤
         │ ░░░ 50 mm insulation ░░░░  │
         │ ░ ┌──────────────────┐ ░   │
         │ ░ │ ▓▓ BLACK ▓▓▓▓▓▓  │ ░   │ ← black interior + a black, lidded
         │ ░ │ ▓▓ interior ▓▓▓  │ ░   │   cast-iron or dark enamel pot
         │ ░ └──────────────────┘ ░   │
         │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │
         └────────────────────────────┘

Materials: two cardboard boxes (one 50 mm smaller all round) or a plywood box · 50 mm of insulation (crumpled newspaper, wool, straw, foam board) · aluminium foil · black matt paint or black card · a sheet of glass, an old double-glazed unit, or twin-wall polycarbonate · black cooking pots with lids · a thermometer.

Build

  1. Nest the small box inside the large one, packing insulation between them.
  2. Line the inside of the inner box matt black (paint or black card) — black absorbs, shiny reflects. The interior must be black; only the reflector is shiny.
  3. Make a well-fitting glazed lid. Seal it — the trapped air is what makes it work. A double-glazed unit is far better than a single sheet.
  4. Add a hinged reflector flap lined with foil, propped at about 30° from the glass, to bounce extra sunlight in. This roughly doubles the temperature rise.
  5. Put the thermometer inside where you can read it through the glass.

Use it: start 30–60 minutes before you need to cook (it needs to warm up) · re-aim it at the sun every hour · use dark, lidded pots · cooking takes roughly twice as long as on a stove, and food cannot burn or dry out, so timing is forgiving. It works from roughly March to October in Central Europe and not at all in winter — so it is a summer fuel-saver and a preserving-season workhorse, not a year-round solution.

5. Solar food dehydrator

1 day · €50 · Skill ⬤⬤○ · Preserve a glut with no jars and no power

An indirect dehydrator — the sun heats air in a collector, and that hot air flows up through the food. Better than drying in direct sun: no UV damage, better colour and vitamin retention, and insects are kept out.

                              ┌────┐ ← chimney vent (hot air out;
                              │ ↑↑ │    this is what drives the airflow)
        ┌──────────────────┐  │    │
        │ ▤▤▤ TRAY 4 ▤▤▤▤▤ │  │    │
        │ ▤▤▤ TRAY 3 ▤▤▤▤▤ │  │    │   DRYING CHAMBER
        │ ▤▤▤ TRAY 2 ▤▤▤▤▤ │  │    │   mesh trays, 100 mm apart
        │ ▤▤▤ TRAY 1 ▤▤▤▤▤ │  │    │   shaded, insect-screened
        └────────▲─────────┘  └────┘
                 │ warm air rises
        ┌────────┴─────────────────┐
       ╱  glazed COLLECTOR, black   ╲  ← 1 m² of glazed black-painted
      ╱   inside, 30–45° to the sun  ╲    box; cool air enters at the
     ╱  ←←← cool air in (screened)    ╲   bottom, heats, and rises
    └──────────────────────────────────┘

Materials: plywood or salvaged timber · glass or twin-wall polycarbonate for the collector · matt black paint · food-safe mesh (stainless, nylon or food-grade plastic — not galvanised, zinc migrates into acidic food) for the trays · insect screen · a small chimney.

Build

  1. Collector: a shallow box about 1 m × 0.6 m, painted matt black inside, glazed on top, angled 30–45° south. Air inlet at the low end, screened.
  2. Chamber: an insulated, shaded box above the collector's high end, with runners for 4–6 mesh trays at 100 mm spacing, and a door.
  3. Chimney: a vent at the top of the chamber creates the stack effect that pulls air through. Screen it.
  4. Everything screened against flies and wasps, which will otherwise find it within an hour.

Use it: slice thinly and evenly (3–6 mm) · a single layer per tray, not touching · rotate the trays through the day since the bottom dries fastest · bring it all indoors at night, or moisture re-absorbs · dry until cracker-crisp — anything leathery will mould in storage. Then condition it in a jar for a week, shaking daily, and watch for condensation before packing for the long term.

In a Central European summer: fruit and tomatoes take 1–3 days, herbs a few hours, mushrooms a day. An electric dehydrator (300–500 W) run off surplus midday solar is the weather-independent complement.

6. Wood store

1 day · €80 · Skill ⬤⬤○ · Holds ~4 m³ — one warm-room winter

Seasoning is the whole point. Fresh wood is 45–60% water, gives half the heat, and coats your chimney in creosote. A proper store gets wood to under 20%.

              ← roof slopes back, 200 mm overhang all round
        ╱▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔╲
       │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │
       │  ░░░ THIS YEAR'S ░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │  3000 wide
       │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │  × 1000 deep
       │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │  × 1800 high
       │  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  │
       ═╪═══════════════════════════════╪═ ← bearers: wood 150 mm OFF the ground
        │                               │
      OPEN SIDES — wind must blow through. A closed store is a mould farm.

Materials: 6 × 70 × 70 posts (2400 mm) · 45 × 95 rails and bearers · corrugated roof sheet · 45 × 45 battens · post shoes or gravel · screws.

Build

  1. Posts at 1.5 m centres along a 3 m × 1 m footprint, in post shoes or on gravel — never concreted straight into soil, they rot in 5 years.
  2. Rails front and back, roof slope 10° to the back.
  3. Bearers 150 mm above the ground, running front-to-back so air passes under the stack. Pallets work perfectly.
  4. Roof with 200 mm of overhang on every side. Corrugated sheet on battens.
  5. Leave the sides completely open. Cover the top only. Airflow through the stack is what dries it; wrapping a stack in tarpaulin traps moisture and grows fungus.
  6. Site it in the sun and the prevailing wind, not tucked against a shaded north wall.

Stack it properly: split before stacking (split wood dries 4× faster than round) · bark side up in the open, bark side down under cover · cross-stack the ends for stability · leave finger gaps between rows.

Run two stores, or one store divided: this winter's seasoned wood and next winter's drying wood. Hardwood needs 18–24 months, softwood 9–12. Buy a €15 moisture meter and actually measure a freshly split face — under 20% or it does not go in the stove.

7. Warm-room tent

1 h · €0–120 · Skill ⬤○○ · 🏢 the apartment heating answer

When you cannot heat a room, heat a volume. An ordinary 3–4 person dome tent pitched inside a living room, or a canopy over a bed, traps body heat in a small space. It sounds absurd and it works extremely well: a tent with two people in it sits 10–15 °C above the room, with no fuel at all.

Options, cheapest first:

  1. A four-poster bed canopy: heavy blankets or a duvet draped over a frame of canes or a clothes airer around the bed. Free.
  2. An inner tent pitched in the room. €60–120 for a used dome tent. Add a groundsheet, a foam camping mat and a rug underneath — the floor is where most of the heat goes.
  3. A room-in-a-room: curtains or blankets hung to enclose the smallest corner of the room, ideally one with an internal wall on two sides.

Details:

  • Insulate underneath. A mattress or two camping mats on the floor matters more than the walls.
  • Vent it. A sealed tent with two people in it condenses heavily. Leave the top vent open — you will lose a little heat and gain dry bedding.
  • Hot water bottles at the foot of each bed, refilled from the pot on the stove. A body in a good sleeping bag with a hot water bottle needs no room heating at all, down to well below freezing.
  • ⚠️ Never put any flame, catalytic heater or stove inside a tent or canopy. Ever. This is the single most reliable way to die of carbon monoxide.
  • Keep the rest of the flat above 5 °C to protect the plumbing, and drain the pipes if you cannot.

Related: Chapter 04 — Heating, cooking & fuel · Food storage builds · Build index

04 — Sanitation Builds

Read Chapter 05 first. This is the category that historically kills more people than any other in a crisis, and these are the cheapest, simplest builds in the folder.

Contents: Bucket toilet · Urine diverter · Humanure compost bays · Handwash station · Plunger washing machine · Greywater mulch basin · Trench latrine

1. Bucket toilet (sawdust toilet)

2 h · €40 · Skill ⬤○○ · Build this in your first weekend

Odour-free, fly-free, usable indoors, and it works whether you are in a farmhouse or on the ninth floor. Done properly it smells of nothing but sawdust. Done badly it is unbearable — and the difference is one habit.

        ┌─────────────────────────────┐
        │ ╔═══════════════════════╗   │ ← standard toilet seat + LID,
        │ ║      SEAT OPENING     ║   │   screwed to the top
        │ ╚═══════════════════════╝   │
        ├─────────────────────────────┤
        │      ┌───────────────┐      │  Box: 500 W × 450 D × 430 H
        │      │               │      │  (430 = normal seat height)
        │      │    20 L       │      │
        │      │   BUCKET      │      │  Front or back panel hinged/lift-off
        │      │               │      │  so the bucket lifts straight out
        │      └───────────────┘      │
        └─────────────────────────────┘
              beside it: a bin of COVER MATERIAL and a scoop

Materials

ItemQty~€
18 mm plywood or OSB (one half-sheet)—20
45 × 45 corner battens3 m5
Standard toilet seat with lid115
20 L buckets (at least 2 — one in use, one in reserve)2–416
Screws, hinges or a turn-button—5
Cover material bin + scoop110

Build

  1. Cut the ply: top 500 × 450 · two sides 450 × 430 · back 500 × 430 · front 500 × 430 (make the front lift off or hinge — this is how you change the bucket).
  2. Batten the corners inside and screw the box together.
  3. Measure your actual bucket rim and cut the hole in the top so the bucket rim sits just under the seat, catching everything. A hole that is too big lets things miss the bucket; too small and the seat overhangs. Cut it 10 mm smaller than the bucket rim diameter.
  4. Screw the toilet seat over the hole.
  5. Sand, and finish with paint or oil so it wipes clean.
  6. Put a large bin of cover material and a scoop right beside it. If the cover material is not within arm's reach, people will not use it, and then it stinks.

The method — this is the whole thing

1. Start every empty bucket with 30–50 mm of cover material in the bottom.
2. Use it normally. Urine and faeces together is fine (but see §2).
3. ★ COVER COMPLETELY AFTER EVERY SINGLE USE ★
   A generous scoop, until nothing is visible. This is the entire trick.
   A covered deposit does not smell and does not attract flies.
4. Lid down between uses.
5. Swap the bucket at two-thirds full. Never carry a full one.
6. Rinse the empty bucket, tip the rinse water on the compost pile, air it.

Cover material — you need a lot, so plan it: sawdust or shavings (untreated wood only) · coconut coir bricks (one 5 kg brick expands to ~70 L — the apartment answer, compact to store) · shredded dry leaves · shredded paper and card · peat-free compost · chopped straw · dry soil · rice hulls. Budget 1–2 L per person per day → ~150–250 L per person per three months.

⚠️ Never use cat litter (clay clumps and won't compost), and never use sawdust from treated, painted or MDF timber.

2. Urine diverter

1 h · €0–80 · Skill ⬤○○ · The upgrade that changes everything

Urine is ~90% of the volume and almost all of the smell once mixed with faeces. Separating it means the solids bucket fills five times more slowly, barely smells at all, needs far less cover material, and gives you a free nitrogen fertiliser.

Three ways, cheapest first:

  1. A dedicated bottle or jug for men and children, used before sitting. Free, and it captures most of the volume immediately.
  2. A funnel and tube fitted at the front of the seat opening, draining to a 5 L jerry can beside the box. €10 of parts.
  3. A commercial urine-diverting seat or insert (€40–80). The comfortable, reliable option, and worth it if this is going to run for months.

What to do with the urine

  • Dilute 1:8 to 1:10 with water and pour onto soil around growing plants. Not on leaves, not on anything about to be eaten raw, and stop 3–4 weeks before harvest.
  • One adult's annual urine contains roughly enough nitrogen for 200–300 m² of vegetable garden — a serious free input when fertiliser cannot be bought.
  • 🏢 In a flat: dilute and pour down a still-working drain, or onto balcony pots and the trees below (ask first). Undiluted urine burns plants and stinks within hours — dilute it and use it the same day.
  • Don't use urine from anyone on medication you would rather not concentrate in your soil.

3. Two-bay humanure compost system

4 h · €40 (free from pallets) · Skill ⬤○○ · 🏡 land only

      ┌─────────────┬─────────────┐
      │             │             │   Two bays, 1 m × 1 m × 1 m each
      │   BAY A     │   BAY B     │
      │  FILLING    │  RESTING    │   Fill A for ONE YEAR.
      │  this year  │  (sealed,   │   Then seal it and fill B for a year.
      │             │   year 2)   │   Two years total before use.
      │             │             │
      └─────────────┴─────────────┘   Front boards slide out for access
       ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓
       300 mm base of straw / wood chips to absorb all liquid

Siting — get this right first

  • At least 30 m from any well, spring, stream or watercourse, and downhill from your water source. Non-negotiable.
  • Out of sight, downwind of the house, but close enough that you will actually walk there twice a day in February.
  • On soil, never on concrete — the soil biology and the drainage are part of the process.

Build: four pallets (or 22 × 100 boards on 70 × 70 posts) forming two adjoining 1 m³ bays, with removable front boards sliding in grooves. Line the inside with cardboard if the pallet gaps are large. A simple lid or a sheet of corrugated tin keeps the rain off (but it must not be sealed — this is an aerobic process and it needs air).

Operate it

  1. Start with a 300 mm base of straw or wood chips in the bay. This is the "biological sponge" — it absorbs every drop of liquid so nothing leaches out.
  2. Empty the bucket into the centre of the pile, then immediately cover it completely with straw, leaves or chips. Never leave deposited material uncovered, even for an hour.
  3. Add kitchen waste, garden waste, weeds and animal bedding to the same pile — they add carbon, nitrogen and biological diversity and help it heat.
  4. Keep the sides and top well covered with a thick carbon layer. A correctly managed humanure pile has no odour and no flies. If it smells, add more cover.
  5. Fill one bay for a full year. Then seal it and leave it completely undisturbed for a second full year while you fill the other.
  6. A hot pile reaching 50–60 °C kills pathogens in days, but a pile built from small daily additions in cold weather often will not get there. The long rest is the real safety margin, not the temperature. Do not shorten it.

Using the finished compost

  • ✅ Fruit trees, berry bushes, ornamentals, hedges, grain and fodder crops, and building soil in new beds.
  • ❌ Not on root vegetables, salads, or anything eaten raw.
  • Be conservative. Caution here costs you nothing.

Hygiene: dedicated gloves and boots for this job, a brush at the door, and hands washed at the tippy-tap on the way back. Every time.

4. Plumbed handwash station

1 h · €25 · Skill ⬤○○

The tippy-tap is the free version. This is the permanent indoor/outdoor one.

Build: a 20 L jerry can with a tap, mounted on a shelf or bracket at about 1.1 m, over a basin that drains into a bucket. Soap on a rope beside it, and a nail for a towel.

Details:

  • Site one at the toilet and one at the kitchen. Handwashing at the point of need is what works; a sink in another room is not used.
  • Refilling it is a named daily job on the rota.
  • Catch the greywater in a bucket underneath and put it on the fruit trees or use it for flushing.
  • A foot-operated tap (a lever pressing the tap arm) is better still — no hand contact with the tap at all, which is the whole point.
  • Warm water doubles compliance in winter. A jug of hot water from the stove into the jerry can once a day makes the difference between people washing and people not washing.

5. Bucket plunger washing machine + wringer

1 h · €35 · Skill ⬤○○

Hand-washing a family's laundry with a plunger is hard work; with a plunger and a wringer it is a manageable Monday morning.

     WASH               RINSE            WRING
   ┌────────┐        ┌────────┐      ╔═══════════╗
   │ ║║║║║  │        │ ║║║║║  │      ║  mop      ║
   │ ║plunger        │        │      ║  wringer  ║ ← or a hand mangle
   │ ║║║║║  │        │        │      ║  bucket   ║
   │ hot +  │        │  cool  │      ╚═══════════╝
   │  soap  │        │  clean │       drips back into the rinse bucket
   └────────┘        └────────┘
   20 L bucket       20 L bucket
   with a lid,       reuse this water as
   hole cut for      TOMORROW'S wash water
   the handle

Materials: 2 × 20 L buckets · a bucket lid with a 40 mm hole cut in the centre · a "breathing mobile washer" plunger (€25) or a clean toilet plunger with six 20 mm holes drilled through the cup · a mop bucket wringer (€20) or a hand mangle · a washboard (€15, genuinely useful for collars and cuffs).

Method

  1. Wash bucket: hot water, soap or washing soda, and no more than 3–4 garments at a time. Overloading is why hand washing feels impossible.
  2. Lid on with the plunger handle through the hole (stops it splashing everywhere and keeps the heat in). Plunge 2–3 minutes.
  3. Soak 20 minutes, plunge again for 1 minute.
  4. Wring, transfer to the rinse bucket, plunge 1 minute, wring again.
  5. Reuse the rinse water as the next load's wash water, and the final greywater on the garden. A family wash costs about 30 L this way instead of 150.

Drying is the real bottleneck in winter. A wringer or mangle removes far more water than hands can, which can halve the drying time. Dry in the warm room with the window cracked — wet laundry in a sealed room causes condensation, mould and respiratory problems. A rack over the stove, or a line in the loft above the warm room, works best.

6. Greywater mulch basin

2 h · €20 · Skill ⬤○○ · 🏡

Turns washing water into irrigation without pipes, filters or pumps.

        greywater bucket or pipe
                 │
                 ▼
        ┌─────────────────────────┐  ← 300 mm deep, 1 m across
        │ ░░░ wood chips / straw ░│    basin around a FRUIT TREE
        │ ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │    (not vegetables)
        │ ░░░░  ░░░░  ░░░░  ░░░░  │    Filled with coarse mulch, which
        └─────────────────────────┘    filters, absorbs and feeds the soil
                 tree

Build: dig a shallow basin 300 mm deep and about 1 m across around a fruit tree or berry bush, outside the trunk's drip zone (never against the trunk — that causes collar rot). Fill it with coarse wood chips or straw. Pour or pipe greywater into it. Top up the mulch twice a year as it breaks down.

Rules

  • Use it within 24 hours. Stored greywater goes anaerobic and foul fast.
  • Plant-safe soap only: no boron, no chlorine bleach, low sodium. Simple soap and potash soaps are fine; most laundry powders are not.
  • Apply to soil under mulch, never to foliage, and never to root crops or anything eaten raw.
  • Fruit trees, berry bushes, sweetcorn, potatoes and ornamentals are ideal targets.
  • Never let it pond or run off. Keep it away from the water source and from where children play.
  • Kitchen sink water with grease and food scraps clogs quickly — screen it first, or send that one to the compost heap instead.

7. Trench latrine

1 h · €0 · Skill ⬤○○ · 🏡 short-term only

For a large group, a temporary camp, or when the bucket system is overwhelmed.

Rules:

  • At least 30 m from any water source, and downhill from it.
  • 300 mm wide, 1 m deep, as long as needed. Pile the spoil beside it with a spade stuck in.
  • A log or a simple frame to sit on, and a screen for privacy.
  • A scoop of soil and wood ash after every use.
  • Backfill and mound over when it is 300 mm from the top, and mark it. Do not fill to the brim.
  • Not usable in frozen ground or where the water table is high.
  • Handwashing station at the exit, always.

⚠️ A trench latrine is a short-term measure. For anything lasting more than a few weeks, the covered bucket system with proper composting is cleaner, safer, closer to the house, works in winter, and returns fertility instead of burying it.

Related: Chapter 05 — Sanitation & hygiene · Water builds · Build index

↑ Contents

05 — Food Storage Builds

Read Chapter 03 first. Growing food is only half the job — preservation capacity, not growing capacity, is the real limit on a homestead.

Contents: Root cellar · ⚠️ Cellar gas safety · Buried barrel cellar · Root clamp · Cool pantry · Mylar packing station · Cold smoker · Drying racks

1. Root cellar (excavated)

1–2 weeks · €600+ · Skill ⬤⬤⬤ · 🏡

The highest-return "appliance" a homestead can own. It holds 2–8 °C year-round on zero energy, forever, and stores a winter's potatoes, roots, cabbages and apples. It does the job of a chest freezer without the freezer, the electricity, or the dependence.

1.1 The four storage zones

Different crops want different conditions. A good cellar has more than one place.

ZoneTempHumidityStoresDuration
Cold & damp0–4 °C90–95%Carrots, beetroot, parsnip, turnip, celeriac, leeks (in damp sand)4–6 months
Cold & damp4–8 °C85–90%Potatoes (dark!), cabbage, apples (kept separate)5–8 months
Cold & dry0–5 °C60–70%Onions, garlic, shallots (hanging, airy)6–9 months
Cool & dry10–15 °C50–70%Winter squash and pumpkins4–8 months

⚠️ Potatoes below 4 °C turn sweet and unpleasant (starch converts to sugar). ⚠️ Keep apples away from everything else — their ethylene sprouts potatoes and spoils neighbours. A separate bay, or a sealed box.

1.2 The warning nobody gives you

Stored vegetables respire. In a sealed or poorly ventilated cellar they consume oxygen and produce carbon dioxide, which is heavier than air and pools at the bottom. People have died walking into their own root cellars, silos and covered pits.

⚠️ VENTILATE BEFORE ENTERING a cellar that has been closed up.
⚠️ Open the hatch and the vents, and wait several minutes.
⚠️ Never enter alone if the cellar is below ground and has been shut.
⚠️ If a candle will not stay lit at floor level, the air is not safe. Get out.
⚠️ Dizziness, breathlessness or headache on entering = leave IMMEDIATELY.

The candle test is crude but it works, and it is free. Use it. This applies to buried barrels and covered pits just as much as to walk-in cellars.

1.3 The design

                      ┌────┐ ← EXHAUST: from the CEILING, up and out
                      │ ↑↑ │    (warm, stale, humid air leaves at the top)
    ═══════════════╤══╧════╧═══════  ground level
     ▓▓▓▓▓▓ 600 mm+ of earth cover ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓
    ┌──────────────┴─────────────────────────┐
    │                                        │  ⇓ INTAKE: pipe down to
    │  ┌───────┐  ┌───────┐  ┌───────┐       │  ⇓ 150 mm ABOVE THE FLOOR
    │  │ROOTS  │  │POTATO │  │ONIONS │       │  ⇓ (cold air falls in)
    │  │in sand│  │ dark  │  │hanging│       │  │
    │  └───────┘  └───────┘  └───────┘       │  │
    │  gravel or earth floor (humidity)      │◄─┘
    └────────────────────────────────────────┘
       2–3 m below ground where the earth sits at the local annual
       mean temperature (~10 °C in Central Europe) — winter ventilation
       then chills it down to 2–8 °C and the mass holds it there

The two-pipe rule is the whole design. One pipe low (intake, ending 150 mm above the floor) and one pipe high (exhaust, from the ceiling, rising above ground). Cold air falls in, warm humid air rises out, and the cellar breathes by itself with no fan. A cellar with one vent, or with both vents at the same height, does not work.

1.4 Build

  1. Check before you dig: the water table (a cellar that floods is worthless), buried services, and your local building regulations. In many places an excavation of this size needs consent.
  2. ⚠️ An excavation over 1.2 m deep can collapse and kill. Batter the sides back at 45°, or shore them properly, or hire someone with a machine and the competence. This is the most dangerous build in this folder. Never work alone in a trench.
  3. Excavate to 2–3 m depth. Deeper is more stable in temperature.
  4. Structure: reinforced concrete, concrete block with a poured lintel, dry stone with a vaulted roof, or a proprietary precast chamber. The roof carries 600 mm+ of wet earth — that is well over a tonne per square metre, so it must be engineered, not guessed.
  5. Waterproof the outside and lay a French drain around the base, draining to daylight or a soakaway. Water ingress is the number one cellar failure.
  6. Insulate the roof (100 mm of foam board under the earth) — the roof is where summer heat comes in.
  7. Earth cover: 600 mm minimum over the roof, graded to shed water away.
  8. Fit the two pipes (110 mm) as above, with mesh caps against rodents and insects, and dampers so you can throttle them — you close them down in hard frost and open them wide on cold autumn nights to chill the cellar.
  9. Floor: gravel or bare earth. Sprinkling it with water raises the humidity, which is exactly what roots want. A concrete floor makes it too dry.
  10. Fit out: timber slatted shelves (never against the wall — leave 50 mm for airflow), boxes of damp sand for roots, hanging nets for onions, and a thermometer and hygrometer you actually read.
  11. Door/hatch: insulated, and in a cold climate a double door creating an airlock stops a blast of frost every time you open it.

1.5 Operating it

  • Chill it in autumn: open both vents wide on cold nights in October and November to drive the temperature down before you load it. Close them on warm days. This is active work and it decides how well the cellar performs.
  • Load in cool weather, not warm. Cure everything first (see §7).
  • Check weekly. Remove anything spoiling immediately — one rotten apple really does take the box.
  • Never store fuel, paint or chemicals in a food cellar.

2. Buried barrel root cellar

4 h · €60 · Skill ⬤○○ · 🏡 The 95%-of-the-benefit version

If you cannot build a real cellar, build this. It stores 150 kg of roots and takes an afternoon.

      ═══════╤═══════════════╤═══════  ground level
             │  ▓ straw ▓    │  ← 300 mm of straw/leaves over the lid,
        ┌────┴───────────────┴────┐     held down with a board and a stone
        │  ╔═══════════════════╗  │
        │  ║   LID (sealed,    ║  │
        │  ║    liftable)      ║  │
     ▓▓▓│  ╠═══════════════════╣  │▓▓▓
     ▓▓▓│  ║                   ║  │▓▓▓  200 L food-grade barrel,
     ▓▓▓│  ║  roots in damp    ║  │▓▓▓  buried UPRIGHT with the lid
     ▓▓▓│  ║  sand, layered    ║  │▓▓▓  100 mm proud of the soil
     ▓▓▓│  ║                   ║  │▓▓▓
     ▓▓▓│  ╚═══════════════════╝  │▓▓▓
        └─────────────────────────┘
            gravel base for drainage

Build

  1. Dig a hole to take a 200 L food-grade barrel upright, with 100 mm of the rim left above ground so surface water cannot run in.
  2. 150 mm of gravel in the bottom of the hole for drainage — a barrel sitting in water will float out and your crop will rot.
  3. Drop the barrel in, backfill and firm the soil around it.
  4. Drill four 10 mm holes just under the rim, above soil level, for a little air exchange. Cover them with mesh.
  5. Layer produce in damp (not wet) sand, sawdust or leaves, with the barrel lid on.
  6. Pile 300 mm of straw or dry leaves over the lid and weigh it down with a board and a stone. This is the insulation that stops it freezing.

Use: one barrel for carrots/beets/parsnips in sand, another for potatoes in the dark. ⚠️ The same gas warning applies — lift the lid and let it air for a minute before reaching in, and don't put your head inside.

Even simpler: an old chest freezer with a dead compressor, buried to its lid, is an excellent insulated root cellar. So is a cast-off fridge. Drill drainage holes in the base, and remove any door latch before burying anything a child could climb into.

3. Root clamp

2 h · €5 · Skill ⬤○○ · 🏡 Free, ancient, and it works

The traditional way to store a tonne of potatoes or mangels with no structure at all.

                 ╱▔▔▔▔╲  ← straw "chimney" left open at the top for
                ╱ ▓▓▓▓ ╲    ventilation, capped with a tile if wet
          ┌────╱────────╲────┐
         ╱  ░░ 200 mm SOIL ░░ ╲
        ╱ ▒▒▒ 200 mm STRAW ▒▒▒ ╲
       │ ●●●●●●● ROOTS ●●●●●●●  │   Heap on a bed of straw on
       │ ●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●●   │   FREE-DRAINING ground
       └──▒▒▒ straw base ▒▒▒────┘
        ═══════════════════════════ drainage ditch dug around it

Build

  1. Pick a well-drained, slightly raised spot. Dig a shallow drainage ditch around where the clamp will sit.
  2. Lay 200 mm of straw as a bed.
  3. Heap the crop into a ridge or cone, up to about 1 m high. Only sound, dry, unwashed, uncut produce — one damaged root rots the clamp.
  4. Cover with 200 mm of straw, leaving a twist of straw poking out of the top as a ventilation chimney.
  5. Cover the straw with 200 mm of soil, firmed and smoothed to shed rain, leaving the chimney clear.
  6. In very hard frost, add more straw over the chimney.

Open from one end only, take what you need, and re-seal. Works for potatoes, carrots, beetroot, swede, turnip and mangel-wurzel (your winter animal fodder — see Chapter 08).

4. Cool pantry conversion

1 day · €150 · Skill ⬤⬤○ · The no-digging option

Convert an existing north-facing room, cupboard, porch or garage corner into a passively cooled larder. In Central Europe this holds 5–12 °C from October to April — which is most of what a cellar does, for a twentieth of the work.

Build

  1. Choose the coolest, north-facing space with an external wall. A cupboard under the stairs against a north wall is ideal.
  2. Insulate it from the house: 50–100 mm of insulation on the walls, ceiling and door that face the heated part of the building, and a well-sealed insulated door. You are insulating it FROM the house, not from outside — that is the key inversion people get wrong.
  3. Two vents through the external wall: one low (intake, 150 mm above floor) and one high (exhaust, near the ceiling), both 110 mm, both mesh-screened against rodents and insects, both with closable dampers.
  4. Leave the external wall uninsulated — it is your cooling surface.
  5. Slatted timber shelves, 50 mm clear of the walls. A cold stone or tiled floor and a thermometer.
  6. Operate the dampers: open at night and in cold weather, closed in warm spells. Close them down in hard frost to stop things freezing.

🏢 In a flat: an unheated north-facing balcony cupboard or a box on a north balcony does the same job from November to March — insulated on the inside face, vented to outside air, mesh-screened. It is free refrigeration for half the year.

5. Mylar packing station

Setup: 1 h · €50 for the kit · Skill ⬤○○

Not a structure — a process and a small jig that turns a chaotic all-day job into a steady production line. Packing 300 kg of staples goes much faster when the bench is set up properly.

The kit: mylar bags (5–7 mil, 20 L) · oxygen absorbers (2,000 cc per 20 L bucket) · food-grade buckets with gasket lids · an impulse sealer (€30) or a hair straightener or a clothes iron and a board · a permanent marker and labels · a scale · a funnel or a scoop · a jar with an airtight lid for unused absorbers.

The jig: cut a piece of ply or card to hold the mylar bag open and upright inside the bucket while you fill it — a simple collar, or two clamps on a board. Without it you need three hands.

The production line

1. FREEZE the grain/beans for 72 h at −18 °C first — kills weevil eggs, which
   are already in almost all grain when you buy it.
2. Let it return to room temperature and stay DRY. Never seal anything damp:
   moisture + no oxygen = a botulism risk. Shop-dry grain and beans are fine.
3. Line the bucket with the mylar bag. Fill.
4. Drop in the O₂ absorbers — WORK FAST, they start the moment the packet opens.
   Keep the unused ones in a sealed jar with the air squeezed out.
5. Squeeze the air out, seal across the top with the iron/sealer, leaving one
   corner open. Press the last air out and seal the corner.
6. LABEL: contents, weight, date packed, review date. Marker on tape, on the
   OUTSIDE, and a paper label INSIDE the bag too.
7. Lid on the bucket. Store cool (<18 °C), dark, dry, off the floor.
8. Check the next day: a correct seal goes hard and "bricked". That is your proof.

⚠️ Never pack anything with fat in it this way for the long term — nuts, brown rice, ground flour, anything oily. Fat goes rancid with or without oxygen. Those go in the rotating pantry.

6. Cold smoker

1 day · €80 · Skill ⬤⬤○ · 🏡

⚠️ Read this first. Cold smoking does not preserve food on its own. It adds flavour and some surface antimicrobial effect, but the preservation comes from the curing that must happen first — salt, and for slow cold- smoked products, curing salt containing nitrite. Cold smoking uncured meat at 20–30 °C is an incubator for botulism and listeria.

Always follow a tested, published cure recipe. Do not improvise, do not use family guesswork, and if you are new to this, start with hot smoking (60–80 °C, which cooks the food) or with fish and cheese rather than charcuterie.

   SMOKE CHAMBER                         FIREBOX
   ┌──────────────┐
   │  ═══ hooks ══│  ← 1000 × 600 × 600 mm     smoke travels 2–4 m and
   │   │  │  │    │     insulated ply or       COOLS on the way: it must
   │  fish/meat   │     an old fridge/cabinet  arrive UNDER 30 °C for
   │   │  │  │    │                            cold smoking
   │  ═══ racks ══│
   │              │        ╔════════════════════╗
   └──────┬───────┘        ║  smouldering       ║
          └────────────────╢  sawdust /         ║
           110 mm pipe     ║  wood chips        ║
           or a covered    ╚════════════════════╝
           trench, 2–4 m   (or a separate smoke generator / maze burner)

Materials: a plywood cabinet, an old fridge with the plastic and insulation foam stripped out, or a purpose-built box · stainless rods and hooks · 110 mm pipe or a covered trench · a firebox (an old steel drum or a brick box) · a damper at the chamber top · a thermometer at food level.

Build

  1. Chamber: 1000 × 600 × 600 mm, with racks and hooks, a door, and an adjustable vent at the top. Untreated timber, stainless or an old fridge body (strip out all plastic, insulation foam and the compressor first).
  2. Firebox 2–4 m away and lower than the chamber.
  3. Connect them with buried 110 mm pipe or a covered trench. The distance is what cools the smoke. Longer = cooler.
  4. Thermometer at food level. Cold smoke must stay under 30 °C; over that you are in the danger zone without cooking.
  5. Fuel: beech, oak, alder, apple, cherry, hickory. ❌ Never resinous softwood (pine, spruce, fir) — acrid and unpleasant — and never treated, painted or MDF wood.
  6. A maze or spiral sawdust burner (€25) gives 8–10 hours of clean cold smoke from one filling and is far easier to control than a fire.

Use: cure first, per a tested recipe · dry the surface until a tacky pellicle forms (smoke will not adhere to a wet surface) · smoke in cool weather — autumn and winter are the traditional smoking seasons for exactly this reason · keep a written log of cure weights, times and temperatures.

For hot smoking (60–80 °C — cooks as it smokes, far more forgiving for a beginner) move the firebox right under the chamber, or build a simple drum smoker.

7. Drying and curing racks

2 h · €30 · Skill ⬤○○

Most storage crops need a curing period before they go into the cellar. Skip it and they rot in December.

CropCureThen store
Onions & garlic2–3 weeks, warm, dry, airy, out of rain until the necks are paperyHanging, cold and dry
Winter squash & pumpkins10–14 days in the sun (or 25–30 °C indoors) to harden the skin10–15 °C, dry, on a shelf
Potatoes2 weeks in the DARK at 10–15 °C to heal the skins4–8 °C, dark, damp
Sweet potatoes7–10 days at 27–30 °C and high humidity13–15 °C
Beans for dryingOn the plant until the pods rattle, then indoors until the bean cracks rather than dents under a hammerSealed jars, after 72 h freezing
HerbsHung in small bunches, shade, airy, 1–2 weeksAirtight jars, whole leaves

Build the rack: a simple frame with removable mesh trays (food-safe stainless, nylon or food-grade plastic — not galvanised, zinc migrates into acidic food), 100 mm apart, in a shaded, airy, rain-proof place: a porch, a carport, an open barn, a greenhouse with the doors open.

Onion and garlic braids hung from the rafters are the classic and they work perfectly — plaiting the dried leaves together and hanging them where air moves. Squash on a slatted shelf in a spare bedroom stores better than in any cellar.

Related: Chapter 03 — Food storage · Cooking & heat builds · Build index

↑ Contents

06 — Garden Builds 🏡

Read Chapter 07 first. Build in this order: beds, compost, protection, then structures. A polytunnel over poor soil grows poor food.

Contents: No-dig beds · Three-bay compost · Brassica cage · Cold frame · Low tunnel · Walk-in polytunnel · Seed-starting rack · Liquid feed barrel · Leaf mould cage

1. No-dig growing beds

1 h per bed · €15 per bed · Skill ⬤○○ · Start here, this weekend

No digging, no rotavating, no double-digging. Cardboard on the ground, compost on top, plant into it. It suppresses weeds, feeds the soil biology, and after 2–3 years outperforms dug beds — while costing about a fifth of the labour. In a crisis, where your own calories are the fuel, labour efficiency is survival efficiency.

     1200 mm bed                450–600 mm path        1200 mm bed
  ├──────────────────┤        ├──────────────┤     ├──────────────────┤
  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░        ▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒       ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░
  ░ 100 mm COMPOST  ░        ▒ wood chip /  ▒       ░                  ░
  ▓▓▓▓ CARDBOARD ▓▓▓▓        ▒ cardboard    ▒       ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓
  ═══ grass / soil ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════

  1200 mm wide so you can reach the middle from either side.
  YOU NEVER STEP ON THE BED. Compacted soil is the commonest cause of poor yields.

Build

  1. Mow or strim the grass/weeds short. Don't remove them — they are the first feed for the soil life.
  2. Lay plain brown cardboard, overlapping every joint by 150 mm so no light gets through. Remove all plastic tape and staples. Wet it thoroughly.
  3. 100 mm of good compost on top — municipal green waste compost, well-rotted manure, or your own. A 1.2 × 5 m bed takes about 0.6 m³.
  4. Plant straight into the compost. Transplants go in immediately; for direct sowing of small seed (carrots, parsnip), wait until year two or sow into a drill of finer compost.
  5. Edges are optional. Unedged beds are free, easy to reshape, and don't harbour slugs. Timber edges look tidy and hold the compost on a slope — use untreated larch, Douglas fir, sweet chestnut or oak, or line treated timber with plastic.
  6. Mulch the paths with cardboard and wood chip. No weeding, and it becomes compost in place.
  7. Top up with 25–50 mm of compost every autumn. That is the entire ongoing maintenance.

Perennial weeds (couch grass, bindweed, ground elder) need more: two layers of cardboard plus 150 mm of compost, or a season under black plastic first. They will still push through at the edges — keep at them.

Persuading the perimeter: 4 beds of 1.2 × 5 m = 24 m² of growing space, which is the right size for a first year. Add two beds a year after that.

2. Three-bay compost system

4 h · €0–60 · Skill ⬤○○ · Compost is the engine of the whole garden

   ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐   Each bay 1000 × 1000 × 1000 mm
   │  BAY 1   │  BAY 2   │  BAY 3   │
   │ FILLING  │ TURNING  │  READY   │   Fill 1 → turn into 2 → turn into 3
   │          │          │          │   Turning is what makes it fast and hot
   │ new      │ 3 months │ finished │
   │ material │   old    │ compost  │
   └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘
    ↑ removable front boards on all three, sliding in grooves

Materials: 9 pallets (free — heat-treated "HT" stamped only, never MB), or 22 × 100 boards on 70 × 70 posts · galvanised wire or brackets · 4 lengths of 45 × 45 as front-board guides · a tarpaulin or old carpet as a cover.

Build: stand pallets on edge to form three adjoining 1 m cubes, wired or screwed at the corners · line the insides with cardboard if the gaps are wide · fit vertical guides at the front of each bay so boards slide in and out as the heap grows · leave the bays open to the soil beneath (worms and soil life must get in) · a loose cover on top to keep rain off without sealing it.

Run it

  • Roughly 2:1 browns to greens by volume. Browns = straw, dry leaves, cardboard, wood chip, paper, spent bedding. Greens = kitchen waste, grass clippings, weeds, manure, comfrey.
  • Damp as a wrung-out sponge. Too dry and nothing happens; too wet and it goes anaerobic and stinks.
  • Turn it into the next bay when it cools — every 3–6 weeks. Turning is the difference between finished compost in 3 months and finished compost in 18.
  • 1 m³ is the minimum size for a heap to heat up. Smaller heaps compost, just slowly and cold — which does not kill weed seeds or pathogens.
  • Bury kitchen waste in the middle and cover it, or you feed the rats.
  • ❌ Not in the heap: meat, dairy, cooked food (rats), dog/cat faeces, diseased plant material, persistent perennial weed roots, anything treated with aminopyralid herbicides.

Keep the humanure system entirely separate — see Sanitation builds §3.

3. Insect mesh cage

2 h · €50 · Skill ⬤○○ · Do this before the damage, not after

Physical barriers are 90% of pest control without sprays. A mesh cage over the brassica bed defeats cabbage white butterfly, cabbage root fly, flea beetle and pigeons in one go, permanently, with no inputs.

        ╔═══════════════════════════════════╗
        ║  ░░░░░ INSECT MESH 0.6–1 mm ░░░░░ ║  ← mesh, not netting.
        ║ ░                               ░ ║    Butterfly netting stops
        ║░       brassica bed              ░║    pigeons; only fine mesh
        ║░   1200 × 5000, hoops at 1 m     ░║    stops root fly and flea
        ║░                                  ░║   beetle.
        ╚═╤═══════════════════════════════╤═╝
          └── mesh edges BURIED or weighted with boards/stones all round
              (a 5 mm gap at the bottom is an open door)

Materials: 25–32 mm MDPE water pipe or 8 mm steel rod for hoops · 500 mm lengths of rebar or timber pegs as sockets · insect mesh, 0.6–1 mm aperture (€3–5/m², buy a big roll) · clothes pegs or clips · boards or stones for the edges.

Build: drive rebar stakes 300 mm into the soil at 1 m intervals down both sides of the bed, leaving 200 mm proud · slide a length of MDPE pipe over each opposing pair to form a hoop about 700 mm high · run a ridge line of pipe or string along the top to stop them flopping · drape the mesh over the whole thing and seal every edge to the ground with boards, stones or buried soil.

Details: mesh is fine enough that you can water straight through it · it also gives light frost protection and wind shelter · leave it on from the day you plant until the day you harvest — one afternoon of access is all a cabbage white needs · lift it for weeding, and check for anything that got trapped inside.

Buy plenty of mesh, netting and fleece now. They last 5–10 years, they are the single most effective non-chemical pest control, and there is no substitute for them if they cannot be bought.

4. Cold frame

4 h · €20 (free from old windows) · Skill ⬤○○ · Best value structure in the garden

Does most of what a greenhouse does for a twentieth of the cost: hardening off, early sowing, winter salad, overwintering, propagation, and season extension at both ends.

            ╱▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔  ← old window as the lid, hinged at the
          ╱   glazed lid, 15–20°      BACK, propped open with a notched stick
        ╱                             (ventilation is the main daily job)
      ┌─────────────────────────┐
      │                         │  BACK 400 mm high
      │   1200 × 600 internal   │  FRONT 250 mm high
      │                         │  Slope faces SOUTH
      └─────────────────────────┘
       sitting on soil, not concrete — the earth is your heat store

Materials: an old window, a double-glazed unit, or twin-wall polycarbonate · 22 × 150 boards or 18 mm exterior ply · 45 × 45 corner battens · 2 hinges · a notched prop stick.

Build: a simple four-sided box sized to your window, back higher than front so the lid sheds rain and faces the low winter sun · hinge the window at the back · make a notched prop stick with 3 or 4 notches so you can vent it by degrees · set it on soil against a south-facing wall if you have one (the wall stores heat and releases it at night).

Use it: ⚠️ The main risk is cooking your plants, not freezing them. On a sunny March day a closed cold frame reaches 40 °C+ and kills everything inside. Vent it every morning, close it every evening, from February onwards. Put an old duvet or a straw mat over it on hard frost nights. Two frames is better than one big one — different crops want different management.

5. Low tunnel

2 h · €40 · Skill ⬤○○

A row-scale version: hoops and a cover over one bed. Buys 2–4 weeks at each end of the season, protects against frost, wind, pigeons and cabbage white, and rolls up in ten seconds.

Build: the same hoop construction as the insect cage (§3) — rebar sockets, MDPE pipe hoops at 1 m centres, a ridge line — but covered with horticultural fleece (frost protection, 2–4 °C, breathable, lets rain through) or clear polythene (more warmth, but you must vent it, and it needs watering).

A single set of hoops per bed, with three different covers (insect mesh, fleece, polythene) swapped through the year, is the most flexible and cheapest protection system there is. Build the hoops once; change the skin by season.

6. Walk-in polytunnel 3 × 6 m

2 days, 2 people · €500 · Skill ⬤⬤⬤

Changes your growing calendar by 6–8 weeks at each end and makes winter salad possible. The highest-value structure on a food-producing property after the root cellar.

                    ╱▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔▔╲          Ridge 2200 mm
                  ╱              ╲
                ╱                  ╲
              ╱                      ╲    Hoops at 1200 mm centres
            ╱                          ╲  → 6 hoops over 6 m
           │                            │
           │      3000 mm span          │
           │                            │
      ═════╧════════════════════════════╧═════
       foundation tubes 600 mm into the ground
       polythene trenched in 300 mm on both sides, or on base rails

       DOORS AT BOTH ENDS — non-negotiable. A tunnel you cannot cross-ventilate
       cooks its contents in April and grows mildew all summer.

Materials: a polytunnel kit is usually cheaper and far better than improvising — 25–32 mm galvanised steel hoops, foundation tubes, ridge bar, crop bars, door frames (~€350–500 for this size) · 720 gauge (180 micron) UV-stabilised horticultural polythene, 5–7 year life · anti-hot-spot tape · timber for door frames · a spare polythene sheet for when this one goes.

Build

  1. Site it: open, sunny, sheltered from the prevailing wind but not in a frost pocket. Run it north–south for even light. Level ground, or terrace it.
  2. Set out accurately. Mark the rectangle and check the diagonals are equal before anything goes in the ground. Everything downstream depends on it.
  3. Foundation tubes 600 mm into the ground at 1.2 m centres, all at the same height, plumb.
  4. Assemble the hoops and drop them into the foundation tubes; fit the ridge bar and crop bars.
  5. ★ Apply anti-hot-spot tape along every hoop before the polythene goes on. ★ Bare metal gets hot in the sun and degrades the film exactly where it touches. Skipping this can halve the life of a €150 cover. It is the single most commonly skipped step.
  6. Build and fit the door frames at both ends.
  7. Cover on a warm, still day — warm polythene stretches and pulls drum-tight; cold polythene never does, and wind makes it dangerous. Two or three people. Never attempt it in a breeze.
  8. Trench the film in: dig a 300 mm trench down each side, pull the film tight, lay it in and backfill. (Or fit base rails, which make re-covering far easier later.)
  9. Fix the film to the door frames with battens.

Use it: ⚠️ Ventilation is the whole job. Open both doors on any sunny day from March onwards — it hits 45 °C inside on a mild spring day. Water in the morning, at the base, never overhead. Rotate crops inside it too; tunnel soil gets exhausted and disease-ridden fast. Mulch heavily — a tunnel dries out extremely quickly.

Buy the replacement polythene sheet now and store it dry and dark. In five years you may not be able to get one, and a tunnel without a skin is a pile of hoops.

7. Seed-starting rack

2 h · €80–150 · Skill ⬤○○

Starting plants indoors is what makes a short season productive. Windowsills give leggy, weak seedlings; a simple lit rack gives stocky ones.

Build: a 4-tier steel or timber shelf unit (or plain shelves on brackets) with an LED grow bar or a plain 4000–6500 K LED batten suspended 100–150 mm above each shelf, on adjustable chains so you can raise it as the plants grow. Add a heat mat (20 W) with a thermostat on the bottom shelf for germinating tomatoes, peppers and aubergines, which need 20–25 °C.

Numbers: 20–40 W of LED per shelf, 14–16 h/day → 280–640 Wh/day per shelf. That is real load — plan it in your power budget (Chapter 02), or run it only in daylight hours off surplus solar, or accept a windowsill and slower growth.

Also: a bench, seed trays and modules (buy plenty of spares — they crack), labels and a pencil (ink fades in a month outdoors), a fine-rose watering can, sieved compost, and a notebook.

8. Comfrey / nettle liquid feed barrel

30 min · €20 · Skill ⬤○○ · Free fertiliser, forever

Comfrey (potassium — for tomatoes, potatoes, fruit) and nettles (nitrogen — for leafy growth) make excellent liquid feeds from plants that grow themselves.

Build: a 60–200 L barrel with a tap at the base, on blocks. Inside, a mesh bag or a perforated bucket holding the leaves so the liquid runs clear. A lid — a tight one, because it smells genuinely appalling. Site it well away from the house and from the neighbours.

Method: stuff the bag with comfrey or nettle leaves, weigh it down, fill with water, cover · leave 3–4 weeks · draw off from the tap and dilute 1:10 to 1:20 until it is the colour of weak tea · water onto soil, not leaves · top the barrel up with fresh leaves and water as you use it.

Concentrate version (no water, no smell): pack a barrel with comfrey leaves only, weighted down, with a tap at the bottom. Over a few weeks they collapse into a black concentrate that drips out. Dilute 1:40. No water added, far less smell, and it stores.

Plant a dozen Bocking 14 comfrey roots in a corner now. Cut it 3–4 times a year. It is sterile so it will not seed itself across the garden, its roots reach down 2 m to mine minerals nothing else can, and it is also chicken and rabbit feed and a fine compost activator.

9. Leaf mould cage

30 min · €15 · Skill ⬤○○ · Free soil improver

Autumn leaves become the best water-retaining soil conditioner there is, for nothing but time.

Build: a cylinder of 1 m-high galvanised wire mesh, about 1 m across, held with 4 stakes. Or four pallets. Or just a heap in a corner.

Use: fill with autumn leaves, tread down, water if dry, and wait 1–2 years (leaves break down by fungal action, not bacterial — it is slow and it needs no turning, no nitrogen and no attention). Shredding with a mower first halves the time. Oak and beech take longest; they also make the best mould.

Build two or three cages, one for each year's leaves, and you get a barrow of free soil conditioner every autumn in perpetuity. Use it as a mulch, a seed compost ingredient, and to bulk out potting mixes when compost is scarce.

07 — Animal Builds 🏡🏢

Housing that keeps animals alive, predators out, and cleaning down to a 30-second job. Read Chapter 08 first — especially the feed arithmetic.

Contents: Chicken coop · Run · Chicken tractor · Feeder & drinkers · Rabbit hutch · Quail cage · Black soldier fly bucket · Fodder rack · Feed store

1. The chicken coop — full plan

For 6–8 hens · 2–3 days · €250–450 new, €80–150 from salvage · Skill ⬤⬤○

1.1 The design rules that actually matter

Everything else is preference. Get these wrong and the coop fails.

RuleNumberWhy
Coop floor area0.4 m²/bird minimumLess and you get feather-pecking, bullying and disease
Run area2 m²/bird absolute minimum, 10 m² is much betterA bare overgrazed run is a parasite and mud problem
Perch length200 mm/birdThey all roost at night; short perches cause fights
Perch shape45 × 45 mm with rounded top edges — NOT a round dowelChickens roost flat-footed and must cover their toes with their body in winter, or they get frostbite
Nest boxes1 per 4 hens, 300 × 300 × 300 mmMore and they sleep in them (and foul them)
Ventilation area~0.1 m² per 1 m² of floor → 0.24 m² for this coopDamp, not cold, is what kills chickens. Ammonia and moisture cause respiratory disease
Vent positionAbove the birds' heads, alwaysVentilation at perch height is a draught, and a draught is what makes them ill
Mesh12.5 mm (½") galvanised weld mesh, 19 gauge❌ A fox tears through chicken wire and a marten walks through it
Raised off the ground500 mmKeeps the floor dry, denies rats a nest, gives the birds shade and shelter underneath
Pop door300 × 400 mm

1.2 The design

                        FRONT ELEVATION (2000 mm wide)
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ← roof, 250 mm overhang
    │▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒ meshed vent strip 2000 × 80 ▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒│    (shaded by the overhang)
    ├────────────┬───────────────────┬──────┬──────────────┤

         ║                                         │ramp    ║   500 mm legs
         ║                                        ╱         ║

                        SIDE SECTION (1200 mm deep)
    ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐

    ├─────────────────────────────────────┤
    │▒vent▒                        ▒vent▒ │  1150 front → 850 back
    │                                     │
    │      ┌───┐         ┌───┐            │ ← perches 45×45, 450 above floor,
    │      └───┘         └───┘            │   400 apart, 300 off the walls
    │   ═════════════════════════         │ ← droppings board, removable,
    │                                     │   250 below the perches
    │  bedding ~100 mm deep litter        │        ┌──────────┐

    ║                                     ║        │  hinged  │   back wall
    ║           500 mm clear              ║        │   lid    │
    ══╩═════════════════════════════════╩═╝        └──────────┘

Overall: 2000 (L) × 1200 (D) coop box, floor 500 mm above ground. Front wall 1150 high, back wall 850 → 14° roof pitch (enough for corrugated sheet and for rain and snow to clear). Floor area 2.4 m² = 6 hens comfortably, 8 at a squeeze.

Cut the cost: clad with salvaged pallet boards or featheredge instead of OSB (−€100), use scavenged corrugated steel for the roof (−€30), build the whole thing from pallets (−€200). A coop is the single most pallet-friendly build there is.

⚠️ Pallets: heat-treated (stamped "HT") only. Never MB (methyl bromide).

1.4 Principal cut list

Cut these to length. Short infill studs and noggins you cut to fit as you go — that is normal and it is faster than measuring everything twice.
